# BRENNA

## On the Heresy of the Albigensians <note>original: "De haeresi Albigensium." The Albigensians, also known as Cathars, were a dualist religious group in Southern France during the 12th and 13th centuries. The Catholic Church launched a crusade against them, viewing their beliefs as a major threat to orthodox Christianity.</note>

# MOST REVEREND FATHER.

<image-desc>A large ornamental woodcut initial "V". The interior of the letter and its rectangular frame depict a classical architectural setting, possibly a theater or a lyceum with tiered seating and columns. Small figures in 17th or 18th-century dress are standing at the base of the structure.</image-desc>

The old custom of our Lyceum <note>original: "Lycei"; a term for a school or academy, derived from the site in Athens where Aristotle taught.</note>, by which every year a field is accustomed to be opened here, as it were, to the talents and diligence of the youth, so that they may test what those talents are worth and what progress they have made throughout the whole course of the year, is both commonly known, and to me—who am undertaking this kind of exercise <note>original: "exercitationis genus"; referring to the public defense of a thesis or an academic debate.</note> for the third time—it must be very well known. In these exercises (just as was done in the ancient athletic, musical, and poetic contests <note>original: "agonibus"; a Greek-derived term for formal competitions.</note>, in which

<summary>This page opens the formal dedication of an academic "exercise" or thesis defense, describing the long-standing tradition of students publicly demonstrating their progress and comparing these debates to the intellectual and physical contests of antiquity.</summary>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the orator’s formal address to the patron. The previous page ended by comparing the academic exercises to ancient Greek contests (agons), where judges presided over the competitors.</meta>

...these contests our people have succeeded), we are most accustomed to seek and desire that someone might stand by us and preside over us as a sort of Master of the Games <note>original: "Agonothetam"; the official in ancient Greece who oversaw public games or festivals.</note>; by whom either our industry may be honored, or the weakness of our intellect may be supported. But it happened that this year, and at this very time—when we were about to enter this literary arena before the formal school holidays <note>original: "Scholasticæ feriæ"; the academic break or vacation.</note> are announced—we heard that the Supreme Magistracy of your Order and Family <note>likely referring to the head of a religious order, such as the Dominicans or Jesuits, often called a "family."</note> had been conferred upon You, **MOST REVEREND FATHER**, to the great applause of the entire City and the greatest consensus of Your people.

Indeed, I do not think it happened by chance, at this particular juncture of time, in this City, and in that place so close and almost joined to our schools and walls, that such a great event occurred; and that this School <note>original: "Athenæum"; a term for an academy or university, here specifically referring to the Collegium Germanicum or a similar institution in Rome or Louvain founded under papal authority.</note>, built by the authority of Gregory XIII for Religion and the Liberal Arts—and especially for what was necessary in those times: to defend and confirm the Christian Religion—ought almost to overhear the voices of those over whose Order you were being placed. This is an Order which, in the most difficult times for the Christian Religion—when the most foul heresies <note>referring to the challenges of the Protestant Reformation.</note> had infected the greater part of the world—was born, tested, and confirmed. 

But who, indeed, was being put in charge? There is nothing among those qualities that can entice men to seek for themselves the patronage <note>original: "patrocinium"; the protection or legal support of a high-ranking person.</note> of some supreme and most distinguished man, which (I pray, **MOST REVEREND FATHER**, that there be room for a voice of truth, entirely free from all flattery) is not found most abundantly in You. For even those praises which are external—and which wise men are accustomed to despise or rank below other virtues—are so heaped upon You, that they alone [would] adorn anyone else...

<summary>The orator celebrates the election of the dedicatee to the supreme leadership of his religious order, viewing it as a providential event that coincides with the school's academic defense. The text highlights the historical role of the Order in defending the Catholic faith against the Reformation.</summary>

<keywords>Master of the Games, school holidays, religious order, Pope Gregory XIII, counter-reformation, patronage, academic defense, Athenaeum</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the speaker's praise of the newly elected head of the religious order. The previous page established the setting of the academic ceremony and the announcement of the patron's new "Supreme Magistracy." This page elaborates on the patron's noble background and the specific virtues that make him fit to lead a global religious organization.</meta>

...could tell and sufficiently illustrate; that you were born, I say, from a most noble family, were educated in the Court of the most powerful Kings, have attained the highest honors, and hold great authority and favor with Princes. But there are besides those qualities which pertain to the soul, and because they are uniquely human, they are held in even higher esteem by wiser and more discerning men. If I wished to recount these one by one, both the day and the time would fail me, and I would perhaps seem to wander too far from my intended purpose.

I will say only this most briefly: of those steps of honors and duties that usually lead to the Supreme Magistracy <note>original: "Magistratum"; here referring to the office of Superior General or the highest administrative position within the religious order.</note>, You have fulfilled them all—and you have done so in such a way that no one has ever achieved them in a shorter time, with greater goodwill from others, or with such apparent divine favor. For indeed, in a Religious family <note>original: "Religiosæ familiæ"; the author uses the term "family" to describe the religious order, emphasizing its communal and spiritual bonds.</note>—especially one that is highly adorned both by the praise of virtue and the splendor of learning, and is furthermore spread far and wide across all regions of the earth—there are men innumerable in multitude, diverse in their variety of peoples and nations, and most distinguished in their lineage, duties, and authority. 

It is therefore fitting that the one who rules them should be he who has joined to these other external praises, and to the highest integrity and sanctity of life, this further quality: that he has not only mastered the most noble and excellent disciplines through study, but has demonstrated his mastery in them through teaching and instruction. Moreover, in the very art of governing <note>original: "gubernandi arte"</note>, which is the most difficult of all tasks, he must have shown himself to be most prudent <note>original: "prudentissimum"; in this context, it implies "practical wisdom"—the ability to make the right decisions in complex situations.</note> and experienced.

You, without doubt, are that man, **MOST REVEREND FATHER**, who, having reached a somewhat more mature age, [entered] the Religious <meta>The sentence ends here as the catchword "vi-" suggests the start of the next page is "vitam" (life).</meta>

<summary>The author praises the patron’s rapid ascent to the leadership of his religious order, noting that his noble upbringing and academic mastery in both learning and teaching make him uniquely qualified to govern a diverse, global community.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the praise of the newly elected Superior General. ...perience, having embraced this life; thus you brought a knowledge and experience of the greatest matters along with a purity of character already observed and approved by all. In only a few—or rather, not very many—years since you entered this profession, you have traversed with the greatest speed all the ranks pertaining to teaching and governing, and you have clearly demonstrated to everyone what kind of man and how great a leader you are. 

You taught Philosophy and the science of natural things in that famous city which was the most ancient home of the liberal arts and disciplines in Italy, Perugia <note>original: "Perusiæ"; the capital of the Umbria region, home to one of the oldest universities in Italy.</note>. In this city [Rome], which is the leader not only of Empire but also of learning, you spent four years explaining that branch of Theology <term>Theology: the study of religious belief and the nature of God.</term> which is dedicated to extracting and clarifying the true and authentic meaning of the Prince of Theologians, St. Thomas <note>original: "S. Thomæ"; referring to St. Thomas Aquinas, the 13th-century Dominican scholar whose "Summa Theologiae" became the standard for Catholic teaching.</note>. You did this with the greatest clarity of understanding, richness of speech, and ease of debate. 

You governed the Province of Aragon <note>A region in northeastern Spain.</note> as the supreme moderator of that same Order with the utmost holiness and wisdom. Finally, you were chosen by the Most Reverend and Distinguished Bremond <note>original: "Bremondio"; likely a reference to a previous Superior General or high official whom the current Patron assisted.</note> to assist him in governing the Spanish provinces. You so proved your worth to him, to your peers, and to the entire Order, that after he was taken from this light <note>A poetic Latin idiom for passing away.</note>, they judged you worthy to be given the helm—not merely of one part of this most distinguished and religious family <note>The author continues to use "family" to refer to the religious Order.</note>, but of the entire organization. 

Therefore, since you are a man of such character and greatness, and since you have arrived at this highest dignity at this time and place, should I not believe it was divinely ordained that you were called to be a Patron to me and my studies? But these are the things I have mentioned so far...

<meta>This page continues the author's dedication to his patron. Here, the author transitions from the patron’s general merits to a personal connection based on their shared interest in Church History and the specific history of the patron's religious order.</meta>

...of such a kind that they could apply to any other student of this Lyceum <term>Lyceum: a place of higher learning or university.</term> and of any faculty. I have something else, specific and clearly my own; for I have been engaged for several years now in the study of Church History <term>Church History: the academic study of the history of the Christian Church and its institutions.</term>, with what success I do not know, but certainly with no small diligence and the greatest dedication. For that you have especially loved this discipline is testified by your disputations and debates <note>original: "disputationes, & concertationes"; formal academic arguments common in early modern universities.</note> held on these very subjects treated in that faculty; it is testified most of all by the fact that, having once been entrusted as a student to your Predecessor, you undertook with him the care of publishing the Papal Documents <term>Papal Documents: official decrees or charters issued by the Pope, often called "Diplomas" or "Bulls."</term> which concern your Order. 

And this, without doubt, must most strongly win you over to me and my studies. For I am engaged in that part of history which, if any other, pertains to the glory and dignity of your religious family. For I am about to speak of the Albigensian Heresy <note>original: "Albigensium Haeresi"; a medieval religious movement in Southern France deemed heretical by the Catholic Church, which led to the Albigensian Crusade in the 13th century.</note>, that very one which the most holy author and father of your Order <note>Referring to Saint Dominic (c. 1170–1221), the founder of the Dominican Order, or the "Order of Preachers."</note> attacked with the greatest skill, opposed with incredible constancy, and crushed with admirable success. In the conquering of this heresy, since his most holy sons were called, as if to pursue and complete the war of the Lord, they set a most noble and successful example for the others who would later be summoned to tame and wear down the heresies that followed. 

These are the reasons which persuaded me to adopt you as the patron for myself and my studies. I wish I had others that might serve to excuse the fact that, with this mediocrity of talent and this thinness of learning, I did not hesitate to voluntarily undertake and demand so great a burden—a task in which the reputation of this Lyceum and College even seems to be at risk.

<summary>The author justifies choosing the patron by highlighting their shared passion for Church History and the patron's work with Dominican documents. He reveals the subject of his work: the Albigensian heresy and the role of Saint Dominic's order in suppressing it.</summary>

<image-desc>An ornate woodcut headpiece featuring a central figure of a scholar or saint seated at a desk with books, flanked by elaborate floral scrolls, cornucopias, and architectural elements.</image-desc>

# ON THE ALBIGENSIAN HERESY
### A DISSERTATION.

<image-desc>A decorative drop cap 'H' containing a landscape with a domed building and trees.</image-desc>

Heresies that had troubled the Christian Church from the very age of the Apostles, after they were extinguished or put to rest in the 10th century <note>original: "sæculo x."; the author suggests a period of relative theological calm before the movements of the High Middle Ages began.</note> so that almost none appeared, broke out again in the 11th, and especially the 12th century. Thereafter, they have been propagated down to these our times, with new errors constantly being born and growing up. Therefore, a second course, as it were, begins from here for that part of Ecclesiastical History <term>Ecclesiastical History: the study of the history of the Christian Church as a divine institution.</term> which considers the causes, origins, and progressions of errors. This study is indeed most useful, and entirely necessary for understanding more clearly and confirming Catholic dogma. It is altogether fitting to pursue this more diligently, especially so that we may resist heterodox men <note>original: "Heterodoxis hominibus"; here referring to Protestant reformers and their followers who held views differing from the Catholic Church.</note>, who try to defend and excuse the "novelty" of their sect and doctrine—a charge leveled by our people—by using these older heretics. They attempt to demonstrate from them a certain perpetual succession of their own errors and of their separation from the Roman Church. We have chosen, among others, the subject of this Dissertation <term>Dissertation: translated from "Exercitatio," an academic or theological discourse meant to be defended.</term>, the so-called Heresy of the Albigensians; because it is indeed very famous, yet not yet sufficiently explained, as it seems to us at least. We believe these to be the principal chapters <note>original: "capita"; literally "heads" or main points of inquiry.</note> that can be investigated concerning this heresy; thus we shall inquire:

Concerning the errors—which ones were theirs, that is, the Albigensians'.

Concerning their various types, names, origin, and spread.

Concerning the principal defenders or supporters of the heresy, and its conquerors <note>original: "expugnatoribus"; literally "those who take by storm," referring to both the military crusaders and the theologians who dismantled the Albigensian movement.</note>.

<vocab>Albigensians, Heresy, Roman Church, Heterodox, Ecclesiastical History</vocab>

<summary>The author introduces his study of the Albigensian heresy, arguing that studying historical errors is essential for defending Catholic dogma against contemporary challengers who claim these ancient sects as their ancestors. He outlines the three main areas of his investigation: the nature of their errors, their origins, and the figures who supported or fought against them.</summary>
<keywords>Albigensian heresy, Church History, Catholic dogma, counter-reformation, medieval heresy, theological methodology</keywords>

<header>Concerning the method employed to extirpate heresy, through disputations, dialogues, councils, military expeditions, or established trials.</header>

### C H A P T E R I.

->*On the errors of the Albigensians.*<-

Explaining the errors of the Albigensians is not very difficult; indeed, we might be content, as some are, to simply call them *new Manichaeans* <note>Followers of Manichaeism, an ancient dualistic religion that taught a struggle between a good spiritual world of light and an evil material world of darkness.</note>, and declare that they embraced the dogmas of the ancient Manichaeans. However, the description of Manichaean dogmas is not as easy and straightforward as it seems to some; and while most agree that the Albigensians were Manichaeans, there are some who deny it. Among these is Johann Christoph Wolf <note>Johann Christoph Wolf (1683–1739) was a German Lutheran scholar known for his studies on Manichaeism.</note> in his book entitled (1) *Manichaeism before the Manichaeans*: because he clearly saw that if the Albigensians were condemned, the Waldensians <note>A medieval ascetic movement often claimed by later Protestant historians as spiritual predecessors to the Reformation.</note>—from whom heterodox men trace their own succession—could not remain safe (2). To us, it seems that the ancient and common opinion must be entirely retained, and we believe we can prove it by comparing the dogmas of the ancient Manichaeans with those that contemporary writers attributed to the Albigensians. "*Certain heresiarchs*," says Caesarius <note>Caesarius of Heisterbach (c. 1180–1240), a Cistercian monk and author.</note> (3), "*collected points from the dogma of Mani... they believe, like the Manichaean, in two principles: a good God and an evil God, that is, the Devil, whom they say creates all bodies, just as the good God creates souls.*" Peter, a monk of the Abbey of Vaux-de-Cernay <note>Petrus Sarnensis, a primary chronicler of the Albigensian Crusade.</note>, another very ancient writer of Albigensian history (4), says: "*First, it should be known that the heretics established two Creators; namely, one of invisible things, whom they called the benevolent God, and one of visible things, whom they named the malignant God, etc.*" and he adds what is known to have been characteristic of the ancient Manichaeans: "*They attributed the New Testament to the benevolent God, but the Old Testament to the malignant one.*" Caesarius agrees with this, when *he denies that they receive Moses and the Prophets*; indeed, Peter records these blasphemies of the Count of Toulouse <note>Raymond VI or VII, the rulers of Toulouse who were accused of harboring heretics.</note>, the chief defender of the Albigensian heresy (5): "*It appears clearly that the Devil made this world. If the God of Moses, in whom you believe, cannot help you, etc.*" Manich-

---

<columns>2</columns>

(1) *Page 256.* (2) *Colberg <note>Ehrenreich Gerhard Colberg (1659–1698), a theologian who wrote on the "Platonic-Hermetic" Christianity.</note> thinks these errors are truly attributed to the Albigensians, yet I cannot help but express a different view from his, since it is to be feared that in this way the Waldensians—whom he nevertheless declares free from these beliefs—might be charged with the same indictment.* (3) *Distinction 9, chapter 21. Cistercian Library, Tissier, volume 2, page 138.* (4) *History of the Albigensians, chapter 2.* (5) *Chapter 4.*

The author references several historical scholars and chroniclers to support the claim that the Albigensians were theological descendants of Mani.</summary>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that the Albigensians were linked to ancient Manichaean and Paulician heresies. The text begins mid-word, completing the term "Manichaean" from the previous page's discussion of theological errors. The author is using citations from Church Fathers and historical records of "anathemas" (church curses) to prove that these medieval heretics held the same views as ancient dualists who believed in two Christs—one physical and "evil," and one spiritual and "good."</meta>

[Ma]nichaean is also that point which follows in that passage (1) from the same Peter; *That Christ, who was born in the earthly and visible Bethlehem, and crucified in Jerusalem, was evil... For the good Christ, as they said, never ate or drank, nor took on true flesh, nor was he ever in this world, except spiritually in the body of Paul.* Moreover, we said "in the earthly and visible Bethlehem" because the heretics imagined *there to be another new and invisible earth*; and in that land, according to some, the good Christ was born and crucified. It is clear from those authors I have named, and from Epiphanius (4), that it was among the tenets of the ancient Manichaeans <note>Followers of Manichaeism, a dualistic religion that taught a struggle between a good spiritual world and an evil material world.</note> that Christ suffered death only in appearance, or as a *phantom* <note>original Greek: phantasia; a reference to Docetism, the belief that Christ's body was not human but a celestial illusion.</note>, as Theodoret says (2); and as Augustine says (3), that he faked his passion, for he had not been born, nor had he shown true flesh to human eyes, but only a simulated likeness of it. Indeed, I judge these men to be suitable witnesses of this matter, however differently Beausobre <note>Isaac de Beausobre (1659–1738), a Protestant historian who argued that Manichaeans were often misrepresented by their enemies.</note> may think. But even Beausobre (5) himself confesses this, and grants that the other idea concerning the invisible world was likewise Manichaean (6).

The fact that Peter added "according to some" clearly shows that among these more recent Manichaeans, there were certain divisions of doctrine; just as there were in those slightly older ones, known as the Paulicians <note>A dualistic Christian sect that emerged in Armenia in the 7th century, often viewed as the ancestors of the Bogomils and Albigensians.</note>. For in the formulas of anathema <note>A formal curse or decree of excommunication used by the Church to define the boundaries of orthodox belief.</note> which were prescribed for them to pronounce when they came to the Catholic Church—which Cotelerius (7) published from the Royal Codex, and Tollius (8) published even more extensively from the Imperial Codex—one reads the following:

*I anathematize those who say that our Lord Jesus Christ was manifested to the world only in appearance, and who refuse to confess that he truly took on flesh from the Blessed Virgin Mary... and was made <term>consubstantial</term> <note>Meaning "of the same substance" or "of the same essence." This term was used to affirm that Christ was fully human and fully divine.</note> with us and a perfect man... I anathematize those who hold opinions contrary to these, and want there to be one Christ who was born of Mary and baptized—or, as they mockingly say, "dipped"—and another who ascended from the water and received the testimony, whom they call the unbegotten Jesus and the light, who appeared in the form of a man; and they fabricate that the former came from an evil principle, but the latter from a good one. I anathematize those who say our Lord Jesus Christ suffered only in appearance, and that he who was on the cross was one person, while another stood far from it and laughed, as if someone else were suffering in his place.*

This formula pertains, as I said, to those Manichaeans who were called Paulicians in the 7th, 8th, and 9th centuries, and whose history Photius <note>Photius I (c. 810–893), the Patriarch of Constantinople, wrote a famous history of the Paulician heresy.</note>...

<meta>This page continues the author's argument linking medieval heretical groups to ancient Manichaeanism. The text begins mid-word, completing the name "Photius" (the 9th-century Patriarch of Constantinople who wrote extensively against heresies). The previous page ended with a discussion of the historian Isaac de Beausobre's defense of the Manichaeans, which the author here begins to refute.</meta>

Pho-tius (1), and Peter the Sicilian—whom Rader (2) extracted from a Vatican manuscript—have pursued these matters: the latter of whom testifies; *the Paulicians do not differ in any way from the Manichaeans... who, although they repeatedly claim to be foreign to the impurities of the Manichaeans, are nevertheless the most watchful guardians and defenders of their dogmas.* Indeed, I believe it has been noticed by others, and I infer it myself from the words in which anathema <note>A formal curse or excommunication by the Church.</note> is pronounced against Paul, Sergius, Tychicus, etc., that this specific formula is no older than the 9th century. 

But to return to the Albigensians <note>A medieval religious sect in Southern France, often accused of dualist "Cathar" heresies.</note>, according to Peter the Monk <note>Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay, a chronicler of the Albigensian Crusade.</note>, their dogmas included: *that all the Fathers of the Old Testament were damned, and that John the Baptist was one of the greater demons.* From Caesarius <note>Caesarius of Heisterbach, a Cistercian monk and author.</note>, we learn *they denied the resurrection of bodies; they rejected Baptism; and they blaspheme the sacrament of the Body and Blood of Christ.* He adds that metempsychosis <term>metempsychosis: the philosophical term for the transmigration of souls or reincarnation</term> was admitted by them; for he tells of a soldier who introduced the idea, and adds: *the fool believed, just like the rest of the Albigensians, that the soul, according to its merit, passes through various bodies, even those of animals and serpents.* William of Nangis reports, moreover, that these heretics *blasphemed the Blessed Mary, the Mother of God* (3). 

Beausobre (4) contends that the ancient Manichaeans did not hold such perverse views regarding the salvation of the Old Testament Fathers, nor regarding John the Baptist; but one may learn otherwise from the *Acts of Archelaus* <note>An early Christian polemic written against Mani, the founder of Manichaeism.</note>, known and authentic, which Epiphanius, Cyril of Jerusalem, Jerome, and other very ancient Fathers saw and included in their own writings. These were published by Zaccagni as they were found, albeit with some interpolations <note>Later insertions or alterations to a text.</note>: and in them, nothing is found that greatly contradicts these charges. 

Among the manuscripts of his Library (5), Photius lists a work by Agapius titled *Urania* (why Fabricius (6) makes her his wife, I do not clearly see), in which he explains his errors—that is, those of the Manichaeans: *among other things which Photius touched upon, he mocks the Old Testament, and Moses himself along with the Prophets; indeed, he even scoffs at the Forerunner of the Lord* <note>John the Baptist.</note>. And Beausobre wrongly rejects that testimony, as he does the other of Didymus of Alexandria (8). He might perhaps use a slightly more convenient excuse for Agapius if he were to deny that he was so ancient that he could bear testimony concerning the oldest Manichaeans. For I, that I might believe that Agapius to be the one who is numbered among the disciples of Mani himself by Peter the Sic-

<summary>This page argues that the medieval Albigensians and Paulicians inherited the radical dualism of the ancient Manichaeans, specifically their rejection of the Old Testament and their belief in reincarnation. The author cites several historical chroniclers and Church Fathers to refute the more lenient historical interpretation offered by Beausobre.</summary>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument linking the medieval Albigensians to the ancient Manichaean heresy by citing early church authorities and Byzantine historians. The previous page ended mid-word; this page begins a new sentence discussing the reliability of Peter the Sicilian.</meta>

I am not easily convinced by Peter the Sicilian (1), and I suspect rather that his work preceded the age of Photius <note>A 9th-century Patriarch of Constantinople and a prolific scholar.</note> by a little. But to me, the age of that man, or his testimony, is equally relevant: for it will be established that the Albigensians were the offspring of the Manichaeans of a later age. This is true: for all the dogmas we have expressed are found in them; and thus the praised Anathematisms <note>Formal curses or decrees of excommunication issued by the Church against specific heresies.</note> (2) state: *Anathema to those who treat the Holy Mother of God <note>original: "Deiparam"</note> Mary with insult;* (3) *I anathematize those denying the resurrection of the flesh;* (4) *I anathematize those who admit metempsychosis <term>metempsychosis: the philosophical concept of reincarnation or the transmigration of a soul into a new body</term>, which they themselves call the "transfer of souls" <note>original: "metangismon"</note> (a pouring from one vessel into another).*

Photius, in his history of the Paulicians (5), writes: *Blaspheming against our most holy Lady, the Mother of God, they do not shudder to say things that can neither be written nor heard.... In like manner, showering the communion of the precious Body and Blood of Christ our God with countless insults... they turn away from the holy Prophets, and all the ancient scripture, and other saints, whom they call thieves and robbers.*

Peter the Sicilian, proposing six main points, or as he calls them, "distinguishing marks" <note>original: "gnorismata"</note> of the Manichaean sect (6), lists after the first point (regarding the two principles of good and evil) a second: *that they reject the Mother of God, ever-Virgin, out of hatred, and say that Christ was not born from her*; and a third: *that they deny the divine and tremendous conversion <note>This refers to the doctrine of Transubstantiation, the belief that the bread and wine become the actual body and blood of Christ.</note> of the Body and Blood of our Lord Jesus Christ from the sacred Mysteries*; likewise a fifth: *that they do not admit the books of the Old Testament, and call the Prophets thieves and robbers.*

The same followed in the writings of Agapius, or Photius writing about Agapius: *He denies the resurrection of all and the Judgment, transferring these terms to other meanings... he carries on an intolerable hatred and implacable war with the Mother of God and ever-Virgin Mary... he does not recognize the Body and Blood of Christ as that which we Christians venerate, but as that which his own frenzied mind has devised... he approves of metempsychoses [reincarnations]*; these points indeed continually agree with the accounts of others.

Most of these things are even found in the works of those who are certainly ancient. For Theodoret says of the same people (7): *They reject the resurrection of bodies as a myth... they say that souls pass into bodies; and that some are sent into the bodies of birds, others into those of cattle, wild beasts, and serpents.* Epiphanius disputes at length against Mani regarding reincarnation (8), and in favor of the resurrection of bodies (9), which Philaster also recorded (10) that the Manichaeans denied along with the Last Judgment; and Rufinus (11) maintains they received this belief from Valentinus <note>A famous 2nd-century Gnostic teacher.</note>. I shall say nothing

<meta>This page continues the author's argument linking the medieval Albigensians to the ancient Manichaean heresy by examining their specific religious practices. It transitions from a list of 'distinguishing marks' of the sect to a broader critique of contemporary historians who defended these groups.</meta>

...specifically concerning the Eucharist. For it is well known that while our Eucharist is considered holy, they held it to be foul and detestable; although Beausobre <note>Isaac de Beausobre (1659–1738), a Protestant historian who wrote a famous defense of the Manichaeans.</note> denies this in our time (1). Regarding Baptism, the matter may seem more obscure (2): But they either used it at their own discretion or denied its necessity, as Tillemont <note>Sébastien le Nain de Tillemont, a 17th-century French ecclesiastical historian known for his accuracy.</note> has shown (3), and as Photius (4) notes: even if they rejected the baptism of salvation, they nevertheless pretended to accept it. I say nothing of marriage, which it is clear from Augustine and all others that the ancient Manichaeans condemned. Concerning the Albigensians, Peter [the Sicilian] testifies that they affirmed *sacred marriage is merely prostitution; nor can anyone be saved within it by begetting children, etc.* <note>original: "sacrum matrimonium meretricium esse; nec aliquem in ipso salvari posse, filios &c. generando"</note>; he adds that since they distinguished two classes among themselves—those called the **Perfect** and those named **Believers**—the **Perfect** feigned chastity and utterly detested the eating of meat, eggs, and cheese. It is surely clear that these practices are Manichaean in origin.

II. And thus far, in a single effort, we have achieved two things: we have explained what the primary dogmas of the Albigensians were, and we have asserted that these were the same as those of the ancient Manichaeans. Johann Christoph Wolf opposed the first of these points, while Beausobre opposed the second. Although Beausobre’s work certainly requires a longer and more diligent refutation—and I might add, by a more learned man—especially its second part, which deals with Dogma. For the first part, which is historical, has been recently and extensively refuted by the author of the *Exercises on the Works of Saint Leo*. Indeed, those books are written not only impiously, but in the manner of old knaves—that is, craftily and fraudulently—yet not in such a way that they can be ignored. To demonstrate his care and diligence, Beausobre breaks things down into minute detail; he inquires anxiously into every point, digresses into various curiosities, and cites a great abundance of uncommon authors. This is clearly done to entice and retain his readers, overwhelming them, if not by the weight of his arguments, then by their sheer number and volume. Furthermore, he clears the Manichaeans of certain charges as if they were imposed through slander, while admitting that other errors were indeed theirs, yet he crafts an excuse for them. In this excuse, he attempts with great zeal to obscure Apostolic tradition and weaken all arguments for the Catholic faith. To do this more freely and to lead the matter where he wishes, he undermines the credibility of the ancient writers whose authority he fears in the Preface prefixed to his other books. He claims some had not seen the Manichaean books and therefore followed uncertain rumors, and others, he claims, did not understand them. Among the former group, he includes Cyril of Jerusalem, even though he cannot deny what Archelaus <note>Archelaus was a 3rd-century bishop whose "Acts of Archelaus" is a primary source for early Manichaeism.</note> recounts.

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of Isaac de Beausobre, a historian who defended the Manichaeans. The previous page established the author's intent to prove that the Albigensians shared the same "monstrous" traits as the ancient Manichaeans, despite Beausobre's attempts to rehabilitate the sect's reputation.</meta>

...than to have insisted upon it, which Beausobre despises, so that he remains in much greater uncertainty; likewise, he condemns the credulity of Theodoret, whose erudition he is forced to praise. Among the latter group, he dares to include St. Epiphanius, as if he did not know Syriac and as if, in the book titled *The Treasure* by Mani—which Epiphanius himself testifies to have read—he saw things entirely different from what was actually written. He praises Titus of Bostra immensely; but after he finds him saying certain things about the <term>Economy of Christ<note>In early Christian theology, "Economy" (oeconomia) refers to God's administration of the world and the specific plan of salvation through the Incarnation of Jesus.</note></term> which oppose his own system, he then denies that faith should be placed in him, claiming it is not credible that Titus found those things in the *Treasure* in which he found the rest. He condemns Leo the Great, John of Damascus, and the formulas of faith, even though they agree both with themselves and with the historians. 

What of Augustine? Among the Manichaeans, Beausobre says, Augustine was a <term>Hearer<note>The "Hearers" (Auditores) were the lower rank of Manichaean followers who were allowed to marry and eat meat, unlike the ascetic elite.</note></term>, not an <term>Elect<note>The "Elect" (Electi) were the inner circle of the Manichaean sect who practiced strict asceticism and were privy to the sect's deepest secrets.</note></term>; therefore, he knew nothing of their secrets. But Augustine disputed with the Elect, such as Fortunatus. Moreover, Tyrbo was also among the Hearers. Here, Beausobre, when he believes he can be helped by Tyrbo, wants the Hearers to know all the Manichaean secrets just as well as the Elect (1). Thus, he who repeatedly boasts that he is not influenced by partisan zeal allows himself to be carried away by such a great love for his Manichaeans that he is inconsistent with himself. 

And he seems to have erred for the same reason when he denies that what Augustine relates about the Manichaeans could be true, arguing it is not credible that Augustine would have embraced such <note>The author uses "portenta" (monsters/portents) to describe the "bizarre and unnatural" doctrines of the sect.</note> monstrous things when he joined the Manichaeans. Yet he admits monstrous errors and what he himself calls (2) a *terrible paradox* among the Church Fathers after the Council of Nicaea; regarding which Council of Nicaea, he follows monstrous ideas himself, since he denies there was such great consensus among the Fathers, and introduces so many <note>original: "Hæreticos"</note> heretical Bishops (3) into that most serious and holy agreement; and he dares either to deny or to cast suspicion upon those things which are supported by the common testimony of ancient writers. 

But the nature of our undertaking does not allow us to proceed further: and we would have remained silent about these things altogether, had they not been necessary for establishing the authority of those writers whom we use; lest, being ignorant of what Beausobre had opposed to them, we might nevertheless seem to call upon them in this cause. I will, however, add another point that pertains to declaring the doctrines of the Manichaeans, and I will endeavor to illustrate those things which belong to the Christian faith, contrary to what Beausobre does. For in Book III, having set out to explain the Manichaean system and the nature of God as they believed it to be, he says that Mani made this nature a certain sincere and most pure light; that this had been handed down by others and even pleased the Christian Fathers; but that the Manichaeans established that this light was by no means corporeal.

<meta>This page continues the critique of Isaac de Beausobre’s history of Manichaeism. The text flows from the previous page's mention of "Beausob..." which is completed here as "corporeal," referring to debates on whether God possesses a physical form.</meta>

...poreal. Thus far, not bad. But he immediately overturns the last point he had established, decreeing that it <note>God or the Divine essence</note> could nevertheless be seen with these very eyes of the body, provided they were strengthened by celestial and Divine power. He claims that this was the opinion of a great number of the Greek Fathers; yet he cites no others than some poem of <term>Damascene<note>St. John of Damascus (c. 675–749), a Syrian monk and influential theologian of the Eastern Church.</note></term> and a passage from <term>Nazianzen’s<note>St. Gregory of Nazianzus (c. 329–390), one of the "Cappadocian Fathers" known for his contributions to Trinitarian theology.</note></term> 40th Oration, which does not settle the matter at all. He devotes himself entirely to the 14th-century Greeks disputing over the <term>Thaboritic light<note>The "Thaboritic light" refers to the light seen by the Apostles at the Transfiguration of Jesus on Mount Tabor; a major controversy in the 1300s (Hesychasm) centered on whether this light was a created phenomenon or the uncreated energy of God.</note></term>, about which <term>Petavius<note>Denis Pétau (1583–1652), a French Jesuit theologian and scholar.</note></term> (1), <term>Thomassinus<note>Louis Thomassin (1619–1695), a French Oratorian theologian.</note></term> (2), and other theologians have long since written; to these he adds the authority of <term>Leo Allatius<note>Leo Allatius (c. 1586–1669), a Greek scholar and librarian at the Vatican who sought to reconcile the Eastern and Western churches.</note></term> (3).

It appears, therefore, that he wished to mock the unwary or the unskilled. Indeed, the <term>authors of the Memoirs of Trévoux<note>The "Trevotienses" refers to the Jesuit editors of the "Journal de Trévoux," a famous 18th-century literary and scientific journal.</note></term> (4) rebuked Allatius quite sharply. We can forgive that learned man <note>Allatius</note> for clinging too tenaciously to his fellow Greeks and for treating the Fathers more in the manner of a grammarian than a theologian. However, we cannot forgive Beausobre at all when, in Book III, chapters 1 and 2, he strives to tear down the Christian dogma regarding the nature of God as free from all matter and body. He denies that it is contrary to the Christian Faith to deny that God is entirely spiritual, in the sense that He is commonly believed and said to be.

He could have been taught better on this point by his own Manichaeans, had he not preferred to turn aside to <term>Vorstius<note>Conrad Vorstius (1569–1622), a theologian accused of Socinianism and teaching that God has a physical location or body.</note></term> and the <term>Socinians<note>A rationalist sect that denied the Trinity and often held heterodox views on the nature of the divine substance.</note></term> in this passage. For the Manichaeans called God "Light" in such a way that they established another principle entirely opposed to Him, which had nothing in common with Him: matter. Therefore, God was removed from all matter. Witnesses to this fact are not only our own writers, who state this as the primary of their dogmas—among whom is **Theodoret** (5): *“He <note>Manes</note> said there are two unbegotten and eternal beings, God and Matter, and he called God Light and Matter Darkness, and Light good and Darkness evil... he said God was removed from matter, and was entirely unknown, both He from matter, and matter from Him.”* 

But even **Mani** himself, according to the testimony of **Epiphanius** (6), began his famous *Treasure* in this way: *“There was God and matter, light and darkness, good and evil, which were so utterly contrary to each other that one had no communication with the other.”* What then shall we say? Only that when Mani said God was light, he named God by the name of light in the same way our painters are accustomed to represent a human soul in the form of a small flame or fire; for, as they express it through a certain visual form, so we express the thing we wish to describe with a certain name.

<meta>Continuing from the previous page's discussion of Isaac de Beausobre and the nature of the divine, this page addresses the terminology used for "spirit" and "matter," the nature of angels, and the beliefs of the Albigensians.</meta>

# 9
## B

...is necessary. But if that name belongs to a corporeal thing—that is, shared with this light of ours—this occurs in other words, even in the one thought to be the most expressive of all: the Latin *spirit* <note>original: spiritus</note>, the Greek *breath* or *spirit* <note>original: πνεῦμα, pneuma</note>, and the Hebrew *spirit* <note>original: רוּחַ, ruach</note>. The things which <term>Beausobre<note>Isaac de Beausobre (1659–1738), a French Protestant historian known for his critical study of Manichaeism.</note></term> (1) discusses concerning *matter* <note>original: ὕλη, hyle</note>—as if the Manichaeans' concept of matter were not correctly explained by that name, and that the <term>Church Fathers<note>The influential early Christian theologians whose writings established the foundations of Christian doctrine.</note></term> were not concerned about it—tend toward the same end as his other arguments produced against their authority and testimony. Therefore, far from this Manichaean dogma being opposed to Christian dogma, it is found to be largely in agreement. This is all the more significant because the <term>Manichaeans<note>Followers of Manichaeism, a major dualist religion founded in the 3rd century by the Persian prophet Mani, which taught a struggle between a good spiritual world of light and an evil material world of darkness.</note></term> truly admitted one God; thus, we determine that they were by no means polytheists. Although they admitted two principles, they by no means admitted two Gods; and on this point—lest we be called enemies of the man—Beausobre decided correctly. 

Just as we say the Fathers should be understood in this way when speaking of God and declaring Him to be light or fire, so too should many of them be understood when they say nearly the same things in their discourse on Angels—though we admit not all can be explained in this way. But here too Beausobre (2) employs exaggeration; and although theologians seem generally to agree that it is not to be held as a certain and defined matter of faith—even after the <term>Lateran Council<note>Likely the Fourth Lateran Council of 1215, which defined the nature of God's creation, stating that God created both spiritual (angelic) and corporeal (earthly) beings.</note></term>—that Angels (especially the fallen ones) are entirely void and free from all matter and every body, and that asserting such a thing is rash; nonetheless, we are of the opinion that there has always existed in the Church a faith and tradition that Angels are *incorporeal*, *immaterial*, and finally *spirits*. Although this tradition may not have been expressed with these specific names and words, it was expressed in others which signified the same thing at that time as these words do now.

### III.
And thus far concerning the errors of the <term>Albigensians<note>A dualist sect in Southern France during the 12th and 13th centuries. Like the Manichaeans, they believed in two opposing principles of good and evil; they were the targets of the Albigensian Crusade.</note></term>, in which these more recent heretics are found to agree entirely with the ancient Manichaeans. <term>Caesarius<note>Caesarius of Heisterbach (c. 1180–1240), a Cistercian monk and chronicler.</note></term> adds: *they mock whatever benefit is bestowed upon the dead by the living; they say that going to churches or praying in them is of no use*. <term>Peter the Monk<note>Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay (d. 1218), a chronicler who documented the Albigensian Crusade.</note></term> attributes other things to these same people, whom he seems to address precisely by the name of 'Heretics': *Likewise, the Heretics used to say that the good God had two wives, Collant and Colibant, and from them he procreated sons and daughters*. He adds certain things about other Heretics, but attributes these to all of them: *All these members of the Antichrist, the firstborn of Satan... infected the province of <term>Narbonne<note>A city and region in Southern France, then a stronghold of the Albigensian movement.</note></term> with the poison of their treachery; they said that almost the entire Roman Church was a "den of thieves," and that she herself was that "harlot" of whom one reads in the <term>Apocalypse<note>The Book of Revelation; the "harlot" refers to the Whore of Babylon, a figure often used by medieval dissenters to criticize the corruption of the Papacy.</note></term>.* <term>William of Puylaurens<note>Guillaume de Puylaurens (c. 1200–1275), a chaplain and chronicler of the Albigensian wars.</note></term> [mentions] the famous...

(2) Part 2, book 5, chapter 6.

<summary>This page analyzes the theological nuances of Manichaean monotheism versus dualism and the traditional view of angels as incorporeal spirits. It also transitions into a historical critique of the Albigensians, detailing their rejection of Church authority and the unusual mythological accusations leveled against them.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the critique of Isaac de Beausobre’s history of Manichaeism. It specifically addresses the debate over whether God possesses a corporeal (physical) nature and how the "Light of Tabor" was interpreted by Eastern Orthodox theologians and the Manichaeans.</meta>

...poreal <note>original: corporeum</note>. So far, not bad. But the final point he had posed, he immediately overturned, deciding nevertheless that it [the divine light] could be seen by these physical eyes, provided they were strengthened by celestial and Divine power. He claimed this same view was held by a great number of Greek Fathers; yet he calls upon no others than some poem of <term>John of Damascus<note>St. John of Damascus (c. 675–749), a Syrian monk and priest known for his defense of icons and systematic theology.</note></term>, and a passage from <term>Gregory of Nazianzus's<note>Gregory of Nazianzus (c. 329–390), a 4th-century Archbishop of Constantinople and one of the "Cappadocian Fathers."</note></term> 40th Oration, which does not settle the matter at all. He saves his full argument for the 14th-century Greeks disputing over the <term>Thaboritic light<note>The "Light of Mount Tabor," referring to the divine light witnessed by the Apostles at the Transfiguration of Jesus; a central topic in the 14th-century Hesychast controversy.</note></term>, about whom <term>Petavius<note>Denis Pétau (1583–1652), a French Jesuit theologian known for his work on the history of dogma.</note></term> (1), <term>Thomassinus<note>Louis Thomassin (1619–1695), a French Oratorian theologian.</note></term> (2), and other theologians have long since written; to these he adds the authority of <term>Leo Allatius<note>Leo Allatius (c. 1586–1669), a Greek scholar and librarian at the Vatican who sought to reconcile the Eastern and Western Churches.</note></term> (3). 

Thus it appears he wished to mock the unwary or the unskilled. Indeed, the <term>Editors of Trévoux<note>The "Trevoltins," Jesuit scholars who published the "Mémoires de Trévoux," a famous 18th-century literary and scientific journal.</note></term> (4) rebuked Allatius rather harshly; we, however, forgive the learned man, who clung too tenaciously to his fellow Greeks, and who here handled the Fathers more in the manner of grammarians than theologians. 

We can by no means forgive <term>Beausobre<note>Isaac de Beausobre (1659–1738), the Huguenot historian whose work on Manichaeism is the subject of this critique.</note></term>, when in Book III, chapters 1 and 2, he strives to tear down the Christian dogma that God’s nature is free from all matter and body, and denies that it is contrary to the Christian Faith to deny that He is entirely spiritual, as He is customarily believed and said to be. He could have been taught here by his own Manichaeans, had he not preferred at this point to turn aside toward <term>Vorstius<note>Conrad Vorstius (1569–1622), a Remonstrant theologian accused of denying God's absolute spirituality.</note></term> and the <term>Socinians<note>A rationalist Christian sect that often held heterodox views on the nature of God, sometimes suggesting God had a form of "spiritual body."</note></term>. For the Manichaeans spoke of God as light in such a way that they established matter as another principle entirely opposed to Him, which had nothing in common with Him; therefore, God was removed from all matter. 

Not only do our own writers testify to this, stating what was the chief of their dogmas—among whom is <term>Theodoret<note>Theodoret of Cyrrhus (c. 393–457), a bishop and influential theologian.</note></term> (5): *He said there were two unbegotten and eternal things: God and matter; and he called God light, and matter darkness; and light good, and darkness evil... he said God was removed from matter, and was entirely unknown, both He by matter, and matter by Him*; but <term>Mani<note>The 3rd-century Persian prophet and founder of Manichaeism.</note></term> himself also began his famous *Treasury* in this way, according to the testimony of <term>Epiphanius<note>Epiphanius of Salamis (c. 310–403), a bishop known for his "Panarion," a work cataloging heresies.</note></term> (6): *There was God and matter, light and darkness, good and evil, which were so utterly contrary to one another that no communion joined one with the other* <note>original Greek: ἦν θεὸς καὶ ὕλη, φῶς, καὶ σκότος, ἀγαθὸν, καὶ κακὸν τοῖς πᾶσιν ἀκρῶς ἐναντία, ὡς κατὰ μηδὲν ἐπικοινωνεῖν θάτερον θατέρῳ.</note>. What then shall we say? Except that when he said God was light, the Manichaean named God by the name of light just as our painters are accustomed to represent the human soul in the likeness of a small flame or fire; since, just as they must express the thing we want by a certain appearance, so we must express it by some name...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on whether the Manichaean conception of God as "Light" implies a physical body. It transitions from a defense of Manichaean monotheism to the errors attributed to the Albigensians by medieval chroniclers.</meta>

...it is necessary. For if that name belongs to a corporeal thing—that is, common to our light here—this also occurs in other words, even in those considered the most significant of all: the Latin *spiritus* <note>spirit</note>, the Greek *pneuma* <note>original: πνεῦμα, meaning spirit or breath</note>, and the Hebrew *ruach* <note>original: רוּחַ, meaning spirit, wind, or breath</note>. What Beausobre <margin>(1) Book 5, chapter 6.</margin> discusses regarding *matter* <note>original Greek: ὕλη (hyle)</note>—as if the Manichaean *matter* were not correctly explained by that name, and as if the Church Fathers were not concerned about it—belongs to the same category as his other arguments brought against their authority and the testimonies he produced. So far is it from being the case that this dogma of the Manichaeans opposes Christian doctrine, that it is found to agree with it most closely. This is all the more valid because the Manichaeans truly admitted only one God; thus we determine that they were by no means polytheists. While they admitted two principles <note>referring to the dualist struggle between Light and Darkness</note>, they by no means admitted two Gods; and in this (lest we be called enemies of the man), Beausobre has correctly established the point. Just as we say the Fathers are to be understood in this way when they speak of God and declare Him to be light or fire; so also many of them expressed almost the same thing in their discourse on Angels; although we must admit that not all of them can be explained in this way. 

But here too Beausobre <margin>(2) Part 2, book 5, chapter 6.</margin> uses exaggeration; and although theologians almost seem to agree on this—that it must not be held as a certain and defined point of faith, even after the <term>Lateran Council<note>The Fourth Lateran Council (1215), which defined that God created both spiritual and corporeal creatures.</note></term>, that angels (especially the evil ones) are entirely void and free of all matter and every body, and indeed, to assert that would be rash—nevertheless, we are of the opinion that faith and tradition have always existed in the Church regarding the fact that Angels are *incorporeal*, *immaterial*, and finally *spirits*. This is true even if that tradition was not conceived in these specific names and words, but in entirely different ones, which nevertheless signified at that time the same thing as those words do now.

### III.

And so much for the errors of the <term>Albigensians<note>A medieval dualist sect in Southern France, often linked by Catholic polemicists to the ancient Manichaeans.</note></term>, in which these more recent heretics are found to agree entirely with the ancient Manichaeans. <term>Caesarius<note>Caesarius of Heisterbach (c. 1180–1240), a Cistercian monk and chronicler.</note></term> adds: *they mock whatever benefit is bestowed upon the dead by the living; they say that going to churches, or praying in them, is of no use.* <term>Peter the Monk<note>Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay (d. c. 1218), a chronicler of the Albigensian Crusade.</note></term> attributes other things to those whom he seems to address precisely by the name of Heretics: *Likewise, the Heretics said that the good God had two wives, Collant and Colibant, and from them he procreated sons and daughters.* He adds certain things about other heretics; but to all of them he attributes these: *All these members of the Antichrist, the firstborn of Satan... infected the province of Narbonne with the poison of their perfidy; they said almost the whole Roman Church was a den of thieves, and that she was that harlot of whom one reads in the Apocalypse.* <term>William of Puylaurens<note>Guillaume de Puylaurens (c. 1200–1275), a chronicler of the Albigensian Crusade.</note></term> [wrote] a famous

<meta>catchword: famous [celebrem]</meta>

<meta>This page continues the historical investigation into the Albigensian heresy, transitioning from a discussion on the nature of God to the specific accusations leveled against the Roman Church by medieval sectarian leaders. It focuses on the continuity between ancient dualist heresies and medieval dissent.</meta>

...relating a <note>The catchword "opi-" from the previous page combines with "-lebrem" to form "celebrem," meaning "famous."</note> famous debate (1) held by Catholics with the Albigensians <note>A dualist religious movement in Southern France during the 12th and 13th centuries, often targeted by the Inquisition.</note> at Montreal <note>original: Montem Regalem, a stronghold of the Albigensians near Carcassonne.</note>: he says, *that the fundamental argument on the part of the Heretics was that the one who was the leader among them called the Roman Church—which the Bishop of Osma <note>Diego de Acebo, who traveled with Saint Dominic to preach against the Albigensians.</note> had defended—not the holy Church nor the Bride of Christ, but the Church of the Devil and the doctrine of Demons; and that it was that Babylon which John called in the Apocalypse <note>The Book of Revelation</note> the mother of fornications and abominations: drunk with the blood of the Saints and Martyrs of Jesus Christ, and that its ordination was neither holy nor good, nor established by the Lord Jesus Christ; and that Christ and the Apostles never ordained or established the Order of the mass, as it is conducted today.*

A similar account is read in the *Life of Gregory IX*, which is attributed to Bernard Gui <note>A famous Dominican friar and Inquisitor.</note> (2) concerning the Albigensians: *For the infidel Heretics call the Catholic Ministers of the Roman Church "Devils," and the Church itself the "synagogue of Satan."* And indeed, Lucas of Tuy <note>Also known as Lucas Tudensis, a Spanish bishop known for his polemics against heresy.</note> attributes those words of Caesar <note>Likely referring to the chronicler Caesar of Heisterbach.</note> either to the Albigensians or their supporters; *For they dare,* he says (3), *to contend that we are aided by the help of the Saints in absolutely no way... In this they seem to favor them in part... asserting that the Prelates of the Church of Christ are in no way able to help the souls of the faithful departed with Remissions of Indulgences.* It is also found in the same author: *They lie that the body was made by the Devil... that no soul of any Saint ascends to heaven before the day of judgment, and that souls suffer punishment nowhere except in Hell: nor do they even have knowledge of those whom they loved while they lived in this world.*

Whether all these things belonged to the ancient Manichaeans <note>Followers of Mani who believed in a cosmic struggle between Light and Darkness.</note> or Paulicians <note>A medieval sect from the Byzantine Empire often seen as ancestors to the Albigensians.</note>, I would not dare to affirm. In Peter of Sicily, one reads (4) only *that they keep Presbyters <note>Priests</note> and Elders from the administration of the Church;* and Photius [notes] they admit neither Presbyters nor Priests of the Catholic Church (5). Caesar, however, affirms that they drew their dogmas from elsewhere: some from errors which Origen <note>An early Christian scholar whose works on the pre-existence of souls were later deemed controversial.</note> is said to have written in *On First Principles* <note>original: Periarchon</note>, adding many things which they had even fashioned from their own hearts. But in this very thing, they did not depart from the practice of ancient Heretics, who were accustomed to continually add new errors to the prior errors of their Ancestors; and to tread more closely in the footsteps of the Manichaeans themselves, who *possessed not just one kind of depravity, but generally a mixture of all errors and impieties at once,* as Saint Leo <note>Pope Leo the Great.</note> says (6); *and from diverse heresies and pagan dogmas they established this perverse [doctrine] of theirs.*

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the historical views and labels applied to medieval heretical groups, specifically addressing how contemporary and later historians categorized their beliefs in relation to ancient precedents like Manichaeism.</meta>

opinion <note>The text continues from the previous page's discussion of a specific viewpoint.</note>; just as is read in the *Life of St. Porphyry*, written in the 5th century by his disciple Mark (1).

IV. Truly, at this point, we must examine the opinion of certain people who, when they read that these were the dogmas of the Albigensians <note>A dualist sect in Southern France</note>, confuse the Waldenses <note>A movement of "Poor Men" emphasizing poverty and lay preaching</note> and the Albigensians; and for the same reason, they rank them with the Petrobrusians <note>Followers of Peter of Bruys, who rejected infant baptism and the veneration of the cross</note>, or the Petrobrusians with the "New Manichaeans" <note>A reference to the ancient dualist religion of Manichaeism, often used as a label for medieval heresies</note>. Regarding the first point—that is, the failure to distinguish the Albigensians from the Waldenses—Jean Benoist <note>Joannes Benedictus</note> (2) long ago complained in his *History of the Albigensians* that many had done this, indeed most who wrote about the Albigensians. Later, in his *Dissertation on the Waldenses* (3), the most learned Thomas Agustinus Ricchinius—whom we gladly acknowledge having profited from greatly, and whom we even prefer and boast of—noted the same. But our historians teach that they must be entirely distinguished: I speak of Caesarius <note>Caesarius of Heisterbach, a Cistercian monk and author</note>, who expressly separates one from the other (4), and Peter of Vaulx-Cernay (5), who likewise calls these people specifically by the name "Waldenses" after he had discussed his "Heretics" (that is, the Albigensians). Indeed, he expressly affirms that these were far *less* *perverse* than the others; and he circumscribes their principal errors in four points: *namely, the wearing of sandals after the manner of the Apostles; and in that they said one should by no means swear an oath, or kill; and furthermore, in this, that they asserted that any of them in a time of necessity—provided they had sandals—could consecrate the Body of Christ without having received orders from a Bishop.* Other and much more serious things are attributed to the Waldenses by those who are thought to have written treatises against them; these were published by Gretser (6): Ebrardus, Bernardus, and Ermengardus. Out of these, Bernard <note>Likely Bernard of Fontcaude, though the author associates him with the Bishop of Narbonne</note>—whom Gretser proclaims to be more learned than the others (he is perhaps also older, if, as it appears, he took part in the disputation or judgment held against the Waldenses by Bernard, Bishop of Narbonne, who held that See from the year 1181 to 1191 (7))—attributes these dogmas to them: *that the Supreme Pontiff and other Prelates are not to be obeyed; that it belongs also to laymen and women to preach; that alms, fasts, solemn Masses, and other prayers do not benefit the faithful departed; that there is no Purgatory, but that spirits released from the flesh go immediately to heaven or hell; that one should not pray in a church, nor should a building be called a "church."* And yet these were the very things which were attributed <note>original: "tribu-" (completed on the next page as "tribuebant")</note> to the Albigensians—

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the historical confusion between different dissident groups, specifically focusing on how later scholars and contemporary witnesses like Caesarius of Heisterbach attempted to distinguish the "Poor Men of Lyon" (Waldenses) from the dualist Albigensians.</meta>

Caesarius <note>original: "buebat Cæfarius", continuing from "exhibebat" on the previous page</note> held this view. Ebrardus <note>Ebrard of Béthune, a 12th-century grammarian and anti-heretical writer</note> also indicates that the errors of the heretics he was refuting are the same ones we attribute to the Albigensians <note>A dualist sect in Southern France who believed in two opposing principles of good and evil</note>; and we can affirm the same regarding Ermengardus <note>Ermengard of Béziers, a former Waldensian who returned to Catholicism and wrote against his former peers</note>. And it was not surprising that this misled men who were otherwise learned, and caused the Albigensians to be confused with the Waldenses, due to those inscriptions titled *Against the Waldenses*. However, the illustrious Ricchinius <note>Thomas Agustinus Ricchinius, an 18th-century editor of inquisitorial texts</note> rightly observed that those titles were added by a hand other than the Author’s. Therefore, these writers who are presented as if they wrote against the Waldenses actually fought more truly against the Albigensians. Unless, indeed, we prefer this explanation: that those Waldenses who had already soaked up the errors of the Albigensians and contracted their entire stain were the ones being attacked. That this is the case is confirmed by, among others, Stephen of Bourbon <note>A Dominican inquisitor and historian</note> (also known as Stephen of Bellavilla) in his book *On the Seven Gifts of the Holy Spirit*, a portion of which the illustrious Argentré has shared with us (1). For he described the Waldenses in such a way as to establish that their principal error was that they prescriptively imposed the life of the Apostles upon everyone, condemning *all who possess earthly goods* <note>original: "Omnes terrena possidentes"</note>. He adds: *Thus* <gloss>so</gloss> *at first, out of presumption and the usurpation of the Apostolic office, they fell* <gloss>so</gloss> *into disobedience, then into contumacy* <note>stubborn resistance to authority</note>, *and finally into the sentence of excommunication.* <gloss>so</gloss> *Afterward, having been expelled from that land, they were called to the Council that was held at Rome before the Lateran* <note>The Third Lateran Council in 1179</note>, *and they were judged to be persistent* <gloss>so</gloss> *schismatics. Later, in the lands of Provence and Lombardy, mixing themselves with other heretics and drinking in and carrying their errors, they were judged heretics, most hostile and* <gloss>so</gloss> *dangerous to the Church.* <gloss>so</gloss> This, therefore, is our opinion, which I see is also favored by most learned men. Pierre-Claude Fontenai <note>A Jesuit historian of the French Church</note> (2) believes that the Waldenses did not immediately defect from the Catholic faith, until after they were unable to obtain the approval for their way of life which they were seeking from the Roman Pontiff (as the Abbot of Ursberg (3) relates). The opinion of the illustrious Ricchinius (4) is almost the same: that in the beginning, the Waldenses erred in only a few things, but they erred enormously after the Fourth Lateran Council <note>held in 1215</note>. *Whether*, he says, *because being intermingled with the Albigensians* <gloss>so</gloss> *they drank in many of their condemnations through that wicked association, or because for those who once decline from the truth of the Faith and unity, the descent through the precipice toward always new errors is easy.* <gloss>so</gloss> Just how much they erred is well demonstrated by the same author using the ancient writer Reinerus Saccho <note>A former Cathar who became a Dominican inquisitor and wrote a famous treatise on heretics</note>.

V. The most learned Bishop of Meaux, Bossuet, contends that the Petrobrusians <note>Followers of Peter of Bruys</note> and the Henricians <note>Followers of Henry of Lausanne</note> should be numbered among the Manichaeans.

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the classification of heretical groups. The previous page discussed how the Waldenses were often confused with the Albigensians in historical records. This page shifts focus to Peter of Bruys and whether his followers should truly be categorized as "Manichaeans," a term typically reserved for those who believe in a dualistic struggle between good and evil.</meta>

Bossuet <note>original: "Bossuetius"; Jacques-Bénigne Bossuet (1627–1704), a prominent French bishop and theologian who wrote on the "variations" of Protestant churches</note> (1), who was joined by Mabillon in the Preface to the works of Saint Bernard (2). Indeed, if we understand as "Manichaeans" anyone who admitted something of what these new Manichaeans put forward, then let us call them Manichaeans. But if by this name we wish to include only those who adopted the principal tenets and <note>original: "γνωρίσματα"</note> distinguishing marks of Manichaean doctrine, then I fear whether they can truly be called or considered Manichaeans. Abelard <note>Peter Abelard (1079–1142), the famous medieval philosopher and theologian</note>, who describes Peter of Bruys to us (3), affirms that he erred in such a way that he forced many to be rebaptized, *and judged that the venerable sign of the Lord’s Cross should be entirely removed, and further asserted that the Sacrament of the Altar should no longer be celebrated at all*. I pass over what is held here concerning baptism; namely, that Peter compelled many to receive baptism again, which signifies that he wished it to be necessary, or at least not entirely superfluous. Whereas—to say nothing of the ancient Manichaeans whom I mentioned before—the "new" ones, or Albigensians, according to the testimony of Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay <note>original: "Petro Vallicernensi"; a chronicler of the Albigensian Crusade</note> (4), *affirmed that the wave of holy baptism did not differ from river water*. But even if I pass over this, there is nothing there by which you could recognize Manichaeans.

Peter of Cluny <note>also known as Peter the Venerable (c. 1092–1156), Abbot of Cluny, who wrote a famous treatise against the Petrobrusians</note> undertook a fuller description of this same heresy and numbered five main points (5):

*I. That small children established below the age of understanding cannot be saved by the baptism of Christ; therefore, when they grew up and reached that age, he compelled them to be baptized.*

*II. That the construction of temples or churches ought not to happen; furthermore, that those already built ought to be pulled down, and that sacred places for praying are not necessary for Christians.*

*III. He commanded that sacred crosses be broken and burned.*

*IV. He not only denied the truth of the Body and Blood of the Lord offered daily and continually through the Sacrament in the Church, but he decreed it to be absolutely nothing, and that it ought not to be offered to God.*

*V. He ridiculed sacrifices, offerings, alms, and other good deeds performed by the living faithful for the departed faithful, affirming that these could not help any dead person even in the slightest degree.*

There is no "scent" of Manichaeism here, unless we prefer to make things "Manichaean" that they simply held in common with others, or which some members of that sect added to Manichaean beliefs as if they were corollaries. But by that same reasoning, we will mix all sects together, and Pelagians <note>followers of Pelagius, who emphasized human free will over divine grace</note> will be Manichaeans; for Augustine himself (6) observed that they agreed on many things. What I have said about Peter of Bruys applies equally to Henry <note>Henry of Lausanne, a 12th-century reformer and contemporary of Peter of Bruys</note>, whom [he involved] in the same errors...

(6) Book 2, *Against Two Letters of the Pelagians*, chapter 2.

<summary>This page argues that Peter of Bruys and his followers (Petrobrusians) were not true Manichaeans, as their errors regarding baptism, the Cross, and the Eucharist did not align with the dualistic core of Manichaean doctrine.</summary>
<keywords>Peter of Bruys, Petrobrusians, Manichaeism, Albigensians, Peter the Venerable, Abelard, baptism, Eucharist, heresy, Henry of Lausanne</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's investigation into medieval heresies, moving from a general classification of the Petrobrusians and Henricians to a specific identification of the "Cologne heretics" as Cathars.</meta>

...lingered, as the Abbot of Cluny <note>original: "Cluniacensis", referring to Peter the Venerable</note> records; and the points he added are peaceful and almost identical, such as the one Baronius (1) cites from the Cluniac source: *God is mocked by Church songs* <note>original: "Irrideri Deum canticis Ecclesiasticis"</note>. Therefore, neither the **Petrobrusians** nor the **Henricians** are **Manichaeans**. These were the characteristics of Manichaeism: that they either assert two Principles of reality <note>A dualist belief in two opposing forces of Good and Evil</note>, or if they admit one creator, they nonetheless say that visible things were distinguished or completed by the Devil; this we know especially from the Venerable Moneta (2). And because the flesh was the work of the Devil, they condemned marriages, or at most admitted the marriage of those (as Eckbert (3) records) who come together as virgins; yet even they could not be saved unless they eventually separated. From this also came the prohibition against eating meat and milk. I do not say all these traits must appear at once, especially since they studiously hid them (which Bossuet (4) thought should be counted among the characteristics of Manichaeism); but something of them always leaks out, either through observation of the heretics' discipline or through their habits of speech.

Hence, while I denied that the Petrobrusians and Henricians were Manichaeans, I admit the **Cologne Heretics** <note>A group discovered in Cologne in the 1140s</note> were Manichaeans, against whom St. Bernard acted in *Sermons 65 and 66 on the Song of Songs*. Mabillon (5) admits these were somewhat different from the Henricians; yet he maintains they sprang from the same root, indeed, from the workshop <note>i.e., the influence</note> of Tanchelin <note>A radical preacher in Antwerp who opposed the church hierarchy</note>. I confess I also cannot agree with that learned man. I am entirely persuaded they should be called **Cathars**, and that they are the very same Cathars against whom Eckbert fought at almost the same time in those sermons of his, and I believe I will provide clear evidence of this.

**First**, they also—as Mabillon noted—condemned marriages, making an exception for those entered into by virgins. In his *1st Sermon on the Cathars*, Eckbert relates this; indeed, in his *5th Sermon*, he affirms this comes from the teaching of Hartuvinus.

**II.** *They are persuaded*, says Bernard (6) regarding the Cologne group he opposes, *that they have the power daily at their table to consecrate the Body and Blood of Christ, to nourish themselves into the Body of Christ and his members.* But Eckbert says of his own subjects (7): *From one man who had left your narrow circles I heard one such piece of your wisdom: "Your body is the Lord's, and you make the Body of the Lord when you bless your bread, and refresh your body from it..." yet this way of speaking is never found in the scriptures, that the body of any man, however holy, should be called the Body of the Lord.*

III.
