<meta>This page continues the investigation into 12th-century heretical groups, specifically moving from the general characteristics of Manichaeism discussed on the previous page to the specific testimonies of St. Bernard of Clairvaux and Eckbert of Schönau regarding the "Cathars" of Cologne and the origins of the Albigensian movement.</meta>

III. Ask them for the Author of their sect; they will provide no one, says Bernard (1); and a little later (2), *they boast that they are successors of the Apostles, and call themselves Apostolicals*. Those followers of Eckbert <note>Eckbert of Schönau, a 12th-century writer who wrote against the Cathars</note> also named no parent of their sect; they called themselves **Cathars** <note>From the Greek 'katharos', meaning 'pure'</note>, **Piphli**, or **Weavers** <note>original: "Texerant"; heresy was often associated with the weavers' guild in medieval cities</note>; and besides *they say they live the life of the Apostles*, says Eckbert (3); but they are contrary to the holy faith, etc. I pass over what both relate regarding their hypocrisy and hidden doctrine, and Bernard’s assertion (4) that those he rebukes *follow the dictates of the Manichaean madness* <note>original: "insania Manichæi præscribere"</note>. And indeed, not only Eckbert but others testify that Cathars, or Manichaeans, existed in Cologne during these times. Caesarius <note>Caesarius of Heisterbach, a Cistercian monk and author</note> records that certain heretics were caught there and condemned to the fire; among these was a Virgin of remarkable beauty who, though she was snatched from the flames, threw herself back into them of her own accord. Godfrey tells the same story in his *Annals* (5), and expressly calls those heretics *Cathars*, recording the event in the year 1163. Since Caesarius says that one of their dogmas was (6) *that any peasant at his table and from his own bread, which he eats, can make the Body of Christ* <note>original: "conficere potest Corpus Christi"</note>—referring to himself and his own body—does he not hint at the same Cathars whom Eckbert and Bernard described?

VI. I return to the dogmas of the **Albigensians**; which indeed, if compared with the **Waldensians** as they were in their beginning, must be said to have been entirely different: if the sect of the **Petrobrusians** or **Henricians** is compared with the sect of the Albigensians and the New Manichaeans, they must be judged entirely distinct. Nevertheless, the Waldensians later seem to have sunk into the stain of the Albigensians, and the Albigensians seem to have adorned their Manichaean beliefs with the dregs of the Petrobrusians and Henricians. This was certainly easy. For regarding Peter’s <note>Peter of Bruys</note> heresy, the *Abbot of Cluny* (7) <note>Peter the Venerable</note> says: *Expelled from your Septimania* <note>A region in Southern France</note> *by your persecution, it prepared dens for itself in the province of Novempopulana, which is commonly called Gascony, and in the parts adjacent to it*. As for Henry <note>Henry of Lausanne, founder of the Henricians</note>, when he had come into the region of Le Mans <note>original: "Cenomanensium"</note> and had there stirred up the greatest hatred of the people against the Clergy (as the *Acts of the Bishops of Le Mans* record, which Mabillon published (8)), having been driven from there by the Bishop, he went down into the lands of Toulouse; this is gathered from St. Bernard and his life written by Gaufridus (9). Nor indeed do I deny that those monstrous heresies brought forth in the previous century...

<meta>This page continues the catalog of 12th-century heretical figures, transitioning from the Cathars of Cologne to the radical social reformers Tanchelin and Arnold of Brescia, before beginning a specific study of the Albigensians of southern France.</meta>

...received; I speak of Tanchelin <note>Tanchelin of Antwerp was an itinerant preacher who famously rejected the institutional Church in the early 1100s</note>, whom Hugo, in the *Life of St. Norbert* (1), calls an *enemy of all the Sacraments*, and of whom it is read in the Letter of the Church of Utrecht to the Bishop of Cologne (2) that he *asserted the Pope, the Bishops, the Archbishops, the Priests and Clergy were nothing*. Furthermore, we gather from St. Bernard (3) that Arnold of Brescia <note>An Italian reformer who argued that the Church should abandon all property and political power</note>, having been forced to leave Italy after the Lateran Council in the year 1139, came to Gaul <note>original: "Galliam"; modern-day France</note>. Otto of Freising (4) records that his dogmas were not only these: *that neither Clerics with private property, nor Bishops with royal rights <term>Regalia: the rights and privileges held by a monarch</term>, nor Monks with possessions can be saved in any way; all these things belong to the Prince, and by his beneficence ought to be yielded to the use of the laity alone*. But he adds further: it is said that he *did not think soundly* regarding the Sacrament of the Altar <note>The Eucharist</note> and the baptism of infants. Bonaccursus (5) attributes this error to the Arnoldists and refutes it: *that because of the wickedness of the Clergy, they say the sacraments of the Church are to be avoided*.

---

### CHAPTER II.

->*Concerning the diverse types and names of the Albigensians, the origin of the Heresy, and its propagation.*<-

I. It is believable in itself that the Albigensians <note>A group of dualist heretics in Southern France, also known as Cathars</note>, like heretics of any other sect, did not all believe the same things; for those who have departed from the truth cannot remain consistent with themselves. As Tertullian wrote (6), *everyone modifies what they have received by their own judgment, just as he who handed them down composed them by his own judgment*. Nevertheless, Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay <note>A Cistercian monk who wrote a primary account of the Albigensian Crusade</note> seems to indicate this specifically. We have mentioned that he calls the Albigensians, about whom he writes, by the name of *Heretics*. Thus, where he has explained what might be called the common dogmas of the Albigensians, he adds: *There were other Heretics who said that there is one Creator, but he had two sons, Christ and the Devil. These also said that all creatures were good, but through the daughters mentioned in Genesis <note>A reference to the "sons of God" and "daughters of men" in Genesis 6</note>, all things were corrupted*. After he also explained other things which he attributed to all Albigensians, he again adds: *We do not believe we should keep silent that certain Heretics also said that no one could sin from the navel downwards. They said the images in the Churches were Idolatry, and they asserted their bells were the trumpets of demons. Likewise, they said that one did not sin more gravely by sleeping with his mother or sister than with* [any other]—

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the Albigensians, focusing on how different groups were grouped under a single name and addressing theological disputes regarding their views on Christ and the serpent in Eden.</meta>

than any other. As I have said, there were various kinds of Albigensians; the Author himself testifies to this in his Prologue to *Innocent III* (as recorded by Tissier), writing: *“In the first part of this work, I touch briefly upon the sects of the Heretics, and how the people of Provence <note>Residents of southern France, then called Provincia</note> were infected by the leprosy of infidelity from times past, etc.”* Therefore, let those who are about to read this know that in many places in this work, the Heretics and their defenders from Toulouse and other cities and castles are generally called *Albigensians*, simply because other nations were accustomed to calling the Heretics of Provence by that name.

And from this we derive a primary benefit: it must not be thought that because these people are addressed by the same name of a single sect, they followed exactly the same beliefs. If historians were to report that different people said different things, the credibility of ancient writers would immediately falter if we assumed they were a monolithic group. We touched upon above how diverse and clearly contradictory the views of the Manichæans <note>Followers of a dualistic religion that saw the world as a struggle between light and dark; medieval heretics were often accused of reviving these ancient views</note> were concerning Christ the Lord. But here Isaac de Beausobre (1) has again intervened, and he compels all Manichæans to follow the same path rather than allowing for diversity. 

For this reason, when St. Augustine asserts that the Manichæans understood Christ by the name of the serpent who deceived Eve, Beausobre thinks Augustine was either disgracefully hallucinating or openly lying. Why? Because Agapius, and Tyrbo in the writings of Epiphanius, make Christ not the serpent, but the tree of knowledge that was in Paradise. We once thought these things could be reconciled by a very slight change of a letter or two in the texts of Epiphanius and Photius, reading <term>the serpent <note>original Greek: τὸν ὄφιν (ton ophin)</note></term> in place of <term>the tree <note>original Greek: τὸ φύτον (to phyton)</note></term>. We had this reason for suggesting such a correction: we judged that the speech of Epiphanius—where he repeats the dogma drawn from Tyrbo—would be more coherent. We did not doubt that if Epiphanius needed correcting, so too did Photius, who perhaps had looked upon a corrupted passage of Epiphanius while explaining that Manichæan dogma and had thus explained his own Agapius in the same way.

However, this is the passage from Epiphanius (2): *“Furthermore, that tree by which good is discerned is Jesus himself, or the knowledge that resides in this world; he who receives it distinguishes good from evil.”* You see how that heretic strives to distort whatever is right, while the Apostle <note>St. Paul</note> clearly cries out and preaches: *“I fear lest, as the serpent deceived Eve by his craftiness, so your thoughts should be corrupted from the chastity and simplicity that is in Christ.”* What does a serpent have to do with a tree here? What does this passage from the Apostle have to do with refuting that point, unless we read “serpent” there instead of “tree”? But lacking the help of manuscript codices, I fear to lay hands upon the ancient writers to change them; and as I said, this whole controversy can be decided by acknowledging the diversity among the Manichæans. Moreover, the Heretics...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the different sects of the Albigensians, specifically identifying subgroups mentioned by historical chroniclers like Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay and Moneta of Cremona. ...heretics <note>original: "tici," completing "heretici" from the previous page</note> whom Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay first designated are the same as, or very similar to, those whom the Venerable Moneta describes as a second type of Cathar <note>The Cathars were a major dualist religious movement in the 12th and 13th centuries</note> at the beginning of Books I and II (1), as does Argentré (2); **"who posit one principle <note>original: "unum principium," a theological term for the source of all existence</note>, but one who had two [aspects], one evil and the other good."** Those described in the second place were the Runcarii, about whom Reinerius says (3): **"The Runcarii agree in large part with the Patarines, except they say that from the waist down no mortal sin is committed, because the Lord says that fornications proceed from the heart; hence [they allow] all abominations, even those against nature."** Reinerius and Pilichdorf (4) suggest that these too were counted among the Cathars. It must be established then that the Albigensians were altogether grouped within the number of the Cathars.

### II. The Various Names of the Albigensians

In the meantime, we shall speak of the various names of the Albigensians. They were first called ***Provincials***; obviously from the place where the heretics lived. And although from the year 1096—as is clear from the letters of Pope Urban, in which he calls Raymond the ***Count of Toulouse, Duke of Narbonne, and Marquis of Provence***—the region of Provence <note>original: "Provincia"</note> seems to be distinguished from the County of Toulouse and the Duchy of Narbonne, nevertheless Hadrianus Valesius (5) correctly noted: for this historian Peter [of Vaux-de-Cernay], the entire land subject to the Count of Toulouse—namely all of Septimania, part of Aquitaine, and the region of Provence up to the Durance and Isère rivers—was called by the one common name ***Province***, and the inhabitants were called ***Provincials***. Indeed, in the letters recorded by Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay (6), Pope Innocent III rebukes the **"wicked and perverse generation of Provincials,"** and later states; **"for such pestilential Provincials strive not just to plunder our things, but to destroy us."** However, he seems to be speaking only of the Province of Auch, or Novempopulana. But, regardless of that, the Prologue of Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay, which we have praised, makes the matter certain.

Secondly, there was another name by which they were called: ***Good Men*** <note>original: "Boni homines"</note>. The witness to this is the Council of Lombers, which everyone agrees was assembled against the Albigensians. For in the very first letters of that Council, these words are found (7): **"In the year of the Lord’s Incarnation 1165, such a definitive sentence was passed regarding the dispute, assertion, and attack on the Catholic faith, which certain men were striving to assault, who caused themselves to be called 'Good Men'; and then the Bishop of Lodève questioned those who caused themselves to be named 'Good Men' by order of the Bishop of Albi, etc. I, Gaucelin, Bishop of Lodève..."**

<meta>This page continues the catalog of various names attributed to the Albigensians, focusing on the titles "Good Men" and "Publicans" (Populicani), and citing historical chronicles to show how these terms were used across France and England.</meta>

by the command of the Bishop of Albi and his assessors, I judge those who call themselves **Good Men** to be Heretics. Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay (1) tells of the Count of Toulouse, the chief supporter of the Albigensians: *when one day he was asked why he was having himself carried away with such haste while laboring under a most grave illness, the wretch responded: "Because there are no **Good Men** in this land in whose hands I might die." For the heretics were called "Good Men" by their supporters.* He adds another saying by the same Count: *“I know I will be disinherited on account of these **Good Men**.”* 

Argentré (2) contends that they were called ***Populicani*** <note>Also spelled Publicani or Publicans; a common medieval label for heretics, likely a corruption of "Paulicians"</note>, and were called Albigensians only after war was waged against them. I do not strongly disagree with him, since it is established that those Heretics who were becoming most powerful in Gaul and especially in Gascony were called by this name. In a manuscript codex cited by the same Argentré, Ralph of Coggeshall, a monk who wrote in the age of Philip Augustus, states: *“In the times of King Louis of France, who fathered King Philip, when the error of certain Heretics, who are commonly called **Publicans**, flourished through several provinces of Gaul, etc.”* William of Newburgh (3) writes: *“In those same days, certain wanderers came into England from that stock, it is believed, whom the common people call **Publicans**. These surely had their origin long ago in Gascony, from an uncertain author, etc.”* 

The Monk of Auxerre (4) says: *“Of those whom they call **Publicans**, or **Cathars**, or **Pantherine Populicani**: an accursed heresy which denies the sacraments of Christ had, during that time, crept in secretly in many places, but had openly occupied the people especially in Gascony.”* But if we weigh the matter a bit more carefully, it seems that all those who were then infected with the stain of the **Manichaeans** <note>Followers of Manichaeism, an ancient dualistic religion; the term was used in the Middle Ages as a generic label for heretics who believed in two opposing principles of Good and Evil</note> were called by this name. Thus Villehardouin (5) refers to those living in Philippopolis; and in a letter of Louis VII found in Freher (6) and Duchesne (7), we read: *“The Archbishop of Rheims, your brother Henry, having recently set out for the land of Flanders, found there depraved men, followers of a most wicked error, fallen into the heresy of the **Manichaeans**, who are commonly called **Publicans**.”* 

Indeed, the decree of the Third Lateran Council (8) states: *“Because in Gascony, the Albigensian territory, and the regions of Toulouse and other places, the condemned perversity of the Heretics—whom some call **Cathars**, others **Patarines**, others **Publicans**, and others by other names—has grown so strong, etc.”* Du Cange (9) therefore thinks that the ***Publicans***, or ***Populicans***, or even ***Poplicans***, were so called because they were ***Paulicians*** <note>A Byzantine dualist sect</note>, whom we mentioned... <meta>catchword: Mani- (likely referring to Manichaeans on the next page)</meta>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the various names applied to the Albigensians, specifically examining the term "Albanenses" and its relationship to other regional groups like the Bagnolenses and the Cathars of Lombardy.</meta>

...were Manichaeans. Another question is regarding the **Albanensians**, whether the name means the same as **Albigensians**. For St. Antoninus (1) recorded what many before him had; *“There were three sects of Heretics, namely the Albanensians, the Bagnolensians, and the Concordentians, who agree in many of their worst errors, though they disagree in a few.”* These people say that *“there are two principles”* <note>original: "sunt duo principia"; this refers to Dualism, the belief that there are two opposing gods—one good and one evil—ruling the universe</note> etc. He adds: *“Note that these Albanensians are the Albigensians: it was against these Albigensians that Innocent III sent [a crusade] etc.”* Guido of Perpignan seems to indicate the same thing; after reviewing the errors of the Cathars, he tells of the errors of those from Concorezio (2), and others from Barulo, then of the heresy of the **Albanensians**, to which he joins the heresy of the **Bagnolensians**; for it is clear from reading their doctrines that they were the same as those whom Antoninus called the *Bagnolensians* and *Concordensians*. And the names of these three sects, which agreed in most things, are often expressed in such a way that they are called not *Albanensians*, but *Albigensians*. 

There exists an excellent record by Peregrinus Priscianus regarding the errors of Armannus Pungilupus, which Ludovico Antonio Muratori published (3), and from him the illustrious Ricchinius, where these things are held: *“And since the above-written witnesses affirm that Armannus was a leader in the Bagnolensian Heresy, we have therefore deemed it necessary to write the chapters and errors of that Heresy, though very briefly... not passing over the fact that at nearly the same times three sects flourished: namely the Albigensians, those of Bagnolo, and those of Concorezio.”* Then, he designates with the initials A.B.C. the points in which they agree (for they agree in almost everything). 

Reinerus <note>Reinerus Sacconi, a former Cathar who became a Dominican friar and inquisitor; he is one of our primary sources for their internal organization</note> settles this matter entirely, both in the larger summary which Gretser published, and in the shorter one first brought to light by Martène (4) and d'Argentré (5). For in listing the sects of the Heretics or the names of the sects, he writes (6): *“The sect of the Cathars is divided into two parts, or principal sects; of which the first are called Albanensians, the second Concorezenses, the third Bagnolensians; and all these are in Lombardy. But the other Cathars, whether they are in Tuscany, or in the Marches, or in Provence, do not differ in their opinions from these Cathars, or from some of them.”* A little later, in numbering the Churches of the Cathars, he has these words (7): *“There are sixteen in all, the Churches of the Cathars... whose names are these: the Albanensian Church or the Church of Desenzano (in the shorter summary, 'or of Donzeno'), the Church of Concorezio, and the Church of the Bagnolensians, or of Bagnolo.”*

(In the short summary: *of the Bagnolensians* or *from Bagnolo*) the *Church of Vicenza* or *of the March*. The *Church of Florence*; the *Church of the Spoleto Valley*; the *Church of France*; the *Church of Toulouse*; the *Church of Carcassonne* (in the short version, *Carcassonne* and *Albi*), the *Church of Albi*; the *Church of Slavonia*; the *Church of the Latins in Constantinople*; the *Church of the Greeks* in the same place; the *Church of Philadelphia* in Romagna; the *Church of Bulgaria*; and the *Church of Dugunthia*. And all these had their origins from the last two mentioned. The first, namely the **Albanensians**, dwell in **Verona** and in several cities of **Lombardy**... Those from **Concorezzo**, however, are found throughout almost all of **Lombardy**... The **Bagnolensians** dwell in **Mantua**, **Brescia**, **Bergamo**, and in the **territory of Milan**, though in small numbers, and also in **Romagna**... 

The **Church of the March** has no presence in **Verona**, but they number about 150. The **Church of Toulouse**, the **Church of Albi**, and the **Church of Carcassonne**, along with certain others that once existed—such as the **Church of Auch** (in the short version, **Agen**) which is now almost destroyed—number about 300. The **Church of the Latins in Constantinople** numbers about 50. Likewise, the **Church of Slavonia**, the **Greek Church of Philadelphia**, **Bulgaria**, and **Dugunthia** consist of people from every nation joined together. 

He adds more concerning the specific opinions of the **Cathars**. The **Albanensians** are divided into two factions with opposing and differing opinions. The head of one faction is **Gelesimantha** (in the short version, **Belasinanza**), their Bishop from **Verona**, and most of the older members and a few young people of his sect follow him. The head of the other sect is **John of Lyons** from **Bergamo**, their "elder son" <note>original: "filius major"; in Cathar hierarchy, this was a high-ranking official designated to succeed the Bishop</note> and ordained Bishop: and on the contrary, the younger members and a few older ones follow him. This faction is significantly larger than the first. The first faction holds the older opinions, which all the older **Cathars** held during the current years of our Lord 1230. He reviews their particular opinions one by one, and finally (1);

Lastly, note that the **Cathars of the Church of Toulouse** and the **Carcassonians** (or in the shorter summary, the **Churches of Toulouse, Albi, and Carcassonne**) hold the errors of **Gelesimantha** and the **Albanensians**, as do all the **Cathar Churches from overseas**, which I listed last. However, no **Church of the Cathars** agrees in all points with the **Cathars of the Church of Concorezzo**. The **Church of France** agrees with the **Bagnolensians**; whereas those of the **Trevisan March**, **Tuscany**, and the **Spoleto Valley** agree with the **Bagnolensians** in more things than with the **Albanensians**, though they are gradually being drawn toward the **Albanensians**. 

We have described these things more extensively from **Reinerus** <note>original: "Reinero"; referring to Rainier Sacconi, a 13th-century Inquisitor and former Cathar member whose treatise is a major historical source</note>, because much can be gathered from his work for our purpose, and several errors of other authors can even be corrected through him. And this **Reinerus** is a man to whom, either because of his learning or his piety, the most learned men give credit...

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>Continuing the discussion of heretical sects from the previous page, specifically focusing on the leadership titles and the geographical origins of the Bagnolensians and Albanensians, and establishing their theological link to the ancient Manichaeans.</meta>

...they command (1). Certainly, the one who is their High Priest, or Bishop—as Luther (2) and Sixtus of Siena (3) call him—must be most skilled in these matters. He calls himself a *Heresiarch* (4) <note>original: "Hæresiarcha," meaning a leader or founder of a heresy</note>, which does not always signify the author of a heresy, but rather a chief supporter, or one who holds the first place among heretics; for this reason, Peter (5) calls the mother of the Count of Foix <note>referring to the noble house of Foix in Southern France, known for Cathar sympathies</note> a great *Heresiarch*. In this sense and with this meaning, we encounter "Heresiarch Baldwin" (6) in the same author, as well as in the History: *the neighboring cities and towns, with Heresiarchs rooted within them, were infected wonderfully and miserably by that plague.* I have noted this in passing because the authors of those Glossaries overlooked it. 

Therefore, based on Reinerus <note>Reinerius Saccho, a 13th-century inquisitor and former Cathar member whose writings are a primary source</note>, Du Cange (7) must first be corrected, as he argues that the *Bagnolensian* heretics were named from the town of *Bagnols* in the province of the same name <note>Bagnols-sur-Cèze in France</note>. Reinerus clearly indicates that they were all Italians, or that those sects were located in Italy. Thus, I prefer to derive the *Bagnolensians* from Bagnolo near the Larius river <note>Lake Como, historically called Lacus Larius</note>, as Leandro (8) holds; just as the *Albanensians* are named from Alba Pompeia <note>modern-day Alba in Piedmont, Italy</note>. 

The *Concordensians* or, as they are sometimes called, *Concorrezes*—and to Guido, who transforms all these names into *Correzidi*—are from the town of Concordia in Lombardy; the same Du Cange agrees. Next, it is clear from the same Reinerus that the Albanensians are different and distinct from the Albigensians, if you look at the men so named or the cities from which they took their names; but if you look at the dogmas and errors with which they were infected, they were almost the same. And for that reason, I believe, those writers name these groups and explain their doctrine without thinking specifically of the Albigensians, whom they knew professed the same things.

III. But the most important thing you will gather from Reinerus is that the Cathars were Albigensians, and were imbued with the dogmas of the Cathars, that is, of the Manichaeans <note>an ancient dualist religion that taught a struggle between a good spiritual world and an evil material world</note>. Saint Augustine (9) is a witness that among the ancient Manichaeans certain ones were called *Catharists*, *that is, cleansers or purifiers* <note>original: "purgatores"</note>, who in a certain execrable manner purified the divine substance which they took in the Eucharist; he also shows in the same place that the Manichaeans thought the substance of God, which they believed was mixed into food, was purified by the way of life led by their "Elect." And the famous Bossuet (10) quite rightly judged that there was the same reason for this name among the "new" Manichaeans <note>the medieval Cathars</note>. The Synod seems to explain the cause of this name in a slightly different way—

...of Arras (1); while the Council of Reims in the year 1157 (2) seems to repeat this from the fact that they condemned marriage. For it states under the Title *Concerning the Piphles* <note>original: "Piphilis," a derogatory term for heretics in Flanders, possibly a corruption of "Publicans"</note>: *Since the most impure sect of the Manichaeans <note>a dualist religious movement that the Church often conflated with medieval heresies</note>, through slippery evasion under the guise of religion, hiding itself among the most unlearned, strives to destroy the souls of the simple, and through the lowliest weavers—who often flee from place to place and change their names—lead captive little women burdened with sins; ... we decree that, since among the many wicked and sacrilegious things with which they attempt not only to stain but to infect the Church, they impudently condemn marriage (which was instituted by the Lord and is holy), so that they may appear as though they are purer and cleaner, etc.* 

It is clear from Eckbert (3) <note>Eckbert of Schönau, a 12th-century Benedictine monk known for his sermons against the Cathars</note> that these people are the Cathars. Our Germany calls them *Cathars* <note>meaning "The Pure Ones"</note>, Flanders calls them *Piphles*, and Gaul <note>France</note> calls them *Texerant* <note>original: "Texerant," from the word for weaving</note> because of their practice of weaving. And from the same source, we know that in the Cathars whom he refutes, there were almost all the same doctrines that Caesar Heisterbach and Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay related concerning the Albigensians; such as that idea regarding the Body of the Lord: *even if it contained the size of the Alps within itself, it would have long ago been consumed by those eating it.* For Eckbert relates (4): *a certain man of our time, who was infamous because he had "tasted of your Catharism," when he was asked on his deathbed whether he wished the Body of the Lord to be given to him, is remembered to have said: "If that Body of the Lord were of such great size as the Rock of Ehrenbreitstein <note>original: "petra Erenberti," a massive stone fortress/rock on the Rhine</note>, it would have been consumed long ago."*

### IV.

And these things concerning the Cathars which we have pursued pertain to finding the origin of the Albigensians. If you desire the name *Albigensians*, this was heard later; it does not appear in the Council of Lombers <note>original: "Lumbariensi"</note> celebrated in 1176, as is commonly believed, as we shall discuss elsewhere. I am speaking of the heretics; for you will find "Albigensian knights" and the "Albigensian city" <note>Albi, in southern France</note> much before this age, even if not among the most ancient geographers or historians (5). 

However, if you seek the heretical men themselves and their doctrines, they are older than their name in Gaul and the neighboring regions. And we have also noticed that those heretics, of whom we just spoke from Caesar and Godfrey, were from the sect of the Cathars—that is, those who, coming to these regions later, were called Albigensians. Heretics of this kind were also those whom we related Saint Bernard to have refuted; and from the letters of Abbot Evervinus (6), it is established that they had settled in Cologne. You may add Eckbert, whom we were just mentioning. Now truly, Godfrey [relates] that the Cathars came to Cologne from the parts of Flanders...

...they command (1). It is certainly necessary that their Leader, or Bishop, be most skilled in those matters, as Ecchard (2) and Sixtus of Siena (3) call him. He calls himself a **Heresiarch** <term>Heresiarch</term> <note>original: "Heresiarcha," from the Greek for a leader or founder of a heretical movement</note> (4), a term which does not always signify the author of a heresy, but rather a chief supporter, or one who holds the principal rank among the heretics; for this reason, Peter (5) calls the mother of the Count of Foix a "most great Heresiarch."

In this sense and with this meaning, the term **Heresiarch Baldwin** occurs in the same author (6), and in the History: *the neighboring cities and towns, with Heresiarchs rooted within them, were wonderfully and miserably infected by that plague.* I have noted this in passing, because it escaped the authors of those Glossaries. Therefore, Du Cange <note>Charles du Fresne, Sieur du Cange, a famous 17th-century scholar of medieval Latin</note> must first be corrected by Reinerius <note>Reinerius Saccho, a 13th-century inquisitor and former member of the Cathars who wrote a detailed account of their sects</note>, since Du Cange wishes the **Bagnolensians** <note>original: "Bagnolenses," a sect of the Cathars</note> to be named after the town of Bagnols in Provence. However, Reinerius clearly indicates that they were all Italians, or at least that those sects existed in Italy. Thus, I prefer to derive **Bagnolensians** from Bagnolo near the Livenza river, as Leander (8) has it; just as the **Albanensians** are named from Alba Pompeia <note>Alba in the Piedmont region of Italy</note>. The **Concordensians**—or as they are sometimes called, the *Concorezenses*—and Guido (who transforms all these names into *Cor...*), are said to be named from Concordia, a town in Lombardy, in which Du Cange agrees. Next, it is clear from the same Reinerius that the **Albanensians** are different and distinct from the **Albigensians**, if you look at the men so named or the cities from which they took their names; but if you look at the dogmas and errors with which they were infected, they were almost the same. And so, I believe, those writers name the former and explain their doctrines, without thinking of the Albigensians, whom they knew to profess the same things.

III. But the principal thing you will gather from Reinerius is that the Cathars and Albigensians were imbued with the dogmas of the Cathars, that is, the Manichaeans <note>followers of a dualist religion that taught a struggle between a good spiritual world of light and an evil material world of darkness</note>. St. Augustine (9) is a witness that among the ancient Manichaeans, some called themselves **Catharists**, *that is, purifiers or cleansers*, who in a certain execrable way "purged" the divine substance which they took in the Eucharist. Augustine also notes in the same place that the Manichaeans believed the substance of God, which they thought was mixed into food, was purged by the kind of life led by their "Elect." And the distinguished Bossuet (10) <note>Jacques-Bénigne Bossuet, a 17th-century French bishop and theologian</note> rightly judged that there was the same reason for this name among the new Manichaeans. Sy... seems to explain the cause of this name slightly differently.

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the origins of the "Piphles" or "Cathars" mentioned on the previous page. ...fridus testifies. And that there were formerly Heretics of this kind in Flanders, or in neighboring Belgium, the **Synod of Arras** <note>original: "Synodus Atrebatensis"; a church council held in 1025 to deal with local heresy</note> (1) is a witness, in which these things are held: *In the year of the Lord’s Incarnation 1025, in the 8th Indiction, while Lord Gerard was ruling the Church of the city of Cambrai and Arras, it happened by a certain custom of annual observation that... the same Prelate had to stay for several days at his seat in Arras. There... it was reported to him that certain men had arrived at that place from the borders of Italy who, introducing a certain sect of a new heresy, were attempting to pervert the discipline of the Evangelical and Apostolic decree, and putting forward a certain justice, they asserted that men were purified by this alone.*

Behold the name of the **Cathars** <term>Cathars</term> <note>From the Greek "katharos" meaning "pure"; the text suggests that while the name isn't used in the 1025 record, their "purification" claims identify them as such</note>; indeed, the dogmas of the Cathars follow. For it is added: *and to reject all Sacraments, to curse legitimate marriages, etc.* Concerning the author of the error, they report: *they say they are listeners of a certain Gandulfus, a man from the regions of Italy.* Therefore, this plague can be seen to have flowed into the Gauls from Flanders, and into Flanders from Italy.

And indeed many accuse Italy of being, as it were, a **cesspool of heresy** <note>original: "hæresis sentinam"</note>, from which they were chiefly scattered into Gaul; among these, **Mabillon** <note>Jean Mabillon (1632–1707), a Benedictine monk and founder of the study of old documents</note> even derives Henry, a follower of **Peter of Bruys** <note>An itinerant 12th-century French reformer and heretic who rejected infant baptism and the veneration of the cross</note>, from Italy. For when, following Henry's travels, he found him at Lausanne, having nothing else from which he could gather the rest of his path, he adds (2): *And perhaps he had come to Lausanne from Italy, from which dregs of this kind migrated into the Gallic regions, the remains of the **Manichaeans** <term>Manichaeans</term> <note>A term used by medieval writers to describe any dualist heretic who believed the physical world was evil</note>.*

But what argument for his suspicion does he bring forth and hold? Namely, that those Heretics of Cambrai who were condemned in the year 1025 in the Synod of Arras were brought from Italy. **Father Daniel** <note>Gabriel Daniel (1649–1728), a Jesuit historian</note> (3), a most elegant writer of French History, determines that the **Albigensian Heresy** <term>Albigensian</term> <note>Another name for the Cathar movement, specifically in southern France near the city of Albi</note> itself burst into Gaul from Italy, on the grounds, of course, that **Rodulphus Glaber** <note>A chronicler and monk of Cluny known for his history of the years around 1000 AD</note> wrote (4): *In the twenty-third year below the aforementioned thousandth [the year 1023], a too raw and insolent Heresy was discovered at the aforementioned city of Orléans... For it is said that this most insane heresy had its beginning in the Gauls from a certain woman coming from Italy.*

In fact, from among the Italian writers themselves, **Ludovico Antonio Muratori** <note>A famous Italian historian and scholar of the 18th century</note>, having related this testimony of Glaber (5), seems to concede the same; which is further held from the history of **Landulfus the Elder** (6), that already in the year 1040, Manichaean heretics were discovered in the Castle of **Monforte** in the diocese of Turin by Heribert, Archbishop of Milan. But it is quite another thing for Heretics to have come into some region and even to have infected [it with] He...

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the geographical origins of dualist heresies in Europe. The previous page established a link between Italian groups and the spread of these ideas into Flanders. Here, the author examines the timeline of these movements in Gaul (France), specifically tracing the label "Bulgarian" and the early presence of Manichaean-style beliefs in Aquitaine and Orléans during the 11th century.</meta>

some; and another thing, that according to them, the **heresy itself must be traced back** to them as if from a first source <note>original: "ex fonte Hærefim"</note>; so that others from those regions did not draw it from elsewhere. We have seen that it could have crept into the regions of Gaul from Flanders or from Belgium, or from the city of Cologne, and that Caesarius <note>Caesarius of Heisterbach, a 13th-century Cistercian monk and author</note> signifies this: indeed, if we examine older records, we will find that the Manichaean heresy <term>Manichaeism</term> <note>A dualistic belief system originating in Persia that taught a struggle between a good spirit world and an evil material world</note> had settled in Gaul before it was brought from the Italian woman to Orléans. For that event is said to have happened in the year 1017. But Adémar de Chabannes, in his Chronicle, where he explained the deeds of the year 1010, relates (1) *A little time later, Manichaeans were found throughout Aquitaine seducing the common people, denying baptism and the power of the Holy Cross, and whatever belongs to sound doctrine, abstaining from foods, etc.* Then, after many intervening passages, he relates that same account of Glaber <note>Rodulfus Glaber, a contemporary chronicler</note> concerning the Heresy of the people of Orléans and the punishment of Lisoie (2), yet he is silent regarding the Italian woman. Therefore, there were Manichaeans in Gaul before that time. 

But let us consider the matter itself more attentively. This heresy of the Manichaeans, or Cathars, which grew strong in Gaul at these times, is called the *Heresy of the Bulgarians*. The Monk of Auxerre <note>Robert of Auxerre</note> says in his Chronology (3) concerning Philip Augustus and the very Heresy of the Albigensians: *He strove vehemently to hunt down the heretics whom they call Bulgarians... At that same time, the execrable Heresy of the Bulgarians, the extreme dregs of all errors, was creeping into many places, the more harmful for being more hidden. But it had grown strongest in the land of the Count of Toulouse and the neighboring Princes... Wherefore, by the counsel of the Lord Pope, the Abbot of Cîteaux and about thirteen other Abbots were delegated, etc.* and at the year 1201: *Euraudus the knight, whom Count Henry of Nevers had placed in charge of his land, was accused of that heresy which they call the Bulgarian before the Legate.* 

Matthew Paris <note>A famous English chronicler and monk of St. Albans</note> says the same in the same words in his Chronicle at the year 1236: *About those days, the heretical depravity of those who are commonly called Paterenes and Bulgarians grew strong in transalpine parts... so much so that they dared to violate the purity of the faith in the borders of France and Flanders by causing disturbance. But by the diligent ministry and tireless preaching of the Minors <note>Franciscans</note> and the Preachers <note>Dominicans</note>, etc.* Here, some editions (4) represent this as *Burgares*: but it is plain that Bulgarians should be understood; so at the year 1238 (5) concerning Friar Robert of the Order of Preachers: *In the kingdom of the Franks he found many stained with heretical depravity, especially however in Flanders... he called them by the common name of "Bugars" <note>The root of the English slur "bugger," originally used to denigrate heretics associated with the Bulgarian Bogomil movement</note>, whether they were Paterenes, or Jovinians, or Albigensians... For the mentioned Robert himself, before he had taken the religious habit, had been a Bugar.* The same in the same words—

<summary>This page discusses the early 11th-century arrival of Manichaean heresies in France, specifically Aquitaine and Orléans, and explores the origins of the term "Bulgarian" (or "Bugar") as a label for these groups. --- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the origins of the Albigensian and Cathar movements, specifically linking their appearance in Western Europe to the "Bulgarian" route used by Crusaders and travelers. It builds upon the previous page’s discussion of early Manichaean traces in 11th-century France.</meta>

William of Nangis <note>A 13th-century monk and chronicler at the Abbey of Saint-Denis</note> in his *Chronicle* for the year 1207 (1). A manuscript of the *History of the Franks* found in the works of Du Cange (2) <note>Charles du Fresne, sieur du Cange, a renowned 17th-century philologist</note> for the year 1223 regarding Philip Augustus states: *He sent his son into the Albigensian territory to destroy the Heresy of the Bulgars of the land* <note>original Old French: "Envoja son fils en Albigeois pour destruire l'Heresie des Bougres du pays"</note>. Pierre de Marca testifies in his *History of Béarn* (3) that other French monuments also prefer this name; he also explains the term, namely the heresy with which the Bulgarians were infected.

For Peter of Sicily, in declaring the plan of the <term>Paulicians</term> <note>A dualist Christian sect, often seen by medieval writers as ancestors to the Cathars</note>—that is, the <term>Manichaeans</term> <note>Followers of a belief system that pits a good spiritual world against an evil material one</note> who were in Armenia—says (4), *I learned from those same impious and delusional men that they were going to send some from their assembly into Bulgaria to turn whomever they could from the Catholic religion toward their own execrated and nefarious sect.* 

That they carried out their plan can be gathered from Reinerus <note>Reinerius Sacconi, a 13th-century inquisitor who was once a member of the sect</note>, who numbered the churches of Bulgaria and "Dugranicia" among the churches of the <term>Cathars</term> <note>From the Greek 'katharos' meaning 'pure'; a major dualist movement in medieval Europe</note>. Where on earth "Dugranicia" might be, many ask, though it is not truly vital to know; yet if one may speculate, I would interpret it as *Dragovintia*, which Ortelius <note>Abraham Ortelius, a famous 16th-century cartographer</note> read in the works of Codinus and believes to be a Prefecture of Thrace, which would certainly be very close to Bulgaria. 

But the words of Reinerus must be most closely attended to, in which he stated that all the churches of the Cathars had their origins from the last two mentioned—that is, from Bulgaria and Dugranicia. Therefore, the Albigensian church, which he mentioned above, shares this origin. Thus everything fits together well: both the name of "Bulgarians" attributed to the Albigensians (or the Albigensian Heresy) and the authority of Reinerus. 

I add also the reasoning of the matter itself. For as Pierre de Marca noted excellently (5): when the Franks gained possession of Jerusalem in the 11th century and founded the Eastern Empire in the 13th, there was a great deal of commerce between the Franks and the Bulgarians, since the region of the latter lay on the land route toward Constantinople. Nothing was easier, then, than to absorb the errors with which they were infected. 

Indeed, before those times—that is, the 11th century—there was no trace of the Manichaean heresy in Gaul, or in Italy, or in the whole West. It is clear, therefore, if one must proceed by conjectures in an uncertain matter, that the Heresy of the Manichaeans or Cathars did not come into Gaul from Italy, but rather set out from Gaul into Italy; and thus the origin of the Albigensians must be traced back to the errors of those Franks who mingled with the Bulgarians. 

But perhaps there will be one who asks about the origin of the name "Albigensians" itself, and from which city they take their name. This—

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the naming and geographical spread of the Albigensian movement, moving from their Bulgarian roots to their specific associations with cities in Southern France like Albi, Toulouse, and Agen.</meta>

To the erudition and diligence of the Most Reverend Father Antoninus Bremond <note>Antonine Bremond (1692–1755), a historical scholar and Master General of the Dominican Order</note>, Master General of the Order of Preachers, we owe the distinction between Alba Augusta—from which many incorrectly derive this name—and Albiga, or Albia, which is commonly called *Albi*. We derive the name from this second city, and not from the first as some have done. But this whole matter is treated so extensively and accurately in the *Annals of the Order of Preachers*, which have recently been published in a most elegant edition (1), that nothing can be added. In that work, the question is also asked why the Albigensians found their name specifically from this city. We agree with the opinion praised therein: that because there was once a great multitude of them in the Albigensian district, serious thought was given to removing them. 

Yet they were not without other titles designated by the names of other regions or towns. The Maurists <note>A congregation of French Benedictine monks known for their high standards of scholarship</note>, who wrote the history of Occitania (2), show that they were called *Tolosates* or *Tolosanos* <note>People of Toulouse</note>. It is also clear they were called *Agenenses* <note>People of Agen</note> from Robert of Torigni <note>A 12th-century chronicler and abbot of Mont Saint-Michel</note>, who writes for the year 1178: *Heretics, whom they call Agenenses, and many others, gathered around Toulouse, holding evil views concerning the Sacrament of the Altar, marriage, and the other Sacraments; for their refutation, Peter, the Roman Legate, and many other religious persons gathered with the aforesaid Kings, but they made little progress.* <note>original Latin: "Hæretici, quos Agenenſes vocant, & alii multi convenerunt circa Toloſam male ſentientes de Sacramento Altaris..."</note> So it appears in the more reliable editions (3). They had undoubtedly infected the city of Agen, as Radulphus Ardens <note>Raoul Ardent, a famous 12th-century preacher</note> says (4): *Such today, my brothers, are the <term>Manichaean</term> heretics, because they have stained the homeland of Agen with their heresy.* Therefore, some have unskillfully preferred to "correct" the text to read *Albigenses*.

### V.

And here indeed the subject demands that we finally speak of the propagation of this heresy. For this will be useful for distinguishing the other names of these heretics. And indeed, Caesarius <note>Caesarius of Heisterbach, a 13th-century Cistercian monk</note> says *that the error of the people of Albi grew so strong* (5) *that in a short interval of time it infected up to a thousand cities; and he adds: and if it had not been repressed by the swords of the faithful, I think it would have corrupted all of Europe.* Some of these cities were so populous that in the one called *Biders* (Béziers) *it was said there were more than one hundred thousand people.* In the Lateran Council, as we advised above, those heretics who seem to some to have been Albigensians are said to have flourished in *Gascony, the Albigensian region, and the parts of Toulouse and other places.* Bernard Gui <note>A famous Dominican Inquisitor and historian</note> in the *Life of Innocent III* (6), recounting that Cistercians were chosen against the Albigensians, says they were *sent into the land of the Albigensians, and into the Toulousain, and into the Carcassonnais*, and again later the *Albigensian and Toulouse—*

...names them the Toulousans and Carcassonians. He also states that Amalric <note>Amalric VI of Montfort (1195–1245), who struggled to maintain the lands his father won during the Albigensian Crusade</note>, son of Simon, Count of Montfort—to whom the regions infected with heresy and conquered by arms had fallen—resigned to the King of France the County of *Toulouse* and all the other land which his father and he himself had acquired and which had been given to him in the *Agenais*, *Albigois*, *Quercy*, and *Carcassonne*. The Bishops of Toulouse and Couserans <note>original: "Conforanensis", a diocese in the Pyrenees</note> came to Rome, as Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay <note>original: "Petrus Vallicernensis", a monk and primary chronicler of the Crusade</note> records (1): *To petition the Lord Pope that he might stretch out a helping hand to the peace of the Church, which was endangered in the Provinces of Narbonne, Bourges, and Bordeaux, as if it were almost shipwrecked.* William of Puylaurens <note>original: "Guillielmus de Podio Laurentii", a chaplain to the Counts of Toulouse and historian of the era</note> in the prologue of his history [writes]: *Since among these things which are reported to have been done for about 100 years, between these seas of ours in Europe, that which was begun for the defense of the Catholic faith and the extirpation of heretical depravity in the Province of Narbonne and the dioceses of Albi, Rodez, Quercy, and Agen, and in certain lands beyond the Rhône belonging to the Lord Count of Toulouse, is to be held as very worthy of memory.*

Nevertheless, Matthew Paris <note>A famous English Benedictine chronicler</note> testifies for the year 1214 that this heresy crossed even these borders (2): *Around those days, the depravity of the heretics who are called Albigensians grew so strong in Gascony, Arumpnia, and the Albigois, in the regions of Toulouse and the Kingdom of Aragon, etc.* It is not easy to guess which region *Arumpnia* is; it seems indeed to have been not far from Albi or Toulouse. I suspect he named Auvergne <note>original: "Arverniam"</note> in this way. Aimoin <note>Aimoin of Fleury (c. 960–c. 1010), a monk and historian</note> gave me the cause for this suspicion; for in his description of Aquitaine, I read: *According to which tradition, the excellent cities of Narbonne, Arunnias—which is now called Clermont <note>original: "Clarus mons", modern Clermont-Ferrand</note>—Cahors, Toulouse, etc., are contained in Aquitaine.* So it is in the Parisian edition edited by Jacques du Breul (3), although in certain others (4) *Arvernus* is found. If, therefore, the city of Arunnias was Arvernus [Clermont]; why should not the region of Auvergne be called *Arunnia*, or by that customary way of pronouncing it, *Arumpnia*? Furthermore, it is held from this that this "plague" also penetrated into Aragon, which I think happened when the King of Aragon came to the aid of Raymond, Count of Toulouse—that is, to the aid of the Albigensians.

Truly, concerning Spain being attacked by the Albigensian heresy, the most illustrious witness is Lucas of Tuy <note>original: "Lucas Tudensis", a Spanish bishop known for his anti-heretical writings</note>, in three books written against the Albigensians, where (5) he also relates what they attempted in León <note>original: "Legione"</note> through the work of false miracles. Many narrate that it spread into England, especially English writers, such as Henry Knighton <note>original: "Henricus de Knyghton", a 14th-century Augustinian chronicler</note> for the 5th year of Pope Innocent III (6): *Albigensian heretics came into England, some of whom were burned alive.* But

<meta>This page continues the geographic survey of the Albigensian heresy, specifically clarifying the ecclesiastical boundaries of the regions mentioned in the previous page’s accounts by Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay and William of Puylaurens.</meta>

A vast multitude of them settled in Gaul; and in the writings of Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay, as we have seen, the provinces named are Narbonne, Bourges <note>original: "Bituricensis"</note>, and Bordeaux; in William of Puylaurens, the Province of Narbonne is mentioned; but the dioceses are Albi, Rodez <note>original: "Ruthenensis"</note>, Quercy <note>original: "Cadurcensis"</note>, and Agen; in the letters of Innocent III, it was called the Province of Auch <note>original: "Auxitana"</note>. It is unnecessary to point out that among these authors, "Provinces" refers to ecclesiastical <term>ecclesiastical province: an administrative district of the Church, usually governed by an archbishop</term> rather than civil divisions; likewise, those dioceses were by no means in the province of Narbonne. 

Before Albi became a metropolitan see <term>metropolitan see: the primary city or "mother church" of an ecclesiastical province</term>—which occurred under Pope Innocent XI—it was in the province of Bourges, as is also read in the *Notitia* <note>The *Notitia Galliarum*, an ancient register of the cities and provinces of Gaul</note> which Holstenius published from the Royal codex; and in that same province, as stated there, were Rodez and Quercy, which are now subject to the Bishop of Albi; while Agen was in the province of Bordeaux. The Province of Auch, which is now commonly called *Ausciensis*, included the bishoprics of Dax <note>original: "Aquensem"</note>, Comminges <note>original: "Convenarum"</note>, and Couserans, which are often named in the history of the Albigensians, and it still includes them today. 

The metropolitan centers today are still Narbonne, Bourges, and Bordeaux; and subject to Narbonne are the bishoprics of Béziers <note>original: "Biterrensis"</note>, Carcassonne, Montpellier, Lodève, and Uzès <note>original: "Uceticensis"</note>, which are equally famous in this history; although Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay more often calls the city and Bishop of Uzès "Uticensis," a name also used in the aforementioned *Notitia*. 

Regarding Narbonne itself, it is clear from our history that the Archbishop held rights over it. Indeed, they say he was the Duke of Narbonne at the end of the 11th century, until Raymond of Saint-Gilles, the Count of Toulouse, attempted to take the title and rights away from him; however, it is certain from the letters of Innocent III and the history of Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay that the Archbishops remained in possession of it until our own times (1). Thus, the Viscounts of Narbonne appear quite often in the records. *Aymeric, by the grace of God, Viscount of Narbonne* signs a charter of Saint Louis <note>King Louis IX of France</note> (2). Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay names him *Aymeric, Lord of Narbonne* (3). 

Similarly, in the same author (4) [we find] *Raymond Roger by name, Viscount of Béziers*; and elsewhere (5) *Raymond Roger, Viscount of Béziers, who was being held in the palace at Carcassonne, died while suffering from illness*. Bernard Gui <note>a famous inquisitor and historian</note>, or the author of the *Life of Innocent* among Muratori's writers (6), calls him *Roger, or Raymond Roger, Viscount of Béziers and Carcassonne, nephew of the Count of Toulouse*. The origin of the first Viscounts of Béziers is not entirely clear. Peter Raymond, Viscount of Béziers, appears in the year 1040, as does the Viscount of Carcassonne. That Carcassonne belonged later...

A very great multitude of them <note>The Albigensian heretics</note> lay upon Gaul; and according to Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay, as we have seen, the provinces named are Narbonne, Bourges, and Bordeaux; according to William of Puylaurens, the Province of Narbonne; and the Dioceses of Albi, Rodez, Cahors, and Agen; in the letters of Innocent III <note>Pope Innocent III, who initiated the Albigensian Crusade</note>, the province was called the Province of Auch. It is unnecessary to remind the reader that among these authors, "Provinces" refers to ecclesiastical districts, not civil ones; likewise, those Dioceses were by no means in the province of Narbonne. Before Albi became a Metropolis <note>An archiepiscopal see with authority over other bishops</note>—which happened under Innocent XI—it was in the province of Bourges, as is also read in the *Notitia* <note>A register of administrative divisions</note> which Holstenius published from the Royal codex; and in the same province were Rodez and Cahors, which are now subject to the Bishop of Albi; while Agen was in the province of Bordeaux. VI. But to return to the regions which the Albigensian Heresy pervaded, Peter makes Toulouse (2) the foremost of those cities. *This Toulouse,* he says, *entirely deceitful* <note>original: "dolosa". The author is recorded here using a common medieval Latin pun: "Tolosa tota dolosa," implying the city was as treacherous as its name was similar to the word for deceitful.</note> *from its first foundation, as it is asserted, has rarely or ever been free of this plague, or rather this detestable pestilence of heretical depravity, the poison of the superstition of infidelity being diffused successively from fathers to sons.* Concerning the city of Béziers afterward (3): *Béziers was a most noble city, but entirely infected by the poison of heretical depravity;* and of Carcassonne (4): *for the citizens of Carcassonne were the worst Heretics, and exceedingly great sinners before the Lord.* But it would be endless to enumerate every single town or castle, since it is entirely clear that those regions were most crowded with them <note>The heretics</note> from the *History of the Albigensians* by Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay, and from Guy's epitome of the same (5), or from the *Life* of Innocent and Honorius III under the name of the same Bernard (6). 

And in the Albigensian territory, Peter relates (7) that there was a most noble castle which is called **Castres** <note>original: "Castra"</note> *as if it were the head of the whole Albigensian territory.* Likewise, he calls another most noble castle near Albi by the name of **Lombers**. Elsewhere (8) in that same Albigensian territory, **Rabastens** <note>original: "Castrum Rabatense"</note> is named, as are **Montaigu** <note>original: "Monsacutus"</note>, **Gaillac**, **Cahuzac**, **Saint-Marcel**, **Varen** <note>original: "Guespia"</note>, and **Saint-Antonin**; these last six Raymond, the Count of Toulouse, had taken at this time from the Viscount of Béziers, namely Raymond Roger, the nephew of the Count of Toulouse (9). To these, when they are reviewed elsewhere (10) by Peter the Monk, is added the castle of **Grave**. 

In the diocese of Toulouse, there are located in the same place **Puylaurens** <note>original: "Podium Laurentii"</note>, which elsewhere (11) he calls the *castle of Puylaurens*, and he adds: *Now, Puylaurens was a certain noble castle three leagues distant from Lavaur in the diocese of Toulouse;* he had previously mentioned that its Lord was Sicard; also **Cassès** <note>original: "Casser"</note>, **Saint-Félix**, **Montferrand**, **Avignonet** <note>original: "Avinio"</note>, **Saint-Michel**, **Cuq** (which elsewhere (12) is called **Mont-Cuq**), and **Saverdun**. Elsewhere at **Pamiers** <note>original: "Apamiae"</note> (13) near Carcassonne, the chief town of the Albigensians and most famous in this history (14), three most strongly fortified castles are located (15) *in which,* says Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay, *there lived at that time the chief enemies of our faith.* **Minerve** <note>original: "Minerba"</note> is also most famous in this history (16), along with the castle of **Pennes** <note>original: "Finarum"</note> and **Cabaret** (17). ...[at least in part] <note>This sentence fragment likely concludes a phrase from the previous page such as "The Albigensian regions at least in part..."</note> obeyed the Count of Toulouse, and Peter indicates there were many such lands (1) and names Caslutium. In the territory of Agen are **Marmande** (2), **Penne** (3), etc. It is not necessary to review every single one. It will not be troublesome to note here that this last castle of *Penne*, so called by Peter, should be understood in the *Life of Innocent* [III], attributed to [Bernard] Gui (4), where it reads: *In the same aforementioned year of the Lord, <term>1212</term> <note>original: MCCXII</note>, on the nones of June [June 5th], the very strong castle of Penne in the Agenais was besieged by Count Simon de Montfort with an army of crusaders <note>original: "crucesignatorum," literally "those signed with the cross"</note> in a powerful force; and after many assaults and warlike efforts, the castle was captured and held on the feast of Saint James the Apostle [July 25th].* For Peter relates all of this (5) and concludes: *Therefore, in the year of the Incarnate Word 1212, in the month of July on the feast of Saint James, the adversaries having been cast out, the Count recovered the noble castle of Penne.*

However, we have omitted what was especially worth mentioning, which might be of some value in illustrating the History of the Albigensians: *Arrium*, or the **Castle of Arrium** <note>This refers to Castelnaudary, a vital stronghold during the crusades against the Albigensian heretics.</note>. Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay mentions this (6): *The Bretons, making their way to the Count, came to the castle by the name of Arrium, which was still in the hands of the Count of Toulouse, in the territory of Toulouse.* More often among the same authors it is found named the *New Castle of Arrium*; William of Puylaurens (7) and the author of the *Life of Honorius* [III] under the name of Bernard Gui (8) relate in almost the same words that this was returned to the Count of Toulouse upon the death of Count Simon de Montfort: *Thus, with the land wavering enough due to unexpected events, after a few days the New Castle which is called "of Arrium" was returned to the Count of Toulouse.* Furthermore, that solemn inauguration of Amaury, son of Simon de Montfort, into knighthood took place—with Bishops and leading men assembling, as Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay teaches (9)—*at the New Castle of Arrium.* 

And the same castle is afterwards named precisely the *New Castle*; *The Count, however, because it was summertime, and because the New Castle could not adequately hold such a great multitude, for the reason that it had already been destroyed once or twice, etc.* From which place and from the history itself it is clear that this is undoubtedly that same castle which he had previously narrated as being extensively besieged (10), and which, after a very sharp conflict and a distinguished victory at the fortress of Saint Martin, was liberated from siege (11). Therefore, it is entirely clear that it is very famous in the History of the Albigensians. But to what purpose so much about it, and what light can this bring to our History? It may seem wonderful [surprising]...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Albigensian strongholds, specifically focusing on the castle of Arrium (modern Castelnaudary). The translator notes the previous page ended with "territor-," which is completed here as "territory."</meta>

...y [in the territory] of the Albigensians, as is often the case, and learned men have wondered that the Albigensians sometimes receive the name of "**Arrians**" <note>original: "Arrianorum"</note> among ancient writers. I do not deny that at least some of them held perverse views regarding the person and divinity of Christ. We can learn enough about this from Moneta (1). I add that others—such as William of Puylaurens in his *Prologue*, the *Summary* of Bernard Gui, and the *Auctarium Aquicinctinum* edited by Miraeus with Sigebert—count them among the errors of the Manichaeans, Waldensians, and even the Cataphrygians <note>The Cataphrygians were an early Christian sect also known as Montanists.</note>, and simultaneously attribute to them the name of the **Arrians**. 

But who would not wonder that they are called "**Arrians**" so precisely and absolutely? Yet, to say nothing of others, they are called such by [Roger of] Hoveden <note>A 12th-century English chronicler.</note>, who, when the Council of Lombers was assembled against the Albigensians, prefixed this title (2): *The Arrian Heresy and its condemnation* <note>original: "Hærefis Arriana ejufque damnatio"</note>; indeed, he frequently calls it the "Arrian Heresy" thereafter. And Baronius <note>Cesare Baronio, a 16th-century Cardinal and historian known for his "Annales Ecclesiastici."</note>, about to present this Council from the same Hoveden, writes (3): *In this same year, the heretics of Toulouse, otherwise called Albigensians, were detected; they are more akin to Manichaeans than to Arians, although they are found named as Arians.* 

Du Cange <note>A famous philologist and historian of the Middle Ages.</note> in his *Glossary* under the entry *Arriani*, admits that these were different from those ancient ones <note>The original Arians of the 4th century who followed Arius.</note>, although they are called by the same name. For my part, I consider them so different that the reason for the name in both cases is quite distinct. A slight discrepancy of a single letter ***r*** prompted me to suspect this. For the published books and manuscripts bring forward that famous Arius and the "Arians" <note>original: "Arianos," with one r</note> named after him; however, I see that everyone by common consent pronounces these others as "**Arrians**" <note>original: "Arrianos," with two rs</note>. 

Therefore, I believe they were named not after Arius the man and arch-heretic, but after the castle of **Arrium** [Castelnaudary]. And indeed, that the sects of the more recent Manichaeans or Albigensians were named after regions or towns—perhaps because they differed in opinions among themselves, or, as I rather think, because it was not so much what they believed that was considered, but which lord they submitted to, following different ones at different times—is proven by the other names: the heretics of **Toulouse**, **Agen**, **Bagnols**, **Concorezzo**, **Alba**, etc., of which we have spoken. 

If, therefore, the historians of these matters—Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay, William of Puylaurens, and the author of the *Life of Honorius III*—mention **Arrium**, the famous castle of the Count of Toulouse, why should we not say that what is so often found in Hoveden, *The Arrian Heresy and its condemnation*, where he reports on the Council of Lombers; and at the year 1178: *Meanwhile the Arrian Heresy, which had been condemned in the province of Toulouse etc.*, was so named not from Arius the arch-heretic, but from the castle of **Arrium**, which is most famous in Albigensian history? Truly, if this is not the case, or if it is plainly false, I would certainly wish to be better informed.

***

### CHAPTER III.

#### On the defenders of the Albigensian Heresy, and its destroyers.

I. I have already mentioned above that no certain author of the Albigensian Heresy can be put forward for these times, as is quite clear from the ancient and proven authors who wrote about it. Therefore, what St. Bernard (1) says regarding the heretics of Cologne applies to all of these: *Ask them for the author of their sect; they will name no one.* And yet there are some who are specifically named by these same historians, either because they brought the heresy into foreign lands or because they were especially eager to propagate it. 

First, if the Albigensians are those heretics of Cologne whom Caesarius <note>Caesarius of Heisterbach, a 13th-century Cistercian monk and author.</note> describes, there was a prominent figure among them named **Arnold**, who was the Master of the others. Caesarius says (2): *Under Archbishop Reginald, many heretics were seized at Cologne. They were examined by learned men and, having been defeated, were condemned by secular judgment. When the sentence was given and they were to be led to the fire, one of them named Arnold, whom the others acknowledged as their master (as those who were present recounted), asked for bread and a basin with water to be given to him, etc.* We have said above that these were either Albigensians or very similar to them.

In the *Chronicle* of John Brompton, a certain **Gerard** is further called a leader of this type of heretic. Regarding the year 1159, he says (3): *Certain heretics entered England, about thirty men and women in all, appearing to be of the German tongue, following a certain Gerard by name—who was somewhat educated—as their teacher and leader, though they themselves were unlearned simpletons.* <note>original: "idiotæ"</note> *The King, however, unwilling to release those caught in custody without examination or punishment, ordered a Council of Bishops to be held at Oxford. There, when questioned about the articles of Faith, that educated man replied with one voice for all: that they were Christians and wished to venerate Apostolic doctrine. However, they unanimously rejected holy baptism, the Eucharist, and marriage with a wicked boldness, etc.* 

William of Newburgh (4) recounts the same thing somewhat more extensively, calling this Gerard their leader: *under a certain leader Gerard, to whom all looked as their teacher and prince.* 

Furthermore, [it mentions] **Hartwin** as the author of that dogma—that only a marriage in which virgins are joined is just, and that they ought to produce only one offspring—as [noted] above from Eckbert... <note>Eckbert of Schönau, a 12th-century writer who wrote against the Cathars.</note>

(2) *Among these events, it reached the ears of those (who had been sent, naturally, to root out the Arian <note>The author uses "Arian" here as a historical label for the Albigensian/Cathar heresy, though their beliefs differed from the original 4th-century Arianism.</note>, that is, the Albigensian heresy) that certain false brothers—namely **Raymond**, and **Bernard Raymond**, and certain other leaders of heresy—transforming themselves into Angels of light, though they are of Satan, and preaching things contrary to the Christian faith, were deceiving the souls of many with their false preaching and dragging them down to hell. **Roger of Hoveden** subsequently records their condemnation and recites the letters written about it to the Catholics by **Peter, the Cardinal Legate**. But there is another figure mentioned by the same author—specifically in a letter he recites from **Henry, Abbot of Clairvaux**—concerning a teacher of the Toulouse Heretics (that is, the Albigensians), *whom the devil had so blinded as his sins demanded, that he called himself John the Evangelist, and separated the "Word which was in the beginning with God" from some other beginning of things, as if from another God; and it is added: This man was the head of those perishing in that city, and the prince of the Heretics.* His name was **Peter Moranus**, as is held in the same letter, and his judgment and penance are described later. 

Somewhat later, after Preachers were sent into the Province of Narbonne and had come to a certain fortified town called Caraman, they found—says **Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay** (3)—*a certain leader of heresy named **Baldwin**, and a certain **Theodoric**, a son of perdition and a spark for the fire <note>original: "stipulam incendii"; literally "stubble of the burning."</note>.... This man, originating from Gaul [France], was indeed of noble birth and had been a Canon of Nevers. Later, however, when a certain knight who was his uncle—and a most wicked heretic—had been condemned for heresy in the Council of Paris before Octavian the Cardinal and Legate of the Apostolic See; seeing that he could no longer remain hidden, he moved to the regions of Narbonne, where he was held in the greatest love and veneration by the Heretics, both because he seemed somewhat sharper than the others, and because he boasted that he came from France, where the fountain of knowledge and religion—*

[of the Christian] religion, a partner in his iniquity, and a defender of his wickedness. 11. But no one, when dealing with the defenders or patrons of the Albigensians, should be mentioned before the **Counts of Toulouse**. For this reason, **William of Puylaurens** <note>original: "Guillelmus a Podio S. Laurentii"; a 13th-century chronicler of the Albigensian Crusade.</note>, in beginning his *History*, explains the entire lineage of the Counts of Toulouse at the start (2). However, **Bernard Gui** (3) treated them separately and distinctly, as did the author who followed the history of the Albigensian heresy itself and the lives of Popes Innocent and Honorius. 

**William [of Puylaurens]** indeed began with **Raymond**, who was present at the siege of Antioch in 1098 and Jerusalem in 1099. Before him, **Gui** names Ysauretum (or Ysaurectus), Bertrand, William, Raymond I of Saint-Gilles, William Taillefer, and Pontius (whom he says was Count in the year 1060), and Aymeric. A shorter history or genealogy, which **William Catel** published from an ancient manuscript decorated with their portraits (4), places **Torsinus** (whom others call Chorson) before all of these; he says this man received the County from **Charlemagne**. 

In truth, Catel (5) explained the various opinions of writers on this matter, as did the most diligent Fathers of the **Congregation of Saint Maur** (6) <note>The Maurists were a celebrated group of French Benedictine monks known for their high standards of historical scholarship.</note>, from whom others have drawn their own work (7). This heresy actually occurred during the times of that **Raymond** who is the 14th Count according to the author of that genealogy, the 4th by that name according to Gui, and the 6th according to Catel. 

However, it is clear from what we were just saying that the heresy had spread through Toulouse earlier; and the father of this Raymond we just mentioned opposed it and summoned the **Cistercian monks** to smother it. In the **Chronicle of Gervase** (8), the following is recorded: 

> *In these days, a malignant heresy had sprouted in the territory of the Count of Toulouse which, nullifying the faith and the ecclesiastical institutions of the Orthodox Fathers, infected with a wicked plague not only the simple commoners but even the Priests of the Church of God and Bishops, along with the lay princes.* 

The detestable perversity of this heresy can be more easily recognized in the letters of the **Count of Toulouse**, which the same Count sent to the General Chapter of the Cistercians. He then includes the letters themselves, in which these words appear among others at the end: 

> *Now, therefore, fleeing to your protection, I implore you with a humble affection of the heart that you might reach out the hand of counsel and the aid of your powerful prayer to extirpate such a great evil of infidelity. For you must know that the heresy [has grown] to such an extent...*
