<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Counts of Toulouse and their involvement with the Albigensian heresy. The text begins mid-word, completing the term "heresy" from the previous page. The catchword "from her" (ab ea) at the bottom indicates the transition to the following page.</meta>

...heresy <note>original: "haeresis"; completing the word from the previous page</note> remains virulent and deep-seated, which cannot be rooted out except by the mighty hand of God and His outstretched arm. And he adds: "Since, therefore, we recognize that the power of the spiritual sword <term>spiritual sword (gladius spiritualis): the Church's authority to excommunicate or punish through religious law</term> can accomplish nothing to extirpate such great depravity of heresy, it is necessary that it be compelled by the punishment of the physical sword <term>physical sword (gladius corporalis): the use of secular military force or physical punishment</term>. To accomplish this, I urge that the Lord King of the Franks be summoned from your regions, because I suspect that through his presence such great evils will come to an end. For I will personally open cities to him, deliver villages and castles under his authority, point out the heretics, and even unto the shedding of blood, I will assist him in whatever business he requires of us to crush the enemies and all the opponents of Christ." These words clearly declare his exceptional care and diligence in defending the faith. Gervase <note>Gervase of Canterbury, a 12th-century chronicler</note> records this under the year 1177. And so these events seem to have preceded what we just reported from Hoveden <note>Roger of Hoveden, a contemporary English chronicler</note> regarding the Council that was held and the heretics condemned. I believe, however, that these matters are older than those. In any case, when Raymond’s son of the same name <note>Raymond VI, Count of Toulouse</note> took up his father's inheritance and the government of the County, "As if from his very first cradle," says Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay (1), "he always loved and cherished heretics, and having them in his land, he honored them in whatever ways he could; even until today, as it is asserted, wherever he goes, he takes heretics with him in common dress, so that if he happens to die, he may die in their hands." Peter describes his character and life at length in that same passage. This is the man who killed the Papal Legate Peter of Castelnau through his henchmen, who misused military force to protect his heretics against Catholics, and who used deception when he had obtained reconciliation from the Legate of the Roman Pope at Montélimar, only to turn his weapons against Catholics again. For this reason, stripped of his lands and dominion, he fled to the King of France and the Pope himself; finally, in the year 1222, he died a sudden death, as we know from the same Peter, William, and other historians. And William <note>William of Puylaurens</note> indeed indicates that he gave certain signs of penance (2); though these were reported by his son to the Roman Pope, as is clear from the documents that Catel (3) cites, they were not sufficient in the Pope's eyes to allow him to receive a Christian burial. Bernard Gui (4) recorded that this man had two brothers, Taillefer and Baldwin. Catel says he found another brother, Peter Raymond, in ancient records (5). But the one who is more frequently named in the *History of the Albigensians* is Baldwin (or Baudonus, as William of Puylaurens calls him) (6), who "having been born and raised in France," as the same author says, "came to his brother to stay with him: yet he was not..."

<meta>This page continues the history of the house of Toulouse during the Albigensian Crusade. It focuses on Baldwin (the brother of Count Raymond VI) and the eventual submission and public penance of Raymond VII ("the Younger") to the Church.</meta>

[Baldwin] was not recognized or received by him <note>referring to his brother, Count Raymond VI</note> as he had hoped. Therefore, it was necessary for him to obtain letters from those who knew him, testifying that he was the son of Lady Constance—mother of the aforementioned Count and sister of King Louis of France. Thus Raymond retained him, but only as a private favor; nevertheless, he appointed him as a warrior in the war he was waging in Provence with the Princes of Baux. Peter (1) records that after Montferrand was lost, this man surrendered himself to Simon, Count of Montfort, and was reconciled to the Church; he praises him thus: *Count Baldwin has been reconciled to the Church; from a servant of the Devil he has become a servant of Christ: behaving faithfully, he attacked the enemies of the faith with all his might from that day forward.* Elsewhere he relates (2) Baldwin’s wretched fate: when he fell into the hands of traitors, he was killed at his brother's command by the Count of Foix, his son, and Bernard de Portelles; William of Puylaurens also records this (3).

Raymond had a son of the same name: for which reason Raymond himself is called "the Elder" or "the Old" by Bernard Gui (4) and others. He had fathered this son by Joan, sister of King Richard of England, in the year 1197 at Beaucaire in the Diocese of Arles, as the same Bernard testifies (5), evidently following William of Puylaurens, who, however, numbers the year as 1196 (6). This Raymond, called "the Younger" (7), was involved in the same wars with his father; after his father's death, although he had recovered some of his domain by force, he consented to peace and was reconciled and absolved by the Church, even taking up the Cross for an overseas expedition <note>original: "transmarinam expeditionem"; referring to a Crusade to the Holy Land</note> in April 1228. It was a moving sight <note>original: "pietas"; implying a scene that evoked both pity and religious devotion</note>, says William (8), *to see such a great man—who for so long had been able to resist so many and such great nations—being led naked in his shirt and breeches and with bare feet to the altar*; Bernard (9) uses the same words. Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay (10) describes a similar rite by which the father <note>Raymond VI</note> had previously been reconciled to the Church: *The Count was led naked before the doors of the Church of Saint Gilles, and there, in the presence of the Legate, Archbishops, and Bishops—more than twenty of whom had assembled for this—he swore upon the Body of Christ and the Relics of the Saints, which were displayed before the church doors with great veneration and in great abundance by the Prelates, that he would obey the mandates of the Holy Roman Church <term>S.R.E. (Sancta Romana Ecclesia): the institutional Catholic Church</term> in all things. Soon the Legate had a stole placed around the Count's neck, and taking the Count by the stole, he led him, absolved but with lashes <note>original: "verberibus"; refers to the ritual scourging or striking with a rod during penance</note>, into the church.* The conditions under which he obtained this are found in the Acts which Catel produced (11); from which also

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Raymond VII ("the Younger"), Count of Toulouse. The previous page ended with Raymond taking up the Cross for the Crusade as part of his reconciliation with the Church after the Albigensian wars.</meta>

It is also well established (1) that he chose to remain in custody in Paris while his daughter was being handed over as a hostage to the legates of the King of France at Carcassonne. When he was restored to his lands in the year 1244, he held what is called the "Great Court" at Toulouse, and in 1245 he attended the Council of Lyon. In 1249, at the age of 51, he died at Millau <note>original: "Emiliani"</note> on the 5th day before the Kalends of October <note>September 27, 1249</note>. (Although because the names are similar, there are some who have written that he died in Milan <note>original: "Mediolani"</note>). He left behind an only daughter who was married to Alphonse, the brother of King Louis IX of France; however, they died without children in the year 1271, *so that it seems*, says Bernard Gui, *that entire line was deleted and wiped away.* <note>original: "deleta tota illa linea & abrasa"</note>

The name of the daughter of the Count of Toulouse was *Joan*. The same Bernard (3) writes that she was born in 1221, while William of Puylaurens (4) says 1220. I do not know if this discrepancy can be excused or explained by any logic. Indeed, the most diligent writers of the Dominican Annals <note>original: "Annalium O. P. Scriptores"; O.P. stands for Order of Preachers</note> have observed that not only William, but also Bernard Gui, calculated years according to the old French custom—that is, starting the year from Easter or from the Incarnation <note>This "Easter style" meant the new year began on the moveable date of Easter, or on March 25th (the Annunciation), rather than January 1st.</note>. And if he is indeed the author of the *Lives of Innocent III and Honorius* which are read in the works of Muratori (5), that point is certainly clear. For example, in the *Life of Innocent* for the year 1212, he first narrates events from the month of June, and then those from the month of February (6). <note>In the Easter-start calendar, February would come after June because the new year hadn't started yet.</note>

However, I will mention something that learned men will find worth noting: almost all the material Muratori published under the title of those *Lives* had already been published by Catel in the appendix to his *History of the Counts of Toulouse* under a different title: *The Illustrious Deeds of the Franks, or a Chronicle from A.D. 1202 to A.D. 1311 by an Uncertain Author*. These same works are found published again by Duchesne under this other title (7): *The Illustrious Deeds of the Franks and their various struggles in many places, carried out with great vigor both against the enemies of the Orthodox faith and against the people of France themselves, from A.D. 1202 to A.D. 1211, which is commonly designated by the name of the Chronicle of Simon de Montfort; the Author, as some believe, being Peter, Bishop of the Church of Lodève.*

But I would not deny that Bernard himself counted years in that old fashion. It is possible that he differed slightly from others. For some began the year from the Nativity <note>Christmas</note>, while others began from Easter, and others from the Annunciation of the Virgin (or the Incarnation of Christ). I believe entirely that our own writers of Albigensian history, William and Peter—a point others have left in doubt—followed this last method <note>The Incarnation style, starting March 25</note>. I gather this from the Statutes written in this century for the churches of Cahors, Rodez, and Tulle.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of medieval dating methods and the political landscape of the Albigensian Crusade. The previous page established that historians like Bernard Gui and William of Puylaurens used the "Easter style" or "Incarnation style" for their calendars, which can cause confusion for modern readers regarding the exact year of an event.</meta>

...from the statutes of Mende in Martene (1); *Note that the Lunar number and the Dominical letter change annually on the feast of the Circumcision <note>January 1st</note>: but the years of the Lord’s Incarnation change in this land on the feast of the Annunciation of the Blessed Mary <note>March 25th; this refers to the 'Lady Day' or 'Annunciation Style' of dating</note>; and in certain regions on the feast of the Nativity of the Lord <note>December 25th</note>.* These statutes were issued, as stated in the same source (2), in the year 1289. Among the regions mentioned in the final words was Dauphiné, where the famous historian Claude de Valbonays shows that years were calculated from the Nativity. Who would doubt that William followed the custom of his own people, having been born or educated in these lands—which is why I believe he is called "of Puylaurens" <note>original: "de Podio S. Laurentii"</note>? The same applies to Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay; his own history (3) testifies that he followed this same old method of counting, and he lived in these regions, as he himself reveals (4).

III. Besides the Counts of Toulouse, who were the primary supporters of the Albigensian Heresy <note>A medieval religious movement in Southern France that the Catholic Church sought to eliminate during the Albigensian Crusade</note>, others are named in a petition presented by the King of Aragon to the Fathers of the Council of Lavaur <note>original: "Vaurensis Concilii"</note>; Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay included this petition in his history (5). In that document, after the King spoke on behalf of the Count of Toulouse, he adds: *And since the Count of Comminges was never a Heretic, nor a protector of them, but rather an opponent, he is said to have lost his land only because he stood by his cousin and Lord, the Count of Toulouse, etc. Likewise, since the Count of Foix is not, nor ever was, a Heretic, the aforementioned King asks and requests for him, as for his most dear kinsman, etc. Likewise for Gaston of Béarn, his vassal, etc.* All of these men are excused from heresy in this petition; however, it is clear from the historians, and from the response given by the Council Fathers (which Peter also recorded), that they protected the heresy more than anyone else. Their names are found in certain Acts written in the year 1212 for this purpose: when Peter, King of Aragon, demanded that he might negotiate their reconciliation. In those documents produced by Catel (6), it reads: *We, Bernard, Count of Comminges, and Bernard his son, etc. We, Raymond Roger, by the grace of God Count of Foix, and Roger Bernard his son, etc. I, Gaston, by the grace of God Viscount of Béarn and Count of Bigorre, etc.* A difficulty arises here because the Count of Foix is called "Raymond," for William of Puylaurens (7), and the author using the same words...

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the specific dates and identities of the Southern French nobility during the Albigensian Crusade, specifically addressing discrepancies in the records of the Counts of Foix and the Viscounts of Béziers.</meta>

In the *Life of Honorius* <note>Pope Honorius III, reigned 1216–1227</note>, under the name of *Bernard Gui* (1): *In that same year, Bernard Roger, Count of Foix, died at the siege of the Castle of Mirepoix; not from a wound, but weighed down by a great ulcer*; and Bernard Gui adds: *to whom succeeded his son Roger Bernard*. Conversely, an Anonymous writer in a Chronicle found in the same Catel <note>Guillaume Catel, a 17th-century historian of Languedoc</note> (2) agrees with those Acts, writing for this year—that is, 1222: *In that same year died Raymond Roger, Count of Foix*. And, if those added names in the genitive case <note>the possessive form used in Latin to indicate parentage</note> indicated the Father, the matter would be settled, since the son is called *Roger, son of Bernard* <note>original: "Rogerius Bernardi"</note>. But indeed it seems that things were sometimes otherwise; they did not always address someone by their father's name, but by some very illustrious ancestor among their forebears. This was noted by Muratori (3) <note>Ludovico Antonio Muratori, a famous Italian historian</note> regarding the most noble family later called Orsini: for even great-great-grandsons were called *sons of Ursus* <note>original: "ex filiis Ursi"</note> after an earlier ancestor named Ursus. However, I would prefer to believe William [of Puylaurens] and Bernard Gui; either because this was the more frequent custom, as the same Muratori (4) and Mabillon (5) <note>Jean Mabillon, a pioneer of the study of ancient documents</note> have shown, or because no reason appears why the father would be called *Raymond Roger* and the son *Roger Bernard*. I think it should be noted, however, that the same name is sometimes expressed in various ways.

Thus, the Viscount of Béziers, who is called by Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay (6) *by the name of Raymond Roger*, is called *Roger* by Bernard Gui, or rather by the Author of the *Life of Innocent* <note>Pope Innocent III</note> (7); or else *Raymond Roger*; and by [Guillaume de] Nangis (8) *Roger of Béziers*: which seems to indicate that *Roger* was a surname. Furthermore, this Viscount Roger is called a *treacherous man* by Nangis, *through whose wickedness a pestilential error had grown*; and by Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay (9) he is called the *nephew of the Count of Toulouse, who, following his uncle's depravity, did nothing to suppress the Heretics*. But the Count of Foix (10) is accused much more severely by the same author, and is called *the worst traitor, the most cruel persecutor of the Church, an enemy of Christ*; and elsewhere (11) he attributes the slaughter of the Cistercian monks, carried out by William of Rochefort, to him; it is also charged against him by the Fathers of the Council of Lavaur that he laid hands on clerics and thrust them into prison, and that he advanced with slaughters against the Crusaders <note>original: "Crucefsignatos", literally 'those signed with the cross'</note>. His son *Roger, not unequal to his father in malice* (12) is called by Peter, and it is related that he killed many Crusaders by treachery (13). Bernard, the Count of Comminges <note>original: "Comes Convenarum"</note>, is also named by Bernard Gui where he describes his death (14): *In the year of our Lord 1225, in the month of February, died Bernard, Count of Comminges, husband of the Lady Maria of Montpel-* <note>The text cuts off mid-word; it refers to Montpellier</note>

...from Montpellier <note>original: "te-Pesulano", referring to Marie of Montpellier</note> who was afterwards the mother of the King of Aragon, the Lord James; and the Count was buried at Mont-Salvi. To him it is mostly attributed by the Council of Lavaur <note>original: "Vaurensi Concilio", a church council held in 1213</note>, that he urged the Count of Toulouse himself to undertake the war against the Church; whence, it says, the same Count stood as the instigator of this war and the evils which multiplied for the Church in many ways. Gaston of Béarn is described by Peter (1) as *a most wicked man, who had always adhered to the Count of Toulouse*: indeed, it is objected by the Fathers of the Council of Lavaur that he came to the aid of the people of Toulouse at the siege of Castelnaudary; he keeps with him the murderer of Brother Peter of Castelnau, Legate of the Apostolic See <note>Peter of Castelnau was the papal legate whose assassination in 1208 triggered the Albigensian Crusade</note>; and he has long held and continues to hold mercenaries <note>original: "ruptarios", companies of mercenary soldiers often accused of pillaging</note>. Others besides these are named as the principal supporters of the Albigensians and are joined together by Peter in Chapter 36. At that time, Peter Roger, lord of Cabaret, and Raymond of Termes, and Aimery, Lord of Montréal, and other knights who were resisting the Church and the Count, sent word to the King of Aragon, who was in those parts, that he should come to them; and by establishing him as their lord, they would hand over the whole land to him. The first of these is described elsewhere by the same author (2) as follows: *Peter Roger, a long-standing practitioner of evil days, was a heretic and a manifest enemy of the Church*. Later, in the year 1210, as Bernard Gui relates in the *Life of Innocent* (3): *Peter Roger, who was the Lord of Cabaret—which is a very strong castle in the Carcassonne region—driven by fear, made an agreement with Count Simon [de Montfort], and surrendered the Castle of Cabaret to him; and the Count gave him other suitable land*. Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay (4) says the same. Regarding the second man, this same Peter (5) writes where he describes the Castle of Termes: *Indeed, the lord of this castle was a certain knight named Raymond the Elder, turned toward a reprobate mind, and a manifest heretic; to express his malice briefly, he neither feared God nor respected men*. He then relates (6) that when the castle had been captured with Divine help, this Raymond was captured by a certain low and abject man from Chartres and handed over to the Count of Montfort; the Count had him thrust into the bottom of the tower of Carcassonne, where for many years he suffered punishments and miseries worthy of his deserts. Similarly regarding Aimery, who is called by him *a knight than whom no one in that whole land, after the Counts, was more powerful or more noble*, he reports that he first adhered to Count Simon, but then defected from him. His end is described by Bernard (8) thus: *In the year 1211 . . . Simon de Montfort raised Aimery, a noble magnate and Lord of Montréal and Laurac, who had undertaken to defend the castle, to the gallows with certain nobles, and [executed] the other well-born men, more than* <note>Historical records indicate that eighty other knights were executed alongside Aimery after the fall of Lavaur</note>

...than 80 [knights] he finished off with the sword. Truly, *Geralda*, the lady of the said castle <note>Giraude de Lavaur, known for her charity; her execution was considered particularly shocking even by contemporary standards</note> and sister of the said *Aymeric*, being a heretic, he caused to be thrown into a well and buried under stones. Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay likewise records these things (1). Regarding the King of Aragon <note>Peter II of Aragon, who initially tried to mediate before joining the fight against the Crusaders</note>, the same authors say much: that he was present at the conference held at Narbonne with the Legates of the Apostolic See (2); that Raymond <note>Raymond VI, Count of Toulouse</note> fled to him to obtain help (3); that he came to Toulouse and spoke with Simon de Montfort and the Legate of the Apostolic See, and proposed those petitions, of which we have spoken, to the Fathers assembled at Lavaur (4); and that he even declared war against the Count de Montfort (5), who nevertheless won a most celebrated victory on the field of Muret (6), where the King himself was killed. Regarding his marriage entered into with Maria, the daughter of William of Montpellier, from whom he fathered James <note>James I of Aragon, known as "the Conqueror"</note>, who was also King of the Aragonese, William [writes] in the History of the Albigensians (7).

We can add many more to these, famous among the same writers and in the history of the Albigensians: William of Rochefort <note>original: Guillelmum de Rupeforti</note>, whom we also named above; he is called by [Peter of] Vaux-de-Cernay (8) *most cruel, and one of the worst enemies of the Church according to his power*, and elsewhere (9) *a most monstrous enemy of Christ, a most ferocious persecutor, who, when he had killed Cistercian monks, received the reward of his cruelty... receiving infinite wounds not long after. At the very gate of Toulouse, he was killed by the soldiers of Christ as he deserved*; Savary de Mauléon (10), *chief of the apostates* etc., who is called thus by the same author [Nangis at the year 1203: Savaricus de Malo-leone]; Foucaud de Berne <note>original: Folcaudum de Belgeno</note>, who is described by William of Puylaurens (11) as *a most cruel and proud man who had established a rule for himself, so it was said, that every man captured in war should die unless he gave a hundred shillings*; and his brother John, etc. 

In the final place, we must name the Archbishop of Auch and Raymond of Rabastens, *who formerly*, says Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay (12), *had been the Bishop of Toulouse, but was deposed as his merits required*. These had been sent to Rome by the Count of Toulouse, when Fulk of Toulouse and Navarre, Bishop of Couserans, had come with him to Pope Innocent, *to entreat*, says the same Peter, *the Lord Pope, to stretch out a helping hand to the Church, which was in danger in the provinces of Narbonne, Bourges, and Bordeaux, and was almost completely shipwrecked, for the sake of the peace of the Church*. The [Archbishop] of Auch <note>original: "Auxitanus" or "Ausciensis"</note> was Bernard, against whom Innocent III wrote quite often. Different from him was that Archbishop of Auch who in—

...among the Crusaders is recorded by Bernard Gui <note>original: "Bernardo Guidonis," a famous Dominican inquisitor and historian</note> in the *Life of Honorius* for the year 1227, along with the Archbishops of Bourges and Bordeaux.

IV. But it is time to turn eventually to the conquerors of the Albigensians themselves. Against those whom we have just named—the Count of Toulouse and his followers—and the Albigensians, all Christians were aroused. *Moreover, the Supreme Pontiff (the Pope), says [Peter of] Vaux-de-Cernay (1), in order to make the faithful people more ready to root out the heretical plague, had sent general letters to all Prelates, Counts, and Barons, and the entire people established in the Kingdom of France, effectively advising and exhorting them to hasten to avenge the injury to the Crucified One <note>original: "injuriam Crucifixi" — referring to the perceived insults to Jesus Christ by heretical teachings</note> in the Province of Narbonne, knowing that the remission of all sins had been granted by God and His Vicar <note>the Pope</note> to all who, kindled with zeal for the Orthodox faith, should gird themselves for this work of piety, provided they were equally contrite and had confessed.* Among other princes, he later names those who gathered at Lyon (2); *Among those who were present there, these were considered the foremost: the Archbishop of Sens, the Bishop of Autun, the Bishop of Clermont, the Bishop of Nevers, Duke Odo of Burgundy, the Count of Nevers, the Count of Saint-Pol, the Count of Montfort <note>Simon de Montfort, the primary leader of the Crusade</note>, the Count of Bar-sur-Seine, Guichard of Beaujeu <note>original: "de bello joco"</note>, William des Roches, the Seneschal of Anjou, Gaucher of Joigny <note>original: "Gualchenus de Jovinjaco"</note>, Guy de Lévis, and Lambert of Thury.* He notes the time they gathered at Lyon as 1206 AD, around the feast of Saint John the Baptist. The Archbishop of Sens was Peter of Corbeil. He is called *Master Peter of Corbeil* <note>original: "Magister Petrus de Corbolio"</note> by William of Nangis (3), where he records his death in 1222; and in 1199, he is noted as *Peter of Corbeil, once the teacher of Pope Innocent*. In 1200, he had been transferred to this Church from the See of Cambrai. The Bishop of Autun was Walter, the second of that name, to whom a letter of Innocent III survives regarding the reform of the Abbey of Autun (4). The Bishop of Clermont was Robert of Auvergne, about whom Alberic says in his *Chronicle* (5): *At Clermont in Auvergne, after Bishop Pontius, Bishop Sigebert presided, and after him was called Bishop Robert, brother of Counts William and Guy of Auvergne, son of Count Robert by the sister of the Duke of Burgundy, of the lineage of the Counts of Champagne.* Mention is made of him frequently in the letters of Innocent [III]: for there were great disagreements between him and his brother Guy. He is addressed by his own name by the same Peter (6) <note>Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay</note> when the siege of Montgrenier is described: *Meanwhile the noble Count de Montfort [took] the camps which had apostatized...*

# 44

In that same year, he was vigorously attacking [the enemy] in the diocese of Nîmes <note>original: "Nemaufenfi"</note>. To his aid had come Gerard, Archbishop of Bourges <note>original: "Bituricenfis"</note>, and Robert, Bishop of Clermont <note>original: "Clarimontenfis"</note>, powerful men who in the preceding year had taken up the cross against the disturbers of peace and the subverters of the faith. He names the preceding year as 1215. However, as is clear from what has been said, they did not then take up the cross for the first time; for they did the same thing quite often. Indeed, to obtain the Church's Indulgence, it was sufficient for them to remain in the army for <term>forty days</term> <note>original: "quadraginta dies," often called a "quarantaine." This was the standard period of service required for a crusader to receive a full remission of sins</note>. 

Peter <note>Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay, the primary chronicler of this crusade</note> indicates this same fact frequently; *the Archbishop of Rouen,* he says (1), *and the Bishop-elect of Laon, and the others who were with them, wished to depart once their <term>forty-day term</term> <note>original: "quadragena"</note> was fulfilled.* Even on a daily basis, the <term>Pilgrims</term> <note>original: "Peregrini" — a term medieval writers used specifically for Crusaders, as the crusade was legally considered a holy pilgrimage</note> departed as their forty-day terms were completed. A little earlier, he writes: *Indeed, almost all the Pilgrims who were with him wished to return to their own homes once their forty-day term in the army was finished.*

Here and elsewhere, he very frequently understands "Pilgrims" to mean the **Crusaders** <note>original: "Crucefignatos," literally "those signed with the cross"</note> themselves who had arrived from foreign lands; for this reason, in the same passage (2), he writes: *since we could not besiege the castle on all sides because of the small number of pilgrims... therefore, as the multitude of pilgrims grew, they occupied the said mountain, and as the pilgrims arrived little by little, they besieged the castle from almost every side.* Elsewhere (3), they are called "pilgrim knights."

The same author narrates that the Bishop of Clermont, together with the Archbishop of Bourges, also wished to depart now that this forty-day term was fulfilled: *Around the same time, the Archbishop of Bourges and the Bishop of Clermont, having completed the term of their pilgrimage—namely forty days—returned to their own homes.* It has been helpful to note these things, as it seems this has been overlooked by the compilers of these names and the lexicographers <note>dictionary-makers</note>.

Many praise William of St. Lazarus, the Bishop of Nevers <note>original: "Nivernenfem"</note>, for the innocence of his life (4). Peter mentions the Duke of Burgundy and the Count of Nevers quite often, especially when the Count of Montfort <note>Simon de Montfort</note> was elected to be placed in command of the captured cities. *The Barons held a council among themselves,* says Peter (5), *regarding whom they should place in command of the aforementioned land. And first, the lordship of the land was offered to the Count of Nevers, and afterwards to the Duke of Burgundy; but they were unwilling to accept it.* Nevertheless, the Count of Nevers departed from the expedition once Simon had been elected, as the same author records (6). After the Count was elected, the Abbot of Cîteaux <note>original: "Cistercienfis"</note> and the Duke himself immediately...

<meta>Catchword: Mare-. Continuity: This page follows the discussion of the 40-day term of service for Crusaders, illustrating how different noble leaders (the Duke of Burgundy and the Count of Nevers) responded to requests to extend their service beyond the minimum requirement.</meta>

# 45

*They approach the Duke of Burgundy and the Count of Nevers, praying and entreating that they might deign to remain just a little longer in the service of Jesus Christ <note>original: "J. C."</note>.... The Duke of Burgundy, however, being a most kind man, kindly agreed to those making the plea.... But the Count of Nevers <note>Hervé IV of Donzy, a powerful noble whose relationship with the crusade leadership was often strained</note> by no means wished to listen to those pleading, but immediately returned to his own lands: for that Duke and Count did not get along well.* The same author adds that the greatest part of the army withdrew with the Count of Nevers (1). Peter <note>Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay, the chronicler</note> himself, and others, frequently recalled the same event afterward. However, the Count of St. Pol <note>Guy de Châtillon</note>, who was numbered among them, will hardly be found in the records. I believe he is the Guy of whom the Author of the *Acts of Louis VIII* <note>original: "Auctor de Gestis Ludovici VIII"</note> speaks when narrating that he and all the **Crusaders** <note>original: "Crucesignatos," literally "those marked with the cross"</note> in the year 1226 besieged Avignon—a very strong and almost impregnable city, which had been subjected to excommunication by the Roman Church for seven years because of heretical depravity. He adds: *there died Guy, Count of St. Pol, struck by a stone from a stone-thrower <note>original: "petraria," a type of medieval siege engine or catapult</note>, a man vigorous in arms, a Catholic, and honest* (2). 

The Count of Bar <note>likely Theobald I of Bar</note> had a great reputation; thus, when news of his arrival was brought to Count Simon <note>Simon de Montfort, the primary military leader of the Crusade</note>, he rejoiced with very great joy, for great things were said of that Count (3). And indeed, he was present at the siege of Toulouse when it was first conducted, as [Peter] testifies in the same place: *the Count of Bar was in that siege, along with several noble men from Germany <note>original: "Alemannia"</note>,* which Bernard Gui also testifies in his life of Innocent V <note>The text says Innocent V, but the date 1211 suggests this is an error for Innocent III</note> regarding the year 1211: *Moreover, there was in his army the Count of Bar, and many nobles from Germany.* And indeed, in these passages, it is indicated that he came from Germany. 

The same Peter, when the Count later withdrew, weary of this military service, reports (4): *Then the noble Count [Simon] asked the noble Count of Bar and the nobles from Germany to go with him, and they all granted it and promised they would go. They began to go, therefore, but when they came near Castelnau <note>original: "Castrum novum"</note>, the Count of Bar recoiled from the promise he had made to the Count... soon the noble Count asked the Germans if they wished to go with him; they promised most willingly that they would go.* Thus, he contributed almost nothing to this expedition. But what of that phrase "above the Seine" <note>original: "supra Secanam"</note>? Is it from the river Seine? Or is it added to signify that this man was from a different Bar, and was a Frenchman? None of these things are certain to me. The story of Lambert of Thury is known from the same Peter, which he narrates in the same place: namely, that he was captured along with Walter of Langton <note>original: "Galtero de Langatone"</note>—or Langetona, as his brother the Archbishop of Canterbury <note>Stephen Langton, a major figure in the signing of the Magna Carta</note> is named by [Matthew] Paris—by the Count of Foix <note>Raymond-Roger, a fierce defender of his lands against the Crusaders</note> and thrust into a most foul prison. *Guy de Lévis*

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the catalog of noble and clerical leaders who participated in the Albigensian Crusade, specifically focusing on those who remained active in the campaign after the initial forty-day terms of service discussed on page 45.</meta>

# 46

Peter <note>Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay, the primary chronicler of the Albigensian Crusade</note> likewise calls the Marshal of the Count of Montfort <note>Simon de Montfort, the leader of the Crusade</note> *a faithful man and vigorous in arms* (1) elsewhere. Guichard of Beaujeu is called *Imbert of Beaujeu* by Bernard Gui in the *Life of Gregory IX* (2), and in the *Life of Innocent* (3), where he is also praised as *a warlike man and inclined to labor*. Elsewhere, the companions of the same expedition are named (4): the Bishop of Chartres, the Bishop of Beauvais Philip, Count Robert of Dreux, and the Count of Ponthieu, having with them a great multitude of pilgrims <note>original: "peregrinorum"; in this context, the term specifically refers to the Crusaders</note>: however, the Christian cause profited little through them.

Nevertheless, in the same place, praise is given to *the Venerable William, Archdeacon of Paris, who, inflamed by zeal for the Christian faith, exposed himself entirely to laborious anxieties for the service of Christ.* It is added: *He preached daily, instructed the people, providently oversaw the collections for the cost of the siege engines, and performed the other duties of this necessary diligence. He went very often to the grove, leading a multitude of pilgrims with him, and saw to it that wood for the use of the stone-throwers <note>original: "petrariarum," a type of medieval catapult</note> was carried back in great abundance.*

The author praises him elsewhere (5) and joins him with the Bishop of Carcassonne and the Archbishop of Reims: *The venerable men and the directors and masters of this business—namely the Bishop of Carcassonne and William, Archdeacon of Paris—labored most insistently in those things which were necessary for the siege. Also, the Archbishop of Reims, who was there, very often and most willingly ministered the word of preaching and exhortation to the pilgrims; by humbly exposing himself to those things which were needed for the siege and spending from his own liberality, he was very necessary to the business of Jesus Christ <note>original: "negotio Jesu Christi," a common term for the Crusade</note>.*

They were, of course, besieging the Castle of Moissac. During that siege, when the Albigensians <note>the Cathar heretics and their supporters</note> had captured a nephew of the Archbishop of Reims, they threw his severed limbs at those who were in the siege; and when the Archbishop saw this, he bore it with such constancy that everyone marveled at this extraordinary example of his fortitude. This man was Aubry de Humbert, called from the Archdeaconry of Paris to that See in the year 1207, as Alberic narrates regarding that year; he held it until the year 1218.

The Bishop of Carcassonne, who had been named, is Guy, formerly the Abbot of Vaux-de-Cernay, whom Peter the Monk of the same monastery often praises (6) and calls himself his nephew. He narrates this especially when he speaks of the city of Albi (7): *The Bishop-elect of the people of Carcassonne was in that city, and I with him; for he had brought me with him from France for his own comfort, a pilgrim in a foreign land, as I was a monk and his nephew.* By these same...

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the catalog of noble and clerical leaders who participated in the Albigensian Crusade, focusing on specific bishops and archbishops like Folquet of Toulouse and the brothers Manasses of Orléans and William of Auxerre, leading into the appointment of Simon de Montfort as the campaign's leader.</meta>

->### LIFE<-

In the same place we cited above, he adds the Bishops of Uzès and Toulouse. The Bishop of Toulouse was Folquet <note>original: "Fulco"; a famous troubadour who became a Cistercian abbot and then the Bishop of Toulouse, known for his fierce opposition to the Cathar heresy</note>, formerly the Abbot of Grandselve, and transferred to this See from Marseille, as William of Puylaurens <note>original: "Gulielmus de Podio"</note> especially narrates (1), following the expulsion of Raymond of Rabastens. Others have noted that we should not follow William the Breton <note>original: "Gulielmus Armoricus"</note>, who introduces a certain "Bernard" in Book 8 of the *Philippide* <note>An epic poem celebrating King Philip II Augustus of France</note>. The Bishop of Uzès—or *Ucetia* (for Probatius names himself Bishop of *Ucetia* in the Council of Agde in the year 506, and Firminus calls it the *City of Ucetia* in the Fourth Council of Orléans in the year 541; elsewhere it is found as the *Castle of Uzès*)—was Raymond, the fourth of this name, promoted to that See from his position as a Canon of Maguelone in the year 1212. You will find others named in the same place: Robert, Archbishop of Rouen; Robert, the Bishop-elect of Laon; and Enguerrand de Boves. Peter <note>Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay</note> specifically mentions Manasses, Bishop of Orléans, and William, Bishop of Auxerre, whom he calls *men in all things praiseworthy and constant, two great, indeed the foremost lights of the Gallican Church <note>The Catholic Church in France</note> at that time, and furthermore biological brothers, who had taken up the cross against the heretics... having gathered as many soldiers as they could, they set out with a marvelous fervor of spirit and courage against the heretics, ready not only to spend their own resources but even to expose themselves to danger and death, if necessary, for the service of Jesus Christ.* Likewise mentioned are Enguerrand de Coucy, Bishop of Paris; Robert of Tournai; and Juhel de Mayenne (2). But it would be an endless task to review them all. Alberic <note>Alberic of Trois-Fontaines, a Cistercian chronicler</note> names these (3) under the year 1209: *The Crusaders <note>original: "Crucefignati," literally "those signed with the cross"</note> against the Albigensians set out in their ranks to visit the provincial land of the Albigensians, to extirpate the various heresies which had sprouted in those parts and were confounding and weakening the Catholic faith as much as they could. Those who were signed with the cross on their breasts were: Alberic of Reims, Peter of Sens, and Robert of Rouen, Archbishops; Walter of Autun, Renaud of Chartres, Robert of Bayeux, Jordan of Lisieux, the Bishop of Limoges, and many other bishops and ecclesiastical persons; Odo, Duke of Burgundy; Hervé, Count of Nevers; and many other Barons, soldiers, and infinite people.*

**V.** But by no means should Simon, Count of Montfort, who was the leader and commander of the expedition, be passed over. And we have indeed alluded to him above, as the one chosen—after the cities of Béziers and Carcassonne were conquered—to take charge of those lands which had belonged to the Count of Toulouse... <meta>catchword: "fue-"</meta>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the account of the Albigensian Crusade, specifically moving from the character of the military leadership to the death of the primary leader, Simon de Montfort, and the subsequent transfer of his claims to the French Crown under King Louis VIII.</meta>

...had been, he might rule. This is narrated at length by Caesarius <note>Caesarius of Heisterbach, a Cistercian monk and chronicler</note> (1), Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay (2), and William of Puylaurens (3), and from these, Bernard Gui (4). All of them indeed praise him wonderfully, especially Peter, who writes these things among others regarding him (5): *“Although some might be found who equaled him in certain graces, I say boldly, hardly or never will one be found in whom such a fullness of gifts, both natural and divinely granted, flowed together at once.”* William (6) records that he was even praised by his own enemy, the Count of Toulouse. Peter then relates that he took up the holy expedition to Palestine <note>Refers to the Crusades to the Holy Land</note>. After he had conducted many affairs prosperously and happily, while he was again besieging the city of Toulouse, *“while the most brave Count stood with his men before his siege engines, lest the enemies should come out again to destroy the aforementioned engines, behold, a stone thrown by the adversaries' mangonel <note>original: "mangonello"; a type of heavy catapult used for throwing stones</note> struck the soldier of Jesus Christ on the head; having received the lethal blow, striking his breast twice and commending himself to God and the Blessed Virgin, imitating the death of the Blessed Stephen <note>The first Christian martyr, who was stoned to death</note> by being stoned in the city, he fell asleep in the Lord.”* Thus writes Peter (7). He calls Toulouse the “City of St. Stephen” because the principal church there is dedicated to that Martyr. Previously, Peter had narrated how Simon had been divinely saved after falling into a river (8).

Amaury <note>original: "Amalricus"</note>, his successor and heir, and also his son (9), is described by Peter (10) as *“a good and vigorous youth, an imitator in all things of his father’s goodness and vigor.”* He was not, however, equal to his father in fortune or glory. Therefore, seeing the inconstancy of the inhabitants of the land, and that from day to day they were turning against him, he resigned the land to the illustrious King of France, Louis <note>King Louis VIII</note>, and made him entirely the successor to his rights... which the same King, being devoted to God and magnanimous, accepted, conferring the office of Constable <note>original: "Constabulatus"; the highest military rank in the French kingdom</note> of France upon Count Amaury, *“whom he knew to be provident, vigorous, and experienced in military exercise”*; as William (11) and, in his words, Bernard Gui (12) write, referring to the year 1224. There was another son of Simon de Montfort named Guy, to whom he had given the Countess of Bigorre as a wife, so that he might strengthen the flanks of the County on the side of Gascony, as William (13) relates. However, he does not seem to have survived Simon for long; for immediately after his father's death, the same William (14) reports, *“it happened one day, in a certain assault, that Guy, Count of Bigorre and brother of Count Amaury, fell and expired, consumed by wounds, whose...”*

<keywords>Simon de Montfort, Amaury de Montfort, Guy de Montfort, Siege of Toulouse, King Louis VIII of France, Albigensian Crusade, Constable of France, Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay, William of Puylaurens</keywords>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page follows the account of the death of Simon de Montfort at the Siege of Toulouse. It transitions from his immediate burial to the broader history of the crusade’s participants, including the involvement of the French crown.</meta>

*The body, having been fittingly cared for, was sent back to his brother on a platform covered in purple:* <note>original: "corpus decenter curatum, in tabulato purpura cooperto remittitur fratri suo"</note> Bernard Gui (1) described these events in his usual manner, though he records them under the year 1218, which followed Simon’s death. Peter [of Vaux-de-Cernay] may seem to have suffered a lapse of memory when he refers to Guy as Count Simon’s brother (2): *“The Count with his knights besieged the castle from the West... while Guy, his brother, pitched his tents on the other side, namely from the East.”* But this is a different man; he is the uncle of the Guy we previously mentioned, who indeed, as the same Peter notes (3): *“had gone across the sea with him [Count Simon], but when the Count returned, this Guy remained in the lands across the sea; for he had married a most noble wife of royal blood there, who was the Lady of Sidon.”* <note>original: "partibus transmarinis"; referring to the Crusader States in the Levant, specifically the Lordship of Sidon in modern-day Lebanon.</note> Therefore, having been in those overseas regions until this day, he is now mentioned for the first time. 

Among all those who pledged themselves to the military campaign to root out the Albigensians, Louis VIII, King of France, must be especially commemorated. In the year of our Lord 1212, Bernard Gui says (4): *“in the month of February, Louis, the firstborn of King Philip of France—a most gentle youth and a young man of good character—marked himself with the sign of the cross against the heretical believers and their defenders, the Albigensians and the people of Toulouse. Out of love and a desire to emulate him, many knights took up the sign of that same life-giving cross, and the date for the Crusaders’ departure* <note>original: "Cruce-signatorum"; literally "those signed with the cross."</note> *against the heretics was set for the Octave of Easter.”* <note>The "Octave of Easter" is the eight-day period starting on Easter Sunday.</note> But because of the wars of the King of France being stirred up again, it was necessary for Louis himself and the Crusaders to be delayed from the execution of this plan for three years. 

And so, he himself later narrates that expedition under the year 1215. Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay reports the same (5). Truly, the bitter death of this same King is unknown to no one. For after La Rochelle was captured and other towns were taken by storm, and even Avignon had surrendered (6), while he was returning to France, he died at the castle of Montpensier in Auvergne in the autumn of 1226. It had been his intention to return to these lands the following spring, had he lived, as William says (7). To these I shall add Leopold, Duke of Austria, and others whom Caesarius mentions. If we focus on the French, we must not overlook Robert Mauvoisin <note>Robert Mauvoisin was a prominent Northern French knight and close associate of Simon de Montfort.</note>, who is praised by Peter as *“a most noble knight of Christ, a man of wonderful integrity, perfect knowledge, and incomparable goodness, who for many years had dedicated himself and his possessions to the service of Christ, and was promoting this business* <note>The Crusade</note> *most ardently and effectively. For it is he through whom, after God and above all others, the knighthood of Christ flourished anew.”*

--- Page 68 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page transitions from discussing the French royalty's involvement in the Albigensian Crusade to the ecclesiastical leadership, specifically the papal legates appointed to oversee the suppression of the heresy.</meta>

VI. The Legates of the Roman Pontiff <term>Legate: a personal representative of the Pope, often granted significant authority to act on his behalf</term> sent for the cause of the Albigensians are commemorated here by the historians. First, as Peter [of Vaux-de-Cernay] records at the beginning of his history, *two Cistercian monks, inflamed by zeal for the faith—namely, Brother Peter of Castelnau and Brother Raoul—were appointed Legates by the authority of the Supreme Pontiff against the plague of infidelity.* <note>original: "Monachi duo Cistercienses zelo fidei succensi... contra pestem infidelitatis instituti Legati"</note> To these same men (1) he later joins the venerable man Arnald, Abbot of Cîteaux. Caesarius [of Heisterbach] (2), where he named several Crusaders, says: *of all these, the preacher and head was Arnald, Abbot of Cîteaux, later Archbishop of Narbonne.* 

However, those first two soon died. Raoul died *in a certain Abbey of the Cistercian Order near Saint-Gilles, which is called Franquevaux* (3); while Peter of Castelnau was most cruelly killed by the henchmen of the Count of Toulouse, as Innocent III writes in letters which the same Peter recites (4). In these, the following is read: *We hope... that the death of the aforementioned man of God will not strike fear into our venerable brother, the Bishop of Couserans, and our beloved son A., Abbot of Cîteaux, Legates of the Apostolic See... etc.* <note>original: "Speramus... quod præfati hominis Dei mors... non solum timorem non incutiet"</note> I do not doubt that the letter A. signifies Arnald, whom we have said was a Legate of the Apostolic See. 

But to him must be added the Bishop of Couserans (also known as Conferanensis), Navarre of Dax, whom the Sainte-Marthe brothers <term>Sanmarthani: the authors of 'Gallia Christiana', a monumental documentary history of the French Church</term> teach was a Legate. From the same Peter (5), *the Lord Pope sent one of his personal clerks named Milo, a man truly honest in life, outstanding in knowledge, and eloquent in speech.* He was sent, however, as follows in the same source (6), *with the said Master Milo, a certain clerk named Master Theodisius, a Canon of Genoa, to assist the oft-mentioned Master Milo and help him in dispatching the business of the faith.* It is added: *This Theodisius was a man of much knowledge, a man of wonderful constancy, a man of exceptional goodness, who conducted himself well in the business of Jesus Christ.* 

The records of this Milo’s deeds are extant among the letters of Innocent III, to the great benefit of our history (7). Nevertheless, the Cistercian Abbot [Arnald] is still mentioned as Legate when Count de Montfort was elected leader of the army (8). As for Theodisius, he was involved when Count Raymond returned from Rome (where he had traveled) and was ordered to undergo a "purgation" <term>purgation: a formal legal process, often involving an oath, to clear oneself of criminal accusations</term> regarding two crimes: heresy and the murder of the Pontiff’s Legate, Peter of Castelnau. Peter (9) records that letters regarding this matter were sent by the Pontiff to the Bishop of Riez in Provence and to Master Theodisius. Both are also named when the siege of Minerve was held (10) and when the purgation was ordered (11). Later, the legation of Arnald, by then Archbishop of Narbonne, is mentioned even more frequently.

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the discussion of papal legates assigned to the Albigensian Crusade, moving from the early legates like Peter of Castelnau to later figures like Peter of Benevento and Robert of Courçon.</meta>

mention is made quite often (1), especially when the King of Aragon presented those requests on behalf of the Count of Toulouse, the Count of Foix, and other supporters of the Albigensians. After him, Master Peter of Benevento, a Cardinal Legate of the Apostolic See sent by the Lord Pope, is encountered in the writings of Peter [of Vaux-de-Cernay] (2) and William [of Puylaurens]. Peter later mentions the same man (3); and he is called Peter, Cardinal Deacon of Santa Maria in Aquiro, in the letters of Innocent III which he recites there. This Peter is famous in French history, and is described by [Peter of] Vaux-de-Cernay as a *wise and discrete* Legate: *for he reconciled the Counts of Foix and Comminges and others (4), receiving from them not only a sworn guarantee to stand by the mandate of the Church, but also certain very strong castles which still remained in the hands of the enemies.* <note>original: "Comites enim Fuxensem, & Convenarum aliosque reconciliavit... recipiens ab eis... quædam castra fortissima"</note> Peter (5), Bernard Gui (6), and others are witnesses that he held a Council at Montpellier in the year 1214. 

In the previous year, as we read in the same Peter (7), *the Lord Pope sent his Legate into France, Master Robert of Courçon, English by birth, with many sets of letters and indulgences, so that he might diligently preach, and cause others to preach, for the relief of the land of Jerusalem.* <note>The "relief of the land of Jerusalem" refers to the call for the Fifth Crusade to the Holy Land.</note> He adds that Robert insisted on this mission with great vehemence; however, those very preachers who were working for the "business of the faith" <term>Business of the faith: a technical term (negotium fidei) used to describe the Albigensian Crusade</term> against the aforementioned heretics, he took away from that task and made them preach for the cause of the Holy Land instead; *and so, according to human judgment, it almost led to the abolition of the business of the faith against the pestilential heretics.* Nevertheless, it is established that he also promoted that cause; *for he was at the siege of Casseneuil, as the same author records (8), and labored as much as he could, as a man of good will, for the capture of the castle.* Since, however, this was not among the primary duties entrusted to him—as is hinted here and elsewhere (9)—he hardly seems to be numbered among these legates of ours, even if [the historian] Raynaldus decides otherwise. 

In the year 1217, Bernard Gui (10) testifies that Cardinal Bertrand was the Legate sent by Honorius III, and that he was present when Toulouse was besieged. Peter (11) and William (12) also mention him. Robert had been sent elsewhere—specifically the count of the Crusaders who were setting out for the East. And to those men, when that Legate had been requested from Honorius, Honorius replied that he had sent Pelagius <note>Pelagius Galvani, a later leader of the Fifth Crusade</note>; and that he could send this man, since he was powerful in eloquence, to minister the word of God to them (13).

--- Page 70 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the history of the various Papal Legates dispatched to southern France to manage the Albigensian Crusade and the subsequent "business of the faith." It transitions from the work of Robert of Courçon to the missions of Cardinal Conrad, Cardinal Romanus, and eventually the appointment of local bishops to handle the burgeoning Inquisition.</meta>

It is clear from these same records that he was the Cardinal of the title of St. Stephen on the Caelian Hill; some call him Curson, others Curton (1). He is called Robert of Courçon in the Council of Paris held in the year 1212 (2). Bertrand was the Cardinal of the title of St. George in Velabro. In the year of our Lord 1221, Bernard Gui says (3), Conrad, a Cardinal-Bishop of Porto of the Cistercian Order, was sent as a Legate from the Apostolic See to the regions of the Albigensians and Toulouse. Caesarius (4) mentions this same Legate; William [of Puylaurens] (5) also recounts that he came to the aid of the Castle of Penne <note>original: "Castri de Penna"; this refers to the siege of Penne-d’Agenais in 1212, though the timeline here discusses the Legate's involvement in the region's defense</note>. Not much later, namely in the year 1224, as Bernard indicates (6), the Lord Romanus, Cardinal-Deacon of Sant'Angelo, was sent as Legate to the regions of the Albigensians and Toulouse. So it is written in the *Life of Honorius*; and the same author, quoting the words of William of Puylaurens (7), praises him thus: *“a man of great discretion, acceptable to God and men, and capable of handling such great affairs; with God's help, he persuaded the King, so that he himself might undertake the task which had been kept for him, though commanded under others.”* <note>original: "vir magnæ discretionis acceptus Deo & hominibus... qui cum Dei auxilio induxit Regem..."</note> This refers to Louis VIII. These same authors report that the Legate was always at Louis's side.

By his authority, the Abbot of Grandselve <note>A Cistercian monastery in the south of France</note> was sent to look after Albigensian affairs. For so says William (8), and from him Bernard (9): *“Meanwhile, the venerable Abbot of Grandselve, Lord Elias Garini, came from France by the authority of the Legate, offering peace to the people of Toulouse.”* The Legate himself held a Council at Toulouse (10). *“Lest the Legate seem—circumspect and far-seeing man as he was—to omit any of the matters at hand, he ordered an inquiry to be made against those suspected of heretical depravity,”* <term>Heretical depravity: *haeretica pravitas*, the standard legal term used by the Church to describe heresy as a criminal and moral corruption</term> says William (11), adding that the Legate returned to Rome and took all the *Acts* <note>The official records of the proceedings</note> with him. 

In the year of our Lord 1230, through the efforts of the council of Prelates, the Venerable Father Lord Clarius, Bishop of Carcassonne, approaching the Apostolic See, obtained the sending of a Legate to pursue the business of peace and the Faith—a man of great integrity and foresight, the Bishop of Tournai. This follows in the account of the same William (12), from whom Bernard (13), as is his custom, takes his material. They both recount his care and diligence. This man was Walter of Marvis, who had received that See in 1219 and held it until 1251. But before his death, as William adds (14) (events which Bernard refers to the year 1234 (15)), the Bishop of Tournai was relieved of the burden of the legation in those days, and the Venerable Father Lord John, Archbishop of Vienne, was substituted as Legate in his place—

*of Vienne*, namely Jean de Bernin, whose praises, expressed in his epitaph, the Sainte-Marthe brothers recorded (1). Finally, as we have from William <note>William of Puylaurens, a contemporary chronicler of the Albigensian Crusade</note> (2) and Bernard (3): with the same Archbishop of Vienne *removed from the office of Legate, the Bishop of Palestrina, a Cardinal in the Roman Church, was sent as a Legate a latere <term>a latere: A high-ranking Papal Legate sent directly 'from the side' of the Pope with full plenipotentiary powers</term> of the Supreme Pontiff*. Bernard, however, seems to refer this to the year 1237. This was, therefore, the man called James of Pecoraria, formerly a Cistercian monk and Abbot of the Monastery of Saints Vincent and Anastasius at the Three Fountains <note>original: "ad aquas Salvias"; this is the Abbey of Tre Fontane in Rome, the site of St. Paul's martyrdom</note>.

VII. Now we must speak of those to whom the task of preaching was committed. It is clear from Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay (4) that the Legates themselves, Pierre de Castelnau and Raoul, also performed this duty; he narrates that *“The people of Toulouse were often and greatly urged to abjure heresy and to banish the heretics”* <note>original: "Suasum est Tolosanis sæpe, & multum, ut hæresim abjurarent, ut hæreticos relegarent"</note>; and to these, as we have said, he also joins Arnold, the Abbot of Cîteaux (5). Concerning the same men later (6), namely Pierre de Castelnau and Brother Raoul, who had been the *Princes and Masters of the Preaching in the aforementioned land*, Caesarius <note>Caesarius of Heisterbach, a Cistercian monk known for his collection of miracle stories</note> says (7): *“Abbots of our order were sent with certain Bishops, to weed out the tares with the rake of Catholic preaching: but because the enemy man <note>A biblical reference to the Devil sowing tares among wheat</note> who had sown these was resisting, they made little progress there.”* However, I believe things that belong to later times are being joined together here. Indeed, whether these Abbots came continuously, and in what year they arrived, or whether they came once or twice to protect and restore the Catholic faith through sermons in these regions—this is truly not so easy to judge. But this entire matter has been explained at length and diligently by others, and in the most recent Annals of the Order of Preachers <note>The Dominicans</note> (8). For our part, if we follow Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay himself, those Abbots seem to have arrived somewhat later, that is, after the debate at Montréal had already been held. *“After the debate was celebrated,”* says Peter (9), *“in Montréal, while our Preachers were still at Montréal and sowing the word of God and warnings of salvation in the surrounding areas, begging for their bread from door to door, the venerable man Arnold, Abbot of Cîteaux, arrived from the regions of France, having twelve Abbots with him. These men of total religion, men of perfect and holy knowledge, men of incomparable sanctity—twelve arrived alongside the thirteenth Abbot, matching the number of the Holy Apostles—ready to give an account to every disputant concerning the Faith and hope that was in them. And all these, with many monks whom they had brought with them, following every path of humili-”*

<meta>Continuity: The previous page discussed Jean de Bernin, the Archbishop of Vienne, and his role as a Papal Legate. It is established from these same sources that he was a Cardinal of the title of St. Stephen on the Caelian Hill <note>Titular churches in Rome are assigned to Cardinals; St. Stephen on the Caelian Hill is an ancient basilica</note>; some call him Curson, others Curton (1). He is called Robert de Courson <note>An English intellectual and influential Cardinal who preached the Crusade</note> in the Council of Paris held in the year 1212 (2). Bertrand was the Cardinal of the title of St. George in Velabro. In the year of our Lord 1221, says Bernard Gui (3), Conrad, a Cardinal-Bishop of Porto from the Cistercian Order, was sent as Legate from the Apostolic See to the Albigensian and Toulousan regions. Caesarius (4) mentions the same Legate; William (5) also recounts that he came to the aid of the Castle of Penne.

Not long after, specifically in the year 1224, as Bernard indicates (6), the Lord Romano, Cardinal-Deacon of Saint Angelus, was sent as Legate to the Albigensian and Toulousan regions. This is noted in the *Life of Honorius* <note>Pope Honorius III</note>; and using the exact words of William of Puylaurens (7), he praises him thus: *a man of great discretion, accepted by God and men, and capable of handling such great affairs, who with God's help persuaded the King to take up the task himself, which had been commanded under others*, meaning Louis VIII. These same authors recount that the Legate was always at Louis’s side. He sent the Abbot of Grandselve <note>A major Cistercian abbey near Toulouse</note> to manage Albigensian affairs. Thus William says (8), and Bernard following him (9): *Meanwhile, the venerable Abbot of Grandselve, Lord Helias Garini, came from France by the authority of the Legate, offering peace to the people of Toulouse.*

The Legate himself held a Council at Toulouse (10). *Lest the Legate—who was a circumspect and provident man—should seem to omit anything relevant, he ordered an inquiry to be made against those suspected of heretical depravity* <note>original: "Hæretica pravitate"; this refers to the formal judicial investigations that evolved into the Inquisition</note>, says William (11); adding that the Legate returned to Rome and took all the official Acts with him. In the year of our Lord 1230, through the counsel of the Prelates, the Venerable Father Lord Clarius, Bishop of Carcassonne, approaching the Apostolic See, obtained the sending of a Legate for the business of peace and Faith—a man of great integrity and foresight, the Bishop of Tournai. This follows in the account of the same William (12), from whom Bernard (13), as is his custom, takes his material. They both recount his care and diligence. This was Walter of Marvis, who had received that See in 1219 and held it until 1251. But before his death, as William adds (14) (which Bernard refers to the year 1234 (15)): *The burden of the legation being removed in those days from the Bishop of Tournai, the Venerable Father Lord John, the Archbishop, was substituted as Legate in his place...*

<meta>Continuity: Following the discussion of Cardinal Legates like Robert de Courson and Romano Bonaventura, this page examines the roles of Jean de Bernin and Giacomo de Pecoraria, then shifts to the specific preaching missions led by Cistercian abbots and early "Preachers" (Dominicans) sent to combat heresy in the Languedoc region.</meta>

*The Archbishop of Vienne*, namely Jean de Bernin, whose praises were recorded by the Sainte-Marthe brothers <note>The Sainte-Marthes were 17th-century historians famous for compiling the *Gallia Christiana*, a massive history of the French Church</note> in his epitaph (1). Finally, as we learn from William (2) and Bernard (3): *when the same Archbishop of Vienne was removed from his office as Legate, the Bishop of Palestrina, a Cardinal in the Roman Church, was sent as a Legate a latere* <note>original: "Legatus de latere"; a "Legate from the side" is the highest class of papal representative, acting with the Pope's own authority</note> *of the Supreme Pontiff*. Bernard, however, seems to date this to the year 1237. This man was therefore the one called Giacomo de Pecoraria, formerly a Cistercian monk and Abbot of the Monastery of Saints Vincent and Anastasius at the Tre Fontane <note>original: "ad aquas Salvias"; an ancient abbey site in Rome where St. Paul was traditionally martyred</note>.

VII. Now we must speak of those to whom the task of preaching was committed. From Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay (4), it is established that the Legates themselves, Peter of Castelnau and Raoul, also performed this duty; he recounts that *the people of Toulouse were persuaded often and much to abjure their heresy and to banish the heretics*; and to these men, as we have mentioned, he also joins Arnold, the Abbot of Cîteaux (5). Regarding the same men—namely Peter of Castelnau and Brother Raoul—he later says (6) that *they had been the leaders and masters of the Preaching in the aforementioned land*. Caesarius says (7): *The Abbots of our order were sent with certain Bishops to root out the weeds with the rake of Catholic preaching: but they made little progress there because of the resistance of the enemy man who had sown these things* <note>A biblical reference to the Parable of the Weeds, where the "enemy" is the Devil</note>. However, I believe things pertaining to later times are being combined here. Indeed, whether these Abbots came continuously, in what year they arrived, or whether they came once or twice to defend and restore the Catholic faith through sermons in these regions—this is truly not so easy to judge. But this entire matter has been explained at length and with care by others, and in the most recent *Annals of the Order of Preachers* <note>The official history of the Dominican Order</note> (8). For our part, if we follow Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay himself, those Abbots seem to have arrived somewhat later—that is, after the debate had already been held at Montréal; Peter says (9): *After the debate was celebrated at Montréal* <note>A strategic hilltop town and Cathar stronghold where a famous public dispute between Catholics and heretics took place in 1207</note>, *while our Preachers were still at Montréal and in the surrounding areas sowing the word of God and warnings of salvation, begging for their bread from door to door, there arrived the venerable man Arnold, the Abbot of Cîteaux, from the parts of France, having twelve Abbots with him. These twelve arrived—men of total religious devotion, men of perfect and holy knowledge, men of incomparable sanctity—matching the number of the most Holy Apostles, with the Abbot being the thirteenth; they were ready to give an account to every questioner regarding the Faith and the hope that was in them. And all of these, with many monks whom they had brought with them, followed the path of all humility...*

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the discussion of the early preaching missions against the Albigensian heresy in southern France. It specifically examines the transition from the legatine missions to the collaborative effort involving the Bishop of Osma, Saint Dominic, and a large group of Cistercian abbots who adopted a lifestyle of apostolic poverty.</meta>

humility, *following the pattern that had been shown to them on the mountain* <note>original: "juxta exemplar quod eis ostensum erat in monte"; a biblical allusion to Exodus 25:40, implying they followed a divinely revealed model of apostolic life</note>, that is, according to what they had heard from the Bishop of Osma <note>Diego de Acebo, the mentor of Saint Dominic</note>, they proceeded on foot. Immediately, they were dispersed far and wide by the Abbot of Cîteaux <note>Arnold Amaury, the lead papal legate</note>; and to each were assigned their own specific boundaries, through which they would travel, applying themselves to preaching and laboring in formal debates. 

However, I do not deny that Abbot Arnold had been present before this, along with some others. And so I explain the account of Guillaume de Puylaurens <note>original: "Guillelmus de Podio"; a 13th-century chronicler of the Albigensian Crusade</note>, who, before he narrates the debate held at Montréal <note>original: "Montem Regalem"</note>, reports that Diego of Osma and Saint Dominic—having gathered those Abbots of the Cistercian Order and other good men—held debates. Unless we prefer to think that these accounts should be referred to the later events he narrates. For Guillaume does not follow a precise chronological order, especially at the beginning of his history. 

There may also be a slight controversy regarding the number of these men. For Robert of Auxerre <note>original: "Robertus Autissiodorensis"</note> at the year 1207, and in the same words Guillaume de Nangis (1), seem to count *fourteen abbots in total: By the counsel of Pope Innocent, the Abbot of Cîteaux and about thirteen other abbots of the same Order are delegated, all men of proven wisdom, prepared with eloquence, and ready to give an answer to anyone asking about the faith, not fearing to even lay down their lives for the faith. Having set out from Cîteaux in the month of May (Robert says March), they sailed down the Saône* <note>original: "Ararim"</note> *into the Rhône* <note>original: "Rodanum"</note> *with little expense and no horses* <note>original: "equitaturis nullis"</note>, *so that they might prove themselves to be evangelical men in all things.* 

These authors write this regarding the year 1207, adding that they spent three months on their work: *Thus for three months, through cities, villages, and towns, sought out with much labor and through ambushes, they recalled a few people, and instructed and confirmed more surely in the faith those few faithful who were found.* 

But Guillaume <note>de Puylaurens</note> testifies (2) that the preaching lasted two years and more: *With this labor continued for two years and more, when these blessed champions of God were unable to extinguish the fire that had been kindled through this path, realizing that this matter required deeper counsel, they were forced to cry out to the Apostolic See; and so that the preaching they had begun might continue, provision was made, by the Lord's inspiration, for the ordaining of perpetual Preachers. For this reason, under the Blessed Bishop Lord Fulk* <note>Fulk of Toulouse, a key supporter of the early Dominicans</note>, *the Order of Preachers was principally begun, whose standard-bearer, Blessed Dominic, took up both the leadership and the labor.* 

Thus writes Guillaume, who lived at that time and was a spectator and witness of these events. Furthermore, I believe that the "two-year period" refers to the preaching of Blessed Dominic, while those "few months" refer to the preaching of the Cistercian Abbots. Bernard Gui <note>original: "Bernardus Guidonis"; a famous Dominican inquisitor and historian</note> ascribes this two-year period to Saint Dominic in his *Life of Innocent*.

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the discussion of the reign of Pope Innocent III and the origins of the preaching mission in southern France. The previous page ended with a reference to the number of abbots involved in the mission as recorded by historical chroniclers.</meta>

[In]nocent [III] (1); *Indeed, Saint Dominic began the Order of Friars Preachers <note>original: "Ordinem fratrum Prædicatorum"; the formal name of the Dominican Order</note> in the regions of Toulouse, where he had begun to preach against the heretics by word and example nearly two years earlier, starting around 1203 A.D. This was in the sixth year of the pontificate of Pope Innocent [III], calculating from his first arrival when Saint Dominic reached Toulouse with Lord Diego <note>original: "Dydaco"</note>, Bishop of Osma <note>A diocese in Spain; Diego was Dominic's mentor</note>, while traveling toward the Marches, or Denmark <note>original: "Daciam"; in medieval Latin, this often referred to Denmark rather than the Roman province of Dacia</note>, and converted his host from heretical depravity on the first night they were received as guests in Toulouse in the aforementioned year of our Lord 1203.* Furthermore, Bernard [Gui] himself, in the booklet or treatise *On the Masters of the Order of Preachers*, which Martène published (2), calculates a two-year period starting from 1205—the year in which Saint Dominic found the twelve Cistercian Abbots at Montpellier; Nicholas Trevet (3) and others do the same. Pierre des Vaux-de-Cernay <note>original: "Petrus Vallicernenfis"; a Cistercian chronicler of the Albigensian Crusade</note>, although he says almost nothing about Saint Dominic, speaks of Diego and narrates that they found only those first Legates of the Roman Pontiff at Montpellier, and he does not seem to agree with these latter writers at all. But this whole matter has been so thoroughly treated in the *Annals* <note>Likely referring to the *Annales Ecclesiastici*</note> that it seems nothing more needs to be added here.

That Saint Dominic remained for a longer time involved in the subduing of the Albigensians <note>A dualist heretical movement in southern France</note> is clear from the fact that in 1213 he was present at the battle in which the King of Aragon was killed <note>The Battle of Muret, where Peter II of Aragon died fighting against the Crusader forces</note>; and thus we read in that *Life* of Innocent: *“Meanwhile, while the war of the Lord was being waged, seven Bishops who had assembled, and three Abbots... with their clerics and certain religious men—among whom was present that religious friend of God, Brother Dominic, Canon of Osma, later the founder of the Order of Friars Preachers—entered the church in the manner of Moses during the battle of Joshua, raising their hands to heaven, praying to the Lord for His servants, who were exposing themselves to the danger of imminent death for His name and faith.”* <note>A biblical allusion to Exodus 17:11; as long as Moses held up his hands, the Israelites prevailed</note> And this manner of addressing Saint Dominic seems to me to indicate entirely that the work is not by [Bernard] Gui himself.

It is clear from what has been said that the Bishop of Osma, with whom Dominic had come, was insistent upon the preaching mission; and Guillaume de Puylaurens <note>original: "Guillelmus de Podio"; a 13th-century chronicler</note> briefly narrates (4): *“In those very days, the Lord God Himself, who keeps chosen arrows in the quiver of His Providence, brought forth two chosen champions from Spain for this work: Lord Diego, Bishop of Osma, and a religious man, his companion Dominic, who was later declared a saint. These two, therefore, putting their hands to strong tasks, etc.”* The same author later narrates (5) what arguments Diego cast against the debating heretics.
