<meta>This page continues the narrative from page 35 concerning the early efforts of Saint Dominic and the Cistercians to combat the Albigensian heresy through preaching and formal debate before the outbreak of open warfare. It marks the beginning of Chapter IV, shifting focus to the specific methods of engagement: colloquies (formal debates) and early trials.</meta>

# CHAPTER IV.

### Concerning the Debates, Councils, and Wars held with the Albigensians, and the Judgments instituted against the Heretics.

I. All those arguments which are usually employed to refute heretics based on Church practice and Christian discipline were applied to the Albigensians. Pierre des Vaux-de-Cernay <note>original: "Petrus Vallicernensis"; a Cistercian chronicler of the Albigensian Crusade</note>, whom we have praised quite often <margin>(1) Chapter 3.</margin>, recounts a Debate <note>original: "Colloquium"</note>, or discussion, held by the Bishop of Osma <note>Diego de Acebo, mentor to St. Dominic</note> and the Cistercian Monks in a certain castle at Caraman <note>original: "Carmano"; a town in the Languedoc region</note> with the heretic Theodoric, or William. William <note>likely William of Puylaurens, another contemporary chronicler</note> indicates that several discussions were held, including one at Verfeil <note>original: "Viridem folium"; Latin for "Green Leaf," referring to the town of Verfeil</note>. Therefore, after those words of his that we recently cited: *These two, then, setting their hands to strong works <note>original: "mittentes manus ad fortia"; a biblical allusion to Proverbs 31:19</note>, and having gathered those Abbots of the Cistercian Order and other good men, [approached] the superstition of the heretics—who boast in the heights of Satan—not with a pompous or mounted multitude, but on foot. They traveled from castle to castle on bare soles and feet to the appointed debates. One of the first encounters was at Verfeil, where many arch-heretics <term>Hæresiarchæ|The leaders or founders of a heretical sect</term> were present, namely Pontius Jordan, Arnaldus Arifati, and others.* 

Pierre also indicates that such debates were frequently organized, recounting what Saint Dominic <margin>(2) Chapter 7.</margin>, a man of total holiness, had with a heretic, recording in writing the authorities <note>citations from Scripture or Church Fathers</note> he had produced. This writing, when it was thrown into the flames three times, was never burned. Both the Bollandists <note>A group of Jesuit scholars who compiled the lives of the saints</note> and the *Annals of the Order of Preachers* follow this marvelous event <note>Known as the Miracle of Fanjeaux</note> in great detail. The most famous among these debates was held at Montréal <note>original: "Monte Regali"</note>, where the same Pierre writes that *all the arch-heretics gathered* <margin>(3) Chapter 3.</margin>. William <margin>(4) Chapter 9.</margin> calls this one more solemn among the many debates they had with the heretics in various places. He says: *In this [debate] our aforementioned champions were present, along with the venerable man Pierre de Castelnau <note>original: "Petrus de Castello-novo"</note>, the Legate, and his colleague Master Raoul <note>original: "Radulphus"</note> on their side; and on the other side were the arch-heretics Arnaldus Othonis, Guilhabert de Castres <note>original: "Guilbabertus de Castris"</note>, Benoit de Termes <note>original: "Benedictus de Termino"</note>, Pontius Jordan, and many others.* And in these passages we have produced, you will notice, I think, how often that name *arch-heretics* occurs; this confirms the use of this word which we indicated earlier. Pierre joins another debate to this one, which he says was held against the Walden[sians]—

<meta>This page continues the discussion of early diplomatic and religious efforts to resolve the Albigensian heresy. It specifically examines the chronological discrepancies between various historical chroniclers—such as Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay and William of Puylaurens—regarding the councils held at Pamiers, Montréal, and Montélimar, and the crucial advice given by Bishop Diego of Osma to adopt apostolic poverty.</meta>

<page-num>57</page-num>
<sig>H</sig>
<meta>catchword: II. But</meta>

...[Pam]iens <note>original: "denfes"; likely a fragment of "Apamienses"</note> (1), at Pamiers in the territory of Toulouse, when those twelve Cistercian Abbots <note>The Cistercians were a monastic order chosen by the Pope to lead the preaching mission against the Albigensian heretics</note> had already arrived. But William (2) places this dispute at Montréal <note>original: "Monte Regali"; a stronghold in the Languedoc</note> before the debate: which indeed demonstrates that which we advised above, that he either did not exactly grasp the chronological order, or wished to neglect it in his writing. Next, Peter (3) adds in his *History* that a Council was held at Montélimar <note>original: "Montilum"</note>: *Master Milo,* he says, *with his colleague Master Theodisius, set out for the regions of Provence, and coming to a certain castle called Montélimar, he summoned many Archbishops and Bishops; when they had come to him, he diligently sought from them how they should proceed in the business of peace and faith, especially regarding the matter of the Count of Toulouse. He also wished that each of the Prelates should deliver to him their written and sealed advice regarding certain chapters about which the Abbot (Arnold) <note>Arnold Amaury, the influential Abbot of Cîteaux and papal legate</note> had instructed him. It was done as he commanded; and what is wonderful to hear, all the advice, both of the Abbot and of the Prelates, agreed without any dissension.* Cossart, Hardouin, and others have included this Council of Montélimar in the list of other Councils, and assigned it the year 1209, which the Illustrious Father Mansi (4) proves was very well done. Hardouin, however, (5) following Cossart, places before this a Council held in the previous year (1207) at an uncertain location; specifically from the writing of Vincent of Beauvais (6): *Twelve Abbots of the Cistercian Order were sent by Pope Innocent with one Legate of the Apostolic See into the land of the Albigensians, so that by preaching the Catholic faith they might expel the heretical poisons with all their might. Having called a Council of the Archbishops, Bishops, and other Prelates of the Church in those parts, they inquired among themselves how they might more conveniently carry out that for which they had come. While they were anxious over this deliberation, the Venerable Diego, Bishop of Osma <note>mentor to Saint Dominic and a key figure in changing the mission's strategy</note>, arrived... This, moreover, was his advice, suggested by the Divine Spirit: that, casting aside the pomp of superfluous equipment... they should show forth in themselves a true and Evangelical poverty.* Cossart warned that no one except the author of the *Acts of Saint Dominic* had mentioned this Council. I fear that this second Council might actually be the Council of Montélimar—where it was likewise asked how to proceed in the business of peace and faith—or it might signify only a private discussion, as Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay (7) seems to indicate; and thus the times and events have been confused.

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the historical records of councils convened to address the Albigensian movement. While the previous page discussed specific disputes at Pamiers and Montréal involving Cistercian legates, this page shifts to a broader chronological survey of earlier 12th-century councils in England and France (Oxford, Tours, and Lombers) where these groups—variously called Publicans, Cathars, or "Good Men"—were first formally condemned by the Church.</meta>

II. But this point presents itself, that we should investigate which Councils were held regarding the Albigensian affair before this time. We are investigating, however, not just any [councils] celebrated against the Cathars or any new Manichaeans <note>The author uses "Manichaeans" as a historical label for these heretics, suggesting they held dualist beliefs (the world is a struggle between a good god and an evil god) similar to the ancient sect of Manichaeism</note> (indeed, the Illustrious Father Thomas Augustinus Ricchinius performed this most diligently and eruditely, reviewing all the Councils by which the Heresy of the Cathars or Manichaeans was proscribed (1)); but rather those which seem to have most especially condemned those who were called Albigensians, and had already settled in these parts and places, or had already migrated from them into other regions.

If the Albigensian Heretics are the Publicans <note>original: "Publicani"; a term of uncertain origin used in the 12th century to describe heretical groups, possibly a corruption of "Paulicians," an earlier sect from the East</note>, then they were already dealt with in the year 1160 in the Council of Oxford. For regarding that year, William of Newburgh <note>A 12th-century English historian and Augustinian canon</note>, whose passage we indicated above, writes: *In those same days, certain erring people came into England from that sort, as it is believed, whom they commonly call Publicans... being detected by certain people investigating curiously because they were of a foreign sect, they were seized and held in public custody. The King, however, being unwilling to either dismiss or punish them without discussion, ordered an Episcopal Council to be celebrated at Oxford. Where, when they had solemnly assembled from the region, etc.*

Three years later, the Council of Tours was celebrated, concerning which Baronius (2) <note>Cesare Baronius, a prominent 16th-century Cardinal and Church historian</note> wrote extensively under the year 1163, producing all the Acts and the established Canons. Moreover, William of Newburgh (3) also recorded certain things in his *History*, and among others, this Canon: *From the regions of Toulouse (4), a damnable heresy emerged some time ago; which, spreading itself like a cancer gradually to neighboring places, has already infected very many through Gascony and other provinces, etc.* In the common collections of the Councils (5), a title is prefixed to this Canon: *That all should flee the company of the Albigensian Heretics*.

The Council of Lombers <note>Lombers is a town in the Tarn department of France</note> is most famous, which Binnius titled *A Gallic Council against the Albigensians*; others (6) [titled it] *against the Heretics who are called Good Men* <note>original: "Boni homines"; this was the term the Cathars/Albigensians frequently used for themselves, emphasizing their claim to follow the true Gospel</note>, because they surely named themselves so, as we have said. The Bishop of Lodève pronounced the sentence against them; he added, however, that he did this by the order and mandate of the Bishop of Albi and his assessors. The Bishop of Albi, confirming the sentence, said: *The sentence which Gaucelinus, Bishop of Lodève, has now delivered, I confirm and praise, and it was given by my command; and I warn the knights of Lombers that they should not maintain them.* For Lombers was in the Diocese of Albi, as we have said; wherefore [they] dwel- <note>The text ends mid-word with the catchword "habi-", likely "habita" (held) or "habitores" (inhabitants)</note>

<meta>This page continues the chronological survey of ecclesiastical councils held against the Albigensians, specifically examining those from the mid-12th century. It picks up from a discussion of how the name "Albigensian" began to appear in official records, transitioning into a detailed list of specific councils like those of Oxford and Tours.</meta>

II. But this point presents itself as an opportunity to investigate which Councils concerning the Albigensian business were held before this time. We are investigating, however, not just any councils celebrated against the Cathars <note>A dualist medieval religious sect</note> or any of the "new Manichaeans" (the illustrious Father Thomas Augustinus Ricchinius has already performed this most diligently and learnedly, reviewing all the Councils in which the heresy of the Cathars or Manichaeans was proscribed (1)), but rather those which seem to have most specifically condemned those who were called Albigensians, and who had already settled in these parts and places, or had already migrated from them into foreign lands.

If the Albigensian heretics are the same as the "Publicans" <note>A common medieval derogatory term for various heretical groups, possibly a corruption of "Paulicians"</note>, then they were already dealt with in the year 1160 at the Council of Oxford. For regarding that year, William of Newburgh <note>A 12th-century English historian</note>, whose passage we indicated above, writes: *In those same days, certain erring people came into England from that group, it is believed, whom the common people call Publicans... some having curiously investigated, because they were a foreign sect, they were discovered and held in public custody. The King, however, being unwilling to either release or punish them without discussion, ordered an Episcopal Council to be celebrated at Oxford. Where, when they were solemnly assembled from the region, etc.*

Three years later, the Council of Tours was celebrated, about which Baronius (2) <note>Cardinal Cesare Baronio (1538–1607), a prominent Catholic historian</note> writes extensively under that year—that is, 1163—producing all the Acts and the sanctioned Canons. William of Newburgh (3) also recorded certain things in his History, and among others, this Canon: *From the regions of Toulouse <note>original: "A partibus Toloſæ"</note> (4), a damnable heresy emerged some time ago; which, spreading itself little by little like a cancer to neighboring places, has already infected very many throughout Gascony and other provinces, etc.* In the common collections of the Councils (5), a title is prefixed to this Canon: *That all should flee the company of the Albigensian Heretics.*

Most famous is the Council of Lombers, which Binius titled the *Gallic Council against the Albigensians*; others (6) call it the council *against the Heretics who are called Good Men* <note>original: "Boni homines"</note>, because they surely named themselves so, as we have said. The Bishop of Lodève pronounced the sentence against them; he added, however, that he did this by the order and mandate of the Bishop of Albi and his assessors. The Bishop of Albi, confirming the sentence, said: *The sentence which Gaucelin, Bishop of Lodève, has now brought forth, I confirm and praise, and it was given by my order; and I warn the soldiers of Lombers that they should not maintain them.* For Lombers was in the Diocese of Albi, as we said; wherefore habi- <meta>catchword: habi- (likely "habitata" or "habita")</meta>

<summary>This page tracks the naming of the Albigensian heresy through various 12th-century church councils, noting that while the group was condemned under many names (Cathars, Patarines, etc.), the specific term "Albigensian" was not yet standard in official decrees.</summary>

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of late 12th-century ecclesiastical actions against heresy. The catchword 'ret' from the previous page likely completed the word 'met' or 'assembled' in the context of the Council of Montpellier.</meta>

...they met (1) at Montpellier for the purpose of celebrating a Council. The Legate, therefore, with the consent of all, placed under anathema <note>A formal curse or excommunication by the church</note> all Heretics, the *Aragonese*, the families which are called *mainatæ* <note>original: "mainatæ"; likely referring to mercenary bands or specific household retainers common in the region</note>, etc. At the end of everything, he also added that, because Heretics exist in several places in the *Province of Narbonne*, it should be up to the Council of the Bishop and Bishops how interdicts <note>A decree prohibiting church services and sacraments in a specific territory</note> ought to be promulgated for the transgression of those things which were decreed, lest by the occasion of a general and long-lasting interdict, an opportunity be opened for the heretics to undermine the simple people of the Catholic faith.

The Council of Sens followed in the year of our Lord 1198, in which, according to Robert of Auxerre <note>A 13th-century French chronicler</note> in the *Chronicle of Auxerre*, the Populican Heresy <note>A term often used by medieval writers for dualist heresies, possibly derived from the "Paulicians" of the East</note> was condemned. There also exist letters of Innocent III by which the case of the Dean of Nevers, noted for his heresy, is committed to Michael, Archbishop of Sens (2). In the year 1201, according to Robert in the *Chronicle of Auxerre*: Euraudus, a knight whom *Henry, Count of Nevers* had placed in charge of his land... His heresy, which they call that of the *Bulgarians* <note>original: "Bulgarorum"; a common medieval label for the Cathars, reflecting their perceived origins in the Byzantine Balkans</note>, was argued before the Legate, and a day was set for him to publicly purge himself of the accused heresy. The Legate called a Council at Paris, in which, with the Archbishops and Bishops of the Kingdom and the Masters of Paris sitting with the Legate, Euraudus was brought forth, etc. And this man is indeed believed to be that knight and most wicked heretic condemned for heresy in the Council of Paris before Octavian, Cardinal and Legate of the Apostolic See, who afterwards moved to the region of Narbonne, and with whom the Bishop of Osma disputed at the Castle of Carman <note>original: "Castro Carmano"; likely modern Caraman</note>, as we said at the beginning. However, the name is inconsistent. For Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay says he had himself called Theodoric, though he was previously called William. But we must return to the history itself of the Assemblies, or Colloquies <note>term: colloquium (a structured public debate or conference)</note>, which were held with the Albigensians and which occur in the records of that heresy. We omit, however, the Colloquy held at Carcassonne in the year 1203 between the Cathar and Waldensian heretics, and the Bishop of Carcassonne and the Apostolic Legates in the presence of King Peter of Aragon, which Percin reports; because it does not relate so closely to the Albigensians themselves.

### III.
After Béziers and Carcassonne were conquered, a discussion was held between the Prelates and Barons through the Legate, says William of Puylaurens (3), regarding who deserved the acquired land and should persist in acquiring more; when those who were the greater men in the army had refused what was offered, there was found the God-devoted and vigorous *Simon, Count of Montfort*, who, overcome by the many prayers of the Prelates and Barons, accepted what he had first refused along with the others, saying that by the failure of a single champion, God’s business should by no means be left undone.

<summary>This page details several councils and legal proceedings against heretics at the turn of the 13th century, culminating in Simon de Montfort's acceptance of leadership in the Albigensian Crusade after the fall of Béziers and Carcassonne.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the historical account of the Albigensian Crusade and the ecclesiastical legal proceedings against Count Raymond VI of Toulouse. The opening syllable "ret" likely completes the word "refert" (reports) from the previous page, indicating that Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay is reporting on the meeting.</meta>

reports. Concerning the same meeting, Peter (1) and Bernard (2) write, the latter of whom assigns it to the year 1209. When Count Raymond, having been driven out from his lands, had come to Rome attempting to see if he might somehow be restored to his territory, as the same Peter says (3); the Pope, lest he drive him into despair, ordered a Purgation <note>A formal legal procedure where an accused person clears themselves of charges by taking a solemn oath of innocence, often supported by "compurgators" or witnesses who swear to the person's character.</note>. *Meanwhile*, says Peter, *Master Milo summoned a Council of Prelates in the Province of Avignon, in which Council the Citizens of Toulouse were excommunicated and exposed <note>This "exposure" meant they were no longer under the legal protection of the church or law, making their property and persons liable to seizure by the Crusaders.</note>, because they had looked with contempt upon fulfilling those things which they had promised to the Legate and the Crusaders <note>original: "Crucefiguatis"; literally "those signed with the cross."</note> regarding the expulsion of Heretics. The Count of Toulouse was also excommunicated in the aforementioned Council, though under the condition that he would be so if he attempted to collect the tolls <note>original: "pedagia"; taxes or transit duties often disputed between secular lords and the church.</note> he had previously renounced.*

Master Coffart proves that this Council was held in the year 1210, and that it is distinct from another Council of Avignon—in which many statutes and decrees were made—which he maintains was celebrated in 1209. In that former council, alongside the name of Milo, the name of Hugo of Riez is read, who is himself also called a Legate of the Pope. Among other things decreed against the Heretics, this also was sanctioned (4): that every Bishop shall compel his citizens, counts, Castellans <note>Lords or governors of a castle and its surrounding territory.</note>, knights, and his other parishioners—as he sees fit through ecclesiastical censure, if necessary—to swear an oath, just as those of Montpellier have sworn, especially regarding the extermination of Heretics.

In the same year, when the Count of Toulouse had obtained permission for a purgation of those two crimes to be announced for him, a meeting was called in the town of Saint-Gilles by the Bishop of Riez and Master Theodisius, consisting of Archbishops, Bishops, and many other Prelates of the churches. The Count of Toulouse was also present, attempting to see if he could somehow clear himself of the death of the Legate <note>The assassination of the Papal Legate Peter of Castelnau in 1208, which triggered the Albigensian Crusade.</note> and the crime of heresy. However, the purgation was not admitted, because he had not fulfilled the commands which had been laid upon him and which he had sworn he would perform. Rather, by the common counsel and assent of the Prelates, for many and very reasonable causes, that most wicked man of Toulouse and all his supporters and helpers were excommunicated again in that place. These things are from Peter (5). Pope Innocent approved the excommunication in letters to the Archbishop of Arles; however, since the sentence is there said to have been brought by the Bishop of Uzès, I think it should be read as "of Riez."

In that same year, 1210, a convention was held at Narbonne. At this meeting, the Bishop of Uzès and the Venerable Abbot of Cîteaux <note>Arnaud Amalric, a prominent leader of the crusade against the Cathars.</note> were present, says Peter (6), ... *and Master Theodisius was also present at the aforementioned meeting, along with many other wise and good men. And he adds: In the aforementioned meeting, the matter of the Count of Toulouse was treated, and great grace and abundant mercy would have been granted to him.*

<summary>This page details the escalating legal and ecclesiastical conflict between the Papacy and Count Raymond VI of Toulouse in 1209-1210, specifically focusing on failed negotiations for his "purgation" and his subsequent re-excommunication at the Council of Saint-Gilles.</summary>

<meta>This page continues from page 61, completing the word "pedagia" (tolls) from the previous sentence regarding the excommunication of the Count of Toulouse should he attempt to collect transit duties he had previously renounced.</meta>

tolls, if the same *Count had been willing to yield to sound advice*. For this they were granting: that provided he expelled the Heretics from his lands, he would be permitted to recover whatever he had lost. However, it is clear that nothing was obtained from him; indeed, I suspect—even if Peter <note>Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay, the primary chronicler of the Crusade</note> remains silent on the matter—that the excommunication <note>original: "anathema"</note> in which he had already been entangled was confirmed. For Innocent III, in letters sent to the Bishop of Arles from the *Lateran on April 15, in the 14th year of his Pontificate*, says that a sentence was promulgated against him by the *Venerable Bishop of Uzès and our beloved son the Abbot of Cîteaux*. Cossartius interprets this as the sentence issued in the town of Saint-Gilles; but since he believes the Bishop of Riez and Theodisius were the ones who issued it, he thinks those names ought to be corrected. But a correction of this kind seems rather bold to me, and I prefer, as I have said, to interpret this as the Narbonnese sentence, where the names of the Judges match perfectly. 

In the year 1212, in the month of November, as read in the same Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay (1), *the noble Count of Montfort <note>Simon de Montfort, the military leader of the Albigensian Crusade</note> convoked the Bishops and nobles of his land at the Castle of Pamiers <note>Apamiarum</note> to celebrate a general assembly. The cause of this assembly was this: so that our Count might cause good customs to be established in the land which he had acquired and subjected to the Holy Roman Church, having driven far away the heretical filth which had entirely corrupted that land.* He adds that twelve men were elected to establish those customs, of whom four were churchmen. Indeed, both the Count and his knights swore that they would observe them. Furthermore, *they were put into writing, and were strengthened and secured by the seal of the Count and of all the Bishops, of whom many were present.* 

In the following year, a famous Council was held at Lavaur <note>Vaurum</note>, which the same Peter describes at length (2): for *a day and place were assigned by common consent between Toulouse and Lavaur where the assembly should be held.* On the third day, when the King of Aragon <note>Peter II of Aragon, who sought to protect his vassals in Languedoc</note> had withdrawn from the place of the assembly and had come to Toulouse, he wrote certain petitions on behalf of the Counts of Toulouse, Foix, and Comminges, and Gaston of Béarn; to which the Fathers <note>the gathered clergy</note> responded with letters, the inscription of which is: *To the illustrious and beloved P. <note>Peter</note>, by the grace of God King of Aragon, Count of Barcelona, the Council at Lavaur [sends] greetings and sincere love in the Lord.* 

The same author relates that when the King had obtained nothing, he appealed to the Apostolic See <note>The Pope in Rome</note>. Likewise, in that Council, it was debated whether the Purgation <note>A legal oath of innocence; see note on previous page</note> which the Pope had granted to the Count of Toulouse ought to be accepted; Cossartius determines from the letters of Innocent that it was decided that it should not be admitted. In the year 1213, when a famous victory was reported by the Catholics at the castle they call Muret <note>The Battle of Muret, a decisive turning point in the Crusade</note>—after it was liberated from siege—the Bishops and Abbots who were gathered there wrote a synodal letter, which

<meta>This page continues the historical account of the Albigensian Crusade, specifically addressing the legal and ecclesiastical transitions of power in the Languedoc region following the Pamiers assembly mentioned on the previous page.</meta>

Both **Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay** (1) and **Matthew Paris** (2) relate these events; the letters are signed as follows: *We, the Bishops of Toulouse, Nîmes, Uzès, Béziers, Agde, and Comminges; and the Abbots of Clairac, Villemagne, and Saint-Thibéry, who had come by the mandate of the Venerable Father, the Archbishop of Narbonne and Legate of the Apostolic See, and were striving with the utmost diligence and zeal to negotiate for peace and concord.*

In the year 1215, a Council was held at Montpellier; *The Archbishops and Bishops, called by Master Peter of Benevento, Legate of the Apostolic See, gathered at Montpellier for a Council, so that the same Legate might ordain—according to the Council of Prelates—those things which pertained to peace and the Faith,* says Peter (3). *Five Archbishops attended that Council, namely those of Narbonne, Auch, Embrun, Arles, and Aix. There were twenty-eight Bishops, and many of the Barons of the land were present there.* 

The account proceeds to relate that it was deliberated in that council to whom **Toulouse**—which the **Count of Toulouse** <note>Raymond VI, who was accused of supporting the Cathar heresy</note> once held—and other lands occupied by the armies of the Christian Crusaders <note>original: "Crucesignatorum"</note> should be granted and assigned. This was to be done in a way that would be most beneficial for the honor of God, the Holy Mother Church, and the peace of the land, as well as for the purging of the "filth of the Heretics." 

It was decided that the lands should be granted to the **Count of Montfort** <note>Simon de Montfort, the military leader of the crusade against the Albigensians</note>. Therefore, the Archbishop of Embrun was sent to Rome as an envoy with letters in which all the Prelates most urgently petitioned the Lord Pope to grant them the noble Count of Montfort, whom they had unanimously chosen, as Lord and Monarch of the land. 

**Innocent III** responded and wrote letters in which he entrusted all that land to the Count of Montfort to be guarded until he should more fully ordain concerning the aforementioned lands in the General Council <note>The Fourth Lateran Council, a major gathering of church leaders in Rome</note> which he had summoned to Rome for the first day of November of that year. Letters from Innocent to the Count of Montfort, by which this is granted, still exist today. 

This was indeed carried out by the Legate after another meeting held at **Carcassonne**; *On a certain day, therefore,* says Peter (4), *the Legate summoned to the house of the Bishop of Carcassonne the Bishops who were present, Louis, the Count of Montfort, and the nobles who were with Louis* (Louis VIII, King of France); *When all were gathered, the Legate, according to the form of the Apostolic mandate, entrusted the land to the Count until the General Council.* In the same Council which we said was held at Montpellier, many decrees were established concerning ecclesiastical discipline and the Heretics; for the purpose of reforming—as the Legate Peter says in his letter to the Archbishop of Narbonne (5)—and confirming the Catholic faith.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Albigensian Crusade, focusing on the legal outcomes of the Fourth Lateran Council and the transfer of the County of Toulouse to Simon de Montfort.</meta>

[Ca]tholic <note>The text begins mid-word, completing "Catholicam" from the previous page.</note> faith in places where it has completely failed or seems to be in danger; and he adds: *for it is not fitting for the Holy Church, or for Christendom, to leave the holy business of Christ against the Albigensian and Toulousain Heretics—promoted by so many and such great divine miracles—unfinished behind them, when through the Grace of God it is already in the harbor of its due completion.* <note>original: "non enim convenit Sanctæ Ecclesiæ... in portu debitæ consummationis."</note> In the same year, 1215, the Fourth Ecumenical Lateran Council was celebrated. Peter <note>Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay, a chronicler of the crusade</note> testifies (1) that both the Count of Toulouse <note>Raymond VI and his son, the future Raymond VII</note> and the Count of Foix came to it, undoubtedly to recover their lands; Simon, Count of Montfort, however, sent his brother Guy. However, it turned out otherwise. For as the same author adds, the land was awarded to the Count of Montfort, the Count of Toulouse being deprived of it by judicial sentence; and the Castle of Narbonne was handed over to the same Count Simon, and the citizens and burghers of Toulouse swore oaths to him, and he himself was named and held as the Count of Toulouse, and was recorded as the Count by notaries in public documents. Peter reports that there were even some Prelates in that Council who favored the Count of Toulouse; but the Pope decreed it according to the majority and the "sounder part" <note>original: "saniori [parte]"; a medieval legal principle where the weight of an opinion was based on the authority and wisdom of the voters rather than a simple head-count</note>. This sentence is found in d’Achery’s *Spicilegium* (3); in it, Count Raymond of Toulouse is granted four hundred marks annually from the land's revenues for his maintenance, as long as he takes care to obey humbly; it is added: *the wife of the Count himself <note>Eleanor of Aragon</note>, sister of the late King of Aragon—to whom praiseworthy testimony is given by everyone that she is an honest and Catholic woman—shall hold the lands pertaining to her dowry in their entirety and in peace; provided, however, that she causes them to be guarded according to the mandate of the Church, so that the business of peace and the Faith cannot be disturbed through them, or that she receives a competent compensation for them according to the judgment of the Apostolic See.* 

We stated that in the year 1221, the Legate Conrad of Urach, a Cistercian, was sent; but William writes (4): *During the time of the same Legate, truces having been taken up in the hope of peace, two conferences were permitted for negotiation: one at Saint-Flour, a castle in Auvergne; and the other at Sens, the metropolitan city of Burgundy: however, the effect of peace followed into the wind [vanished]; for the sins of the Amorites were not yet full.* <note>A biblical reference to Genesis 15:16, used here to suggest that the time for a final resolution or divine judgment had not yet arrived.</note> I do not know whether that conference at Sens was the one called there—

<meta>This page continues the historical account of the Albigensian Crusade, transitioning from the legal settlements of the Fourth Lateran Council to reports of a rival "heretical papacy" emerging in the East and South of France.</meta>

Matthew Paris reports <note>Matthew Paris (c. 1200–1259) was a famous English Benedictine monk and chronicler.</note> that it happened, writing (1) regarding the year 1223: *Around those days, the Albigensian Heretics <note>The Albigensians, or Cathars, were a dualist sect in Southern France deemed heretical by the Catholic Church.</note> established for themselves an Antipope in the territories of the Bulgarians, Croatia, and Dalmatia, named Bartholomew. In those parts, that error grew so strong that it even lured the Bishops and many others of those regions to its own depravity. Against him, Conrad, Bishop of Porto and Legate of the Apostolic See in those parts, wrote to the Archbishop of Rouen in these words.* He then recites the letters themselves, in which these things, among others, are said concerning the Antichrist: *That man of perdition who is exalted above all that is worshiped or called God <note>original: "Ille homo perditus qui extollitur super omne quod colitur aut dicitur Deus" — a reference to the Antichrist from 2 Thessalonians 2:4.</note>, already has a precursor of his own treachery, a Heresiarch whom the Albigensian Heretics call their Pope, dwelling in the borders of the Bulgarians, Croatia, and Dalmatia, near the Hungarian nation. The Albigensian Heretics flock to him so that he may respond to their consultations. Indeed, Bartholomew, a bishop of the heretics originally from Carcassonne and acting as a deputy for that Antipope, showed him funereal reverence by granting him a seat and a place in the village called Porlos, and transferred himself into the regions of Toulouse. This Bartholomew, in the course of his letters flying about everywhere, entitles himself in the first address of his greeting in this manner: "Bartholomew, servant of the servants of the holy faith <note>original: "Bartholomæus servus servorum sanctæ fidei" — This is a direct parody of the official Papal title, "Servant of the servants of God."</note>, to such a person, greetings." He even, among other enormities, creates bishops and strives to ordain churches in a treacherous manner.*

Here, Conrad seems to distinguish between two people: the Pope of the Albigensians living in the village of Porlos, and Bartholomew dwelling in Carcassonne, whom Paris has confused. Conrad continues, asking the Bishops and commanding them: *that you should come to Sens <note>original: "Senonis"</note> in the upcoming octaves of the Apostles Peter and Paul, where other Prelates of France will also be gathered, the Lord willing, prepared to give counsel in the aforementioned business, and to provide for the Albigensian matter with others who will be present there.* For although William <note>likely William of Puylaurens</note> indicates that this was done only so that peace might be cared for, it could also have been this other matter which he himself neglected to tell, or perhaps nothing was done regarding Bartholomew because, as the same Paris adds: *The death of the aforementioned Antipope quickly ended this tumult.* William the Breton (2), Rigord, and others also remember this Council. 

In the year of our Lord 1224, King Louis of France <note>Louis VIII, who reigned 1223–1226.</note> held a general parliament at Paris on the third of the Nones <note>A Roman dating term; here likely referring to the beginning of a month.</note>, in which Pope Honorius, by his own authority, revoked for a time the Indulgence that had been instituted in the Lateran Council against the Albigensian Heretics, and approved Count Raymond of Toulouse as a Catholic in faith: so it is read in a historical fragment in the works of Du Chesne (3). <meta>This page continues the historical account of the Albigensian Crusade, moving from rumors of a heretical "Pope" in the East to the specific legal and military actions taken by the French Crown and the Church between 1226 and 1227. It details the councils held in Paris and Narbonne that finalized the dispossession of the Count of Toulouse.</meta>

...other historians say nothing. I do not know if Spondanus <note>Henri de Sponde (1568–1643) was a French jurist and historian who continued the ecclesiastical annals of Caesar Baronius.</note> intended to indicate this when he testified that the *Annals of Foix and the Franks* record that, while Louis <note>Louis VIII of France (reigned 1223–1226), known as "the Lion."</note> was on his expedition, a Legate held an assembly of Prelates and Nobles which absolved the Count of Foix. However, he speaks of the Count of Foix; yet neither the place matches—*at Saint John of the Virgins near Foix*—nor the time, namely the year 1226, by which Louis had already surely undertaken his expedition. In the *Chronicle of Tours* for the year 1226, two Parisian Councils are narrated.

I. The first, in which the Legate, by the authority of the Lord Pope, **excommunicated Raymond, Count of Toulouse, and his accomplices, and confirmed his land to Louis, King of France, and his heirs forever, as that of a condemned heretic.** <note>original: "Raimundum Comitem Tolosanum suosque complices excommunicavit, & terram illius, tamquam damnati Hæretici Ludovico Regi Franciæ & Hæredibus ejus in perpetuum confirmavit"</note> For Amaury <note>Amaury de Montfort, son of Simon de Montfort, who had struggled to maintain control of the lands his father conquered during the earlier phases of the crusade.</note> had ceded the land to the King, as we have already said, and this is also recorded here. It is added, moreover, that delegates were appointed to preach the taking up of the cross against the Heretics, and—excepting the vow of pilgrimage to Jerusalem—they were to absolve the crusaders from all other vows as well as from their crimes. Furthermore, the Legate promised the King 100,000 pounds every year for a five-year period, to be drawn from the tithe of the incomes and revenues of the Clergy.

Regarding the second Assembly, held on March 29 <term>4th Calends of April</term>: **King Louis convoked a council at Paris. And there, treating many matters with the Legate, Bishops, and Barons regarding the Albigensian business, he commanded and ordered by letter all and individuals of his kingdom who owed him military service to meet him with proper arms at Bourges on the fourth Sunday after the Resurrection.** <note>This was the mobilization for the massive royal campaign of 1226, which led to the siege of Avignon.</note>

In the year 1227, the Council of Narbonne was held, the Acts of which the praised Catel <note>Guillaume Catel (1560–1626), a historian from Toulouse who documented the history of the Counts of Toulouse.</note> published. In that council, especially in chapter 17, the Albigensians are censured: for it is ordered that Raymond the Younger <note>Raymond VII, Count of Toulouse.</note>, the Count of Foix, and Trencavel, Viscount of Béziers, and especially those of Limoux and others who had sworn oaths to King Louis but later turned away from the Church, should (along with their supporters) be denounced as excommunicated with candles lit and bells ringing. 

According to William of Puylaurens (1), when Elias Garin, the Abbot of Grandselve <note>A significant Cistercian abbey near Toulouse.</note>, had come by the authority of the Legate to offer peace to the people of Toulouse, negotiations took place around *Vasegia* <note>original: "Vasegiam"—likely referring to La Vaissière, a site of diplomatic talks.</note>; and it was ordained that they must go to France. The place for the meeting was first assigned at Meaux in Brie, a city of the Count of Champagne. Present there were those ordered to attend and those who came voluntarily: the Archbishop and his suffragans <note>Bishops who are subordinates to an Archbishop.</note> of the Province of Narbonne; and the Count of Toulouse arrived there, along with other men and citizens of Toulouse.... The Legate was also there, along with many other Prelates who had been summoned, where they delayed for several days discussing the terms of peace. Then they set out for Paris, so that everything might be finalized in the presence of the King; and with everything said and sealed at the end, things re-

<meta>This page continues the historical account of the resolution of the Albigensian Crusade. It specifically details the legal and ecclesiastical aftermath of the conflict, focusing on the reconciliation of Raymond VII, Count of Toulouse, and the establishment of new laws and penances for heretics under the authority of King Louis IX and the Church Legate.</meta>

The Count was reconciled on Good Friday <note>original: "in die Parasceves"</note>, and those who were with him were bound by the sentence of excommunication. Guillaume Catel <note>Guillaume Catel (1560–1626) was a magistrate and historian from Toulouse, author of the "Memoirs of the History of Languedoc."</note> reports the treaty made at that time (1). There are also the Statutes of Saint Louis IX <note>Louis IX of France (reigned 1226–1270), later canonized as a saint.</note> addressed to the people of Narbonne recorded in the same author's work (2). The same William thereafter (3) <note>William of Puylaurens, a chronicler of the Albigensian Crusade.</note>, where he narrated the reconciliation of the city of Toulouse through Master Peter of Colmieu acting as the Legate’s deputy, reports a Council held at Toulouse by the same Legate in the year 1229. 

And the same man celebrated a Council there at Toulouse after the summer, which was attended by the Archbishops of Narbonne, Bordeaux, and Auch, as well as bishops and many other prelates. Also present were the Count of Toulouse and other Counts, except for the Count of Foix; barons, the Seneschal of Carcassonne, and two Consuls of Toulouse—one from the City and the other from the Borough—who swore to the statutes of peace on the soul of the whole community <note>original: "in totius universitatis animam," a legal formula where representatives swore an oath that bound the entire corporate body of the city.</note>: and both the Count and the others approved and performed it; and the whole land did the same afterward. 

Furthermore, collectors of the Councils have reported the statutes decreed in that Council. Among other things, it was enacted concerning Heretics in this **Canon 10**: that heretics who had been "clothed" <note>original: "Hæretici vestiti." This refers to the *Perfecti*, the Albigensian elite who wore specific religious habits, as opposed to ordinary believers.</note> but returned to the Catholic Church should, in detestation of their old error, henceforth wear two crosses prominently, of a different color than their garments—one on the right and the other on the left. In the Council of Béziers in the year 1233, Canon 4, it was decreed that those who do not do this are to be considered Heretics and their goods confiscated. 

In the year 1229 likewise, as William records in the same place, when the Legate had crossed the Rhône, he celebrated his Council in the Cisalpine city of Orange <note>original: "Aurasia"</note> with Archbishops, Bishops, and Prelates; from Mornas <note>A village in the Vaucluse department of France.</note> where he was staying, he sent back to the Bishop of Toulouse the letters of penance which he had ordained against suspects whom he had discovered through the inquisition in Toulouse. When the Bishop of Toulouse returned, he summoned those individuals and published the letters in the Church of Saint James. 

In the year 1232, the assembly of Melun was held, which we shall report from the same William (4). Since, therefore, the Count of Toulouse seemed to adhere less than strictly to the Parisian decrees, the Legate—having called to himself the venerable Archbishop of Narbonne and some of his Suffragan Bishops <note>Bishops who are subordinates to a Metropolitan or Archbishop.</note>—met with the Count (who had been summoned by the King) in his presence regarding several articles expressed in the Peace of Paris that had been observed less truly than was fitting and necessary. It was finally ordained that the same Count should amend everything according to the judgment of the Bishop of Toulouse and one knight, whom the King would dispatch with the same Bishop to handle and conduct these matters: and once the colloquy held at Melun was dismissed, the Bishop, arriving ahead of the others, arranged the articles of reformation. He adds, mi- <note>The text breaks mid-sentence, continuing on the next page.</note>

<meta>This page continues the historical overview of councils and legal measures taken against the Albigensian heresy in the 13th century. Also that chosen official Giles of Flavy <note>Ægidius de Flaviaco</note>, who, when he had come to *Toulouse*, all the articles were set forth to the Count of *Toulouse*; from these, the Count formed his statutes and published them in the presence of the Legate and many others in the Cloister of St. Stephen of *Toulouse* in a public assembly. Hence, we have another Toulouse Assembly. Indeed, Catel <note>Guillaume Catel (1560–1626), a French historian and magistrate.</note> published those statutes (1). A little later, William <note>William of Puylaurens, the chronicler.</note> adds: "Likewise, around that time, the Lord Legate celebrated a Council at *Béziers*." There exist twenty-six canons of this Council in the name of Roman the Legate (2), in the first of which it is commanded that heretics, and those who believe them, their supporters, harborers, and defenders, should be excommunicated every Sunday. We make the Council of Narbonne the final one, in which certain laws were established for the Inquisitors of the Faith. The chapters which exist in the collections of Councils (3) are addressed by Peter of Narbonne, John of Arles, and Raymond of Aix, Archbishops, and other Prelates, to: *Our beloved and faithful sons in Christ, the brothers of the Order of Preachers <note>The Dominicans, who were primary figures in the early Inquisition.</note>, Inquisitors of Heretics appointed throughout the mentioned and surrounding provinces*. Regarding the year, nothing can be determined.

IV. To the colloquies, disputations, and Councils, another much more forceful argument was added for the rooting out of the Albigensian Heresy: that action should be taken against them with arms and war. *The Supreme Pontiff*, says Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay <note>Petrus Vallicernensis (c. 1182–1218), a Cistercian monk and chronicler of the Albigensian Crusade.</note> (4), *in order to make the faithful people more ready to root out the heretical faction, had sent general letters to all Prelates, Counts, and Barons, and the whole people established in the Kingdom of France, effectively warning and exhorting them to hasten to avenge the injury of the Crucified One <note>original: "injuriam Crucifixi," referring to Christ.</note> in the Province of Narbonne, knowing that the remission of all sins <note>term: Indulgence. A grant by the Church of the remission of the temporal punishment in purgatory still due for sins after absolution.</note> had been granted by God and His Vicar to all who, kindled with zeal for the Orthodox Faith, should gird themselves for this work of piety, provided they were both contrite and confessed. What more? This Indulgence is published in France; a great multitude of the Faithful arms itself with the sign of the Cross.* However, it is not our purpose to narrate these things, for we are writing a dissertation, not a history; therefore, we touch only upon those things that seem worthy of notice. **P. Langlois** (5) has carried out the account of that expedition in a book published on this matter, and others have done so most diligently. It seems to have happened for the first time now that, with the authority of the Roman Pontiff interposed and the Treasury of the Church <note>term: Treasury of the Church (Thesaurus Ecclesiae). The merit of Christ and the saints which the Church claims the power to distribute for the benefit of the faithful.</note> opened, war was declared against heret-

...heretics. And from this source, perhaps, William (1) writes: *When the Heresy had already grown so great, with the nobles of the land consenting to it, that it could not rouse as many scholars against itself as it could an armed hand of knights, suitable men were sent into France—which has always been accustomed to wage the wars of the Lord—to the King, the Barons, and the common people. They began to preach the indulgence <note>A grant by the Church of the remission of temporal punishment for sins.</note> by Apostolic authority against the Heretics, which was usually granted to those crossing the sea to the aid of the Holy Land.* Although only France is named here, there are indeed letters from Innocent III (2) addressed to the Counts, Barons, and all knights established throughout the Provinces of Narbonne, Arles, Embrun, Aix, and Vienne, as found in the works of Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay. Likewise, among the letters of Innocent himself, there are others addressed to the Bishops (3) of these Provinces, specifically to the Bishop of Lyon (4), and also to Philip, King of the French (5); nevertheless, Caesarius (6) testifies that the same thing was done in Germany <note>original: "Alemannia"</note>. He says, *In the year 1210, the cross was preached against the Albigensians in all of Germany and France, and there went up against them in the following year from Germany Leopold, Duke of Austria; Engelbert, then Provost and later Archbishop of Cologne; and his brother Adolph, Count of Berg <note>original: "Comes de Monte"</note>; William, Count of Jülich <note>original: "Comes Juliacensis"</note>; and many others of various condition and rank. A similar thing was done in France, in Normandy, and in Poitou.* We have said above that the Count of Bar was also among these. Brouwer <note>Christoph Brouwer (1559–1617), a Jesuit historian.</note> records the same from other authors in book 15 of the *Annals of Trier*. Later, Peter noted (7) that this was achieved through the work of William, Archdeacon of Paris, and James of Vitry: *At that time, the Venerable William, Archdeacon of Paris, and indeed another, Master James of Vitry—at the command and request of the Bishop of Uzès, whom the Lord Pope had appointed Legate for the business of the Faith against the Heretics—inflamed by zeal for the Faith, traveled through France and even Germany all that winter, and marked an incredible multitude of the faithful for the knighthood of Christ with the sign of the cross upon their chests.* These last words are worthy of notice. For the Crusaders <note>original: "Crucesignati," literally "those signed with the cross."</note> against the Albigensians seem to have had this peculiarity: they wore the Cross on their chests. Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay (8) writes: *very many nobles and commoners had armed themselves against the enemies of the cross with the sign of the cross on their chests.* Thus, Robert of Torigni <note>original: "Robertus de Monte"</note> seems to have spoken deliberately in his supplement to Sigebert for the year 1208: *Many in Gaul took up crosses on their chests to fight against the Albigensians.* And furthermore

...moreover, they had that cross sewn onto their garments: for which reason Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay (1) reports that it once happened that when an entire cloak was burned, that single patch in which the cross had been sewn remained whole and entirely unburnt. He hints at the same elsewhere (2); one of the adversaries, aiming an arrow from a stone-thrower <note>original: "balistæ"; while often a crossbow, in this context it refers to a mechanical siege engine or large crossbow.</note> at one of our men, struck him forcefully in the chest, in the very place where the sign of the Cross was affixed. Gretser noted (3) that the <term>Crusaders</term> <note>original: "Crucesignatos," literally "those signed with the cross."</note> for the expedition to Palestine were accustomed to wear the sign of the Cross either on their forehead or on their right shoulder blade; Thus, Robert the Monk says of Bohemond: *When he asked what sign of pilgrimage they should carry, he learned that they wore the sign of the holy Cross either on their forehead or on their right shoulders.* To these other testimonies can be added that Marino Sanuto (4) introduces Saint Louis IX saying to the Bishop of Paris: *Lord Bishop, I require of you that you ought to place the overseas cross upon my shoulders.* And from this and many other examples, it is established that, especially for more noble men, this Cross was given with a solemn ceremony either by Bishops or by Reverend Father Legates. The Count of Toulouse himself, according to Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay (5), received the Cross from a Legate. Gretser (6) explains the ceremonies and rites by which this was done. Here it must only be observed that the ritual was different from that when a new soldier was being initiated. The rite by which this was done, although it is frequently explained in the *Life of Saint Louis IX*—for he made both his brother Charles and his son Philip knights—nonetheless, I do not know if it is explained more clearly anywhere than in Vaux-de-Cernay regarding Amaury, the son of Simon de Montfort (7); *The most Christian Count desired and asked the Bishop of Orléans to make his son a soldier of Christ, and that he himself should hand over to him the military belt... On the very day of the birth of Saint John <note>June 24th, the Feast of St. John the Baptist.</note>, the venerable Bishop of Orléans dressed himself in his Pontifical robes to celebrate the solemnity of the Mass in a certain pavilion. Both clergy and knights gathered to hear the Mass: therefore, while the Bishop stood before the altar celebrating the Mass, Count Amaury, taking his firstborn by the right hand and the Countess by the left, approached the Altar and offered him to the Lord, asking the Bishop to make him a soldier for the service of Jesus Christ. What more? Immediately the Bishops of Orléans and Auxerre, with knees bent before the altar, girded the boy with the military belt <note>original: "cingulo militari," the formal belt signifying the rank of knighthood.</note>, beginning with the greatest devotion: "Come, Creator Spirit" <note>original: "Veni creator Spiritus," a traditional liturgical hymn.</note>; ... In this manner and order, the aforementioned boy was made a new knight with great solemnity.* The prefixed num-

<meta>The previous page discussed the physical rituals of taking the cross, specifically the sewing of the cross onto garments and the distinction between crusading vows and the rite of knighthood. This page continues the history of these "signed with the cross" (Crucesignati) and their organization in Toulouse.</meta>

We have spoken elsewhere of the full forty-day <term>Indulgence</term> <note>A grant by the Church that reduced the "temporal punishment" or penance required for sins; here, it was the primary spiritual reward for participating in the crusade.</note> granted to the <term>Crusaders</term> <note>original: "Cruce-signatis," meaning those marked with the sign of the cross.</note>. We must not fail to mention here the Great Confraternity established in Toulouse, which was also "signed with the cross" against heretics and manifest usurers, as recorded in the title of Chapter 15 of the History by William <note>likely William of Puylaurens, a major chronicler of the Albigensian Crusade.</note>, who explains it thus: *With the venerable Father Foulques the Bishop keeping a watchful eye, so that his citizens of Toulouse might not lack that indulgence which was granted to foreigners, and that through this devotion he might gather them to the Church, more easily strike down heretical depravity through them, and extinguish the fervor of usury, he obtained—with God’s help and the assistance of the Legate—the creation of a great confraternity in Toulouse. He marked all the brothers for the Lord with the sign of the Cross, and almost the entire city joined, except for a few; some from the suburb <note>original: "Burgo"; refers to the commercial district outside the old Roman city walls of Toulouse.</note> also consented.* He tells other stories about this group. From this we gather that, because arms were primarily being taken up against the people of Toulouse themselves, no one was sent there to recruit crusading soldiers from among them.

Furthermore, although no mention of Saint Dominic is made in this matter by William or other ancient writers, there are nonetheless those—as the Annals of the <term>Order of Preachers</term> (1) <note>The Dominican Order (O.P.).</note> state—who believe, and not without strong reasoning, that these were the beginnings of the Third Order of Preachers <note>A "Third Order" allows laypeople to live out the spirituality of a monastic order without becoming monks or nuns.</note>. Arguments for this are presented there, and several very learned modern men have judged it probable or agreed it to be true, such as William Cuper and Flaminio Cornaro—who, in addition to his high dignity as a Venetian Senator and countless other praises, wished to serve the study of History well by publishing outstanding monuments of his own intellect and learning.

### V.

Regarding the time when the Crusaders were first enlisted, we agree with those same diligent writers of the Annals that it clearly began in the year 1208, as is evident from the letters of Pope Innocent III. William of Nangis and Alberic assign it to the same year. However, because they did not burst into <term>Narbonnese Gaul</term> <note>The region of southern France around Narbonne, often called Occitania.</note> until the following year, and in subsequent years recruits for the Crusaders were again gathered in other provinces of Europe, different writers have attributed the start to different years. In the year 1209, the first remarkable deeds of the Crusaders occurred, along with the victories at the cities of Béziers and Carcassonne. This is, as it were, the first era. The second seems to be the first siege of Toulouse in 1211, which Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay describes in Chapter 55. The third is the second siege, or rather the death of Simon, Count of Montfort, during the siege of Toulouse itself—a city he had previously captured but which the Count of Toulouse had recovered. Here Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay ends his history in the year 1218. He is an older writer than not only William, who followed up on the remaining events, but also Caesarius. For Caesarius relates...

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, which discussed the formation of a crusading confraternity in Toulouse, this page examines the chronologies of the chroniclers Caesarius of Heisterbach and William of Puylaurens, before shifting to the role of miracles, siege technology, and liturgical prayer in the Albigensian Crusade.</meta>

He relates that Raymond of Toulouse moved to Toulouse, of which he says: *up until today he does not cease to harass and attack the faithful*; and then: *And just as in this year Lord Conrad, Bishop of Porto and Cardinal, was sent as Legate against the Albigensians in the year 1221.* Conrad, as we have seen, was sent as Legate in 1221. Therefore, it seems <term>Caesarius</term> <note>Caesarius of Heisterbach (c. 1180 – c. 1240), a Cistercian monk and author famous for his "Dialogue on Miracles."</note> wrote these things around that year. Another era can be established by the death of Count Raymond, which <term>William</term> <note>William of Puylaurens, a chaplain and chronicler whose "Chronica" is a primary source for the Albigensian Crusade.</note> places in 1222. Finally, at that time when the same William says the Inquisition of Heretics was entrusted to the <term>Friars of the Order of Preachers</term> <note>The Dominicans, who were officially tasked with the Inquisition in 1233.</note>. And indeed, he puts an end to his History, recounting that Philip, King of France <note>Philip III, known as "the Bold."</note>, came to the regions of Toulouse and led the captured Count of Foix to France in the year 1277.

Nor should we be silent about the fact that these expeditions were promoted by marvelous things. These [miracles] are very frequent, especially in the History by <term>Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay</term> (1) <note>A Cistercian monk who accompanied the Albigensian Crusade; his chronicle is highly partisan but detailed.</note>, yet their frequency does not make them suspicious; if we turn our attention to the Palestinian expeditions <note>The Crusades to the Holy Land.</note> themselves, in which they were also extremely frequent. Another aid was from human skill—the war machines themselves, which appear most accurately described in this History, such as the <term>Cat</term> <note>original: "Catus"; a mobile protective shelter used to reach city walls safely.</note>, the <term>Mangonel</term> <note>original: "Mangonellus"; a type of catapult for hurling stones.</note>, the <term>Petraria</term> (2) <note>original: "Petraja"; a stone-throwing engine.</note>, and so on. And if Charles d'Aquino and others who wrote about such matters had consulted these historians of ours, they could have been greatly helped. 

But these same historians testify that the chief support, as it should have been, was placed in prayers poured out to God. Peter, recounting the siege of <term>Moissac</term> (3) <note>A strategic town in southern France besieged in 1212.</note>: *When this assault occurred, the Bishop of Carcassonne and I were running through the army exhorting our men. The Archbishops of Reims and Tulle, the Bishop of Albi, and William, Archdeacon of Paris, also the Abbot of Moissac with certain monks, and the rest of the clergy of the army, stood at the descent of the mountain before the castle, dressed in white robes and with bare feet holding the Cross before them with the Relics of the saints, singing with the loudest and most devout voice, "Come, Creator Spirit" <note>original: "Veni creator spiritus"; a famous 9th-century hymn invoked for divine guidance and protection.</note>, imploring divine aid.* And when they attacked <term>Casseneuil</term> (4): *the Bishop of Carcassonne and the clergy who were in the army with him gathered in a higher place near the castle to cry out to heaven and pray for our fighters... Meanwhile, our clergy were singing "Come, Creator Spirit" with the greatest devotion.* We learn from these passages that they used that prayer most of all, so that they might invoke the Holy Spirit by singing His hymn—

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, which discussed the frequency of miracles in the chronicles of the Albigensian Crusade, specifically those of Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay, this page focuses on the religious devotion of Simon de Montfort and the institutional establishment of the Inquisition.</meta>

...called upon them. Not only the clergy, but the Count of Montfort <note>Simon IV de Montfort (c. 1175–1218), the primary military leader of the Albigensian Crusade.</note> himself was constant in prayer. It will be helpful to relate here something that <term>Peter</term> <note>Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay, a Cistercian monk and pro-crusader chronicler.</note> himself narrates, which represents traces of an ancient rite that still persisted at that time. When he was in the field of <term>Muret</term> <note>The Battle of Muret (1213) was a decisive victory for the crusaders against the Albigensians and their ally, the King of Aragon.</note> about to fight against the King of Aragon:

*As he entered the fortification of the Castle to arm himself, and passed before his Basilica, he suddenly looked inside and saw the Bishop of Uzès celebrating Mass, and saying "The Lord be with you" <note>original: "Dominus vobiscum"</note> after the Gospel for the offering: immediately the most Christian Count ran, and with knees bent to the ground and hands joined before the Bishop, said to him: "I give and offer to you my soul and my body..." After this, entering the fortification, he equipped himself with arms, and returning again to the Bishop in the aforementioned Basilica, he once more offered himself and his arms to him.* (1)

I have said that traces of a very ancient rite exist here, which lasted until then, namely the offerings made by the people during the Mass. <term>Innocent III</term> <note>Pope Innocent III (r. 1198–1216), the Pope who authorized the crusade against the Albigensians.</note> is a witness, clearly narrating this (2): *While the offertory is being sung, the Priest receives the offerings from the people or the hosts from the Ministers.* An ancient writer of the *Life of Saint Elizabeth, Queen of Portugal*, who lived in this century (3), says: *To hear the daily Mass, the Queen approached with great humility on bended knees before the Priest who was celebrating the Mass, kissing his hands and offering her gift before the altar, which she was accustomed to offer every day, increasing that offering according to the significance of the feast.* Indeed, those words of our Count—the offering of himself and his arms—seem to signify that very thing.

### VI.

We make our final argument (not in terms of time; for we do not account for time in this place, but in our own sequence of speaking) for the uprooting of the Albigensian Heresy: the Tribunal of the **Inquisition**, which <term>William</term> <note>William of Puylaurens, a contemporary chronicler of the Albigensian wars.</note> indicates was both established previously and permanently committed to the **Order of Preachers** <note>The Dominicans, founded by Saint Dominic, who were tasked with preaching against heresy and later administering the Inquisition.</note> (3). *In the days of the Legation of the same Bishop of Tournai, the Inquisition to be carried out in these lands against the Heretics was committed by the Supreme Pontiff to the Brothers of the Order of Preachers.* An anonymous Carthusian, writing on the origin of religions, refers to this as the highest honor of that Order. Thus, he introduces the character of a son speaking (4): *There is one thing in which this Order seems to excel, namely that the Inquisition of the Faith is committed to its professors alone, and not to others of whatever rank, condition, or Order.* So wrote he who lived in the 15th century. Nor indeed had other things that adorn that Order passed him by. This venerable Order wonderfully spread— <note>The text ends mid-sentence with the catchword "dila-", likely "dilatatus" (spread or expanded).</note>

...it was expanded, and bore much fruit for Christ and for us, by winning souls, leading them to the true faith, destroying heresies, and pursuing heretics everywhere. This Order has had almost countless praiseworthy fathers and doctors worthy of every praise, as well as distinguished Preachers, many of whom were even placed at the highest peak <note>referring to high ecclesiastical offices like the papacy or cardinalate</note>, some of whom he then enumerates. But we can say here what <term>William of Puylaurens</term> <note>original: "Guillelmus de Podio"; a 13th-century chronicler of the Albigensian Crusade.</note>, whom we have often named and who lived closer to those times, said when he praised St. Dominic and his Order (1): *it is not necessary for me to continue my narrative on this, since through the history of his life and the evidence of the Order expanded everywhere, it is already manifest to those who are willing and unwilling alike; and truly, according to what the Blessed Apostle says, it was necessary for heresies to exist in our times and regions, so that the blessed Order might appear fruitful and public, not only among us but throughout the whole world.*

Similar are the words of <term>Honorius III</term> <note>Pope from 1216 to 1227, who officially confirmed the Dominican Order.</note> and <term>Gregory IX</term> <note>Pope from 1227 to 1241, known for institutionalizing the Inquisition.</note> (2): *Since iniquity has abounded and the charity of many grows cold, the Lord has raised up the Order of Friars Preachers, as we believe; seeking not what is their own but what is Christ's, they have dedicated themselves to the Evangelization of the Word of God in the lowliness of voluntary poverty, both to drive out heresies and to uproot other deadly pests.* The same Honorius III, in letters given to St. Dominic and his companions on January 26, 1217 <note>original: "VII. Kal. Febr. a. 1217"</note> (3), wrote: *Thus, like faithful servants distributing the talents entrusted to you, so that you may bring back the same doubled to the Lord; thus, like invincible athletes of Christ, armed with the shield of Faith and the helmet of salvation, not fearing those who can kill the body, you magnanimously draw the word of God, which is sharper than any two-edged sword, against the enemies of the Faith.*

And so, Honorius experienced in truth what <term>Innocent III</term> had seen in his dreams: St. Dominic supporting the <term>Lateran Church</term> <note>The Cathedral of Rome, symbolizing the stability of the entire Church.</note> on his shoulders (a story also told regarding St. Francis). And indeed, that this vision was truly presented to Innocent is certain enough by the authority of others, even if some ancient writers remain silent on it; history shows that the meaning behind the vision was most certain. But there is no need to discuss this further, nor the <term>Inquisition</term> itself, which has been debated with great accuracy both by others and in the recently published Annals. Following our custom, as we think some things relevant to this matter should be noted, we here explain—taking the occasion of the Inquisition against heretics—what we omitted to do above: namely, what the <term>clothed heretics</term> <note>original: "Hæretici vestiti"</note> are, who appear in the Councils of this time and other records. And <term>Du Cange</term> <note>Charles du Fresne, sieur du Cange (1610–1688), a scholar of medieval Latin.</note> (4) interprets them as heretics who have been convicted of heresy, and they are contrasted with others who...

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's investigation into the terminology of the Albigensian (Cathar) heresy, shifting from the institutional history of the Dominicans to the specific distinctions between different classes of heretics, such as "Believers" and "the Clothed."</meta>

They were called *Believers* <note>original: "Credentes"; in the Cathar faith, this referred to the rank-and-file followers who had not yet undergone the full initiation.</note>, because only a suspicion of them had emerged. I do not deny that things were so; but I still seek the reason for the name: why heretics of this kind were called *the Clothed* <note>original: "vestiti"; this refers to the "Perfects" (perfecti) who wore distinctive black robes until forced into hiding.</note>. I believe I have found the cause in the writer whom we have often named as the most diligent historian of the Albigensians, <term>Peter, a Monk of the Monastery of Vaux-de-Cernay</term> <note>original: "Petrus Monachus Cœnobii Vallium Cernaii"; a 13th-century Cistercian chronicler who accompanied the Crusade against the Albigensians.</note>. For concerning the <term>Count of Toulouse</term> <note>referring to Raymond VI, whose tolerance of heresy led to his excommunication and the Albigensian Crusade.</note> he relates (1): *First it must be said that, almost from his earliest cradle, he always loved and favored Heretics; and having them in his land, he honored them in whatever ways he could; even to this day, as it is asserted, wherever he goes, he leads Heretics with him under common clothing <note>original: "sub communi habitu"; suggesting they were disguised as ordinary travelers or seculars.</note>, so that if it should happen that he dies, he might die in their hands.* And a little later: *This being done, he brought two Heretics with him in common clothing as a surplus, so that if by chance it happened that he died, he might die in their hands.* What is that "in common clothing"? It means there were, therefore, proper garments belonging to the Heretics, which the Count did not want those heretics of his to wear, so that he might falsely present himself as a Catholic and appear to stand by the promises he had made to the Roman Pontiff. This seems clearly evident to me from the words of Peter just recited; and also the reason why they were the *Clothed Heretics*, whom the Councils of Toulouse, Narbonne, Béziers, and others named.

(1) Chapter 4.

it expanded and bore much fruit for Christ and for us, by winning souls and leading them to the true faith, destroying heresies, and pursuing Heretics everywhere. This Order had almost countless laudable fathers and doctors most worthy of all praise, as well as distinguished Preachers, many of whom were even placed in the highest ranks of the Church; he then enumerates several of them. But we can say here that a writer older than him, whom we have often named, <term>William of Puylaurens</term> <note>original: "Guillelmus de Podio"; a 13th-century chaplain and chronicler of the Albigensian Crusade.</note>, praised St. Dominic and his Order (1) in this way: *over which it is not in my interest to pursue my narrative, since through the History of his life and the evidence of the Order expanded everywhere, it is already manifest to both those willing and unwilling to see it; and it is true, according to what the Blessed Apostle says, that it was necessary for Heresies to exist in our times and regions, so that the blessed Order might appear fruitful and public not only among us, but throughout the whole world.* <note>The author is referencing 1 Corinthians 11:19: "For there must be also heresies among you, that they which are approved may be made manifest among you."</note> Similar are the words of <term>Honorius III</term> and <term>Gregory IX</term> (2): *Since iniquity has abounded and the charity of many grows cold, the Lord has raised up, as we believe, the Order of Friars Preachers, who, seeking not what is their own but what is Christ's, have dedicated themselves to the Evangelization of the Word of God in the lowliness of voluntary poverty, both for the purpose of putting heresies to flight and for extirpating other deadly plagues.* The same Honorius III, in letters given to St. Dominic and his companions on January 26, 1217 (3), wrote: *Thus, like faithful servants distributing the talents entrusted to you, so that you may bring back the same doubled to the Lord; thus, like invincible athletes of Christ armed with the shield of Faith and the helmet of salvation, not fearing those who can kill the body, you magnanimously unsheathe the word of God, which is sharper than any two-edged sword, against the enemies of the Faith.* 

Honorius thus truly experienced what <term>Innocent III</term> <note>Pope from 1198 to 1216, who initially authorized the Dominican and Franciscan orders.</note> had seen in his dreams: St. Dominic supporting the <term>Lateran Church</term> <note>The Cathedral of Rome, symbolizing the institutional weight of the Papacy.</note> on his shoulders (a story that is likewise told of St. Francis). And indeed, that vision—even if some ancient authors remain silent on it—is certain enough by the authority of others to have truly been presented to Innocent; history shows the significance of the event to be most certain. But we must not dwell longer on this, nor on the <term>Inquisition</term> itself, about which discussions have been held most accurately both by others and in the recently published Annals. 

Following our custom, as we think some matters pertaining to this should be noted, we explain here—on the occasion of the Inquisition against Heretics—what we omitted above: what the <term>clothed Heretics</term> <note>original: "Hæretici vestiti"; this refers to the "Perfects" (the Cathar elite) who wore a specific religious habit or robe, as opposed to the "Believers" (Credentes).</note> are, who appear in the Councils of this time and other records. And indeed, <term>Du Cange</term> (4) <note>Charles du Fresne, sieur du Cange (1610–1688), a famous philologist and historian of the Middle Ages.</note> interprets them as Heretics who have been convicted of Heresy, and they are contrasted with others, who

<summary>This page recounts the early success and papal endorsements of the Dominican Order as a frontline defense against heresy, highlighting the vision of Pope Innocent III. It also transitions into a technical definition of "clothed heretics" versus "believers" in medieval law.</summary>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the praise of the Dominican Order to a technical discussion regarding the terminology used for the Albigensian heretics, specifically the distinction between "Believers" and "Clothed Heretics." It concludes the main body of the text.</meta>

They were called **Believers** <note>original: "Credentes"; the lower rank of the Cathar/Albigensian sect who had not yet undergone the full initiation.</note> because only the suspicion regarding them had spread. I do not deny that this was the case, but I still seek the reason for the name: why heretics of this sort were called ***clothed***? I believe I have found the cause in the writings of that most diligent chronicler of Albigensian history whom we have frequently named, <term>Peter the Monk</term> <note>original: "Petrus Monachus"; Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay, a 13th-century Cistercian who wrote the *Historia Albigensis*.</note> of the Monastery of Vaux-de-Cernay. Concerning the <term>Count of Toulouse</term> <note>referring to Raymond VI, who was frequently accused of harboring heretics while publicly professing Catholicism.</note>, he records (1): *First, it must be said that from his very infancy he always loved and favored Heretics, and keeping them in his land, he honored them in every way he could; even to this day, it is claimed, wherever he travels, he takes Heretics with him under common clothing, so that if he should happen to die, he might die in their hands.* <note>This refers to the "Consolamentum," a Cathar deathbed ritual performed by the "Perfects."</note> And a little later: *Which being done, he brought two Heretics with him in common clothing as an extra precaution, so that if he were to die, he might die in their hands.*

What does this "in common clothing" mean? It implies that the Heretics had their own distinct garments which the Count did not want his followers to wear, so that he could feign being a Catholic and appear to uphold the promises he had made to the Roman Pope. This seems clearly evident to me from the words of Peter quoted above; it also explains what the ***clothed heretics*** <note>original: "hæretici vestiti"; the "Perfecti" or leaders of the sect who traditionally wore a distinct black habit until persecution forced them into "common" or secular dress.</note> were, whom the Councils of Toulouse, Narbonne, Béziers, and others have named.

(1) Chapter 4.
